## Part XV. Transversality and invariants (HWZ Chapter 15, §§15.1–15.4)

Chapter 15 (Springer 2021, pp. 683–703; the 2017 numbers in parentheses) is the perturbation theory of an sc-Fredholm section functor $f$ of a strong bundle $(P : W \to X, \mu)$ over an ep-groupoid by sc⁺-multisections $\Lambda$, and the invariants obtained from the perturbed solution sets $\Lambda \circ f$. §15.1 defines the linearization $T(f, \Lambda)$ of $\Lambda \circ f$, a section functor with values in the formal sums $\mathrm{GrF}$ of sc-Fredholm operators, and proves that it is well defined through a separation argument for finitely many sections (Theorem 15.1.1, Remark 15.1.2, Proposition 15.1.3, Lemmas 15.1.4, 15.1.5, Remark 15.1.6, Definition 15.1.7 (2017: Theorem 15.1, Remark 15.1, Proposition 15.1, Lemmas 15.1, 15.2, Definition 15.1; Remark 15.1.6 is new in 2021)). §15.2 defines transversality and good position and describes the local solution sets, which make $\Lambda \circ f$ a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid (Definitions 15.2.1, 15.2.4, Theorems 15.2.2, 15.2.5, Remarks 15.2.3, 15.2.6). §15.3 controls compactness by an auxiliary norm, defines admissible perturbations and general position, and constructs perturbations: Theorem 15.3.7 (general position), Theorem 15.3.9 (transversal extension of boundary data) and Corollary 15.3.10 (regular perturbations exist, and any two are joined over the cylinder $[0, 1] \times X$). §15.4 orients $\Lambda \circ f$ by an orientation of $f$ (Theorem 15.4.1, Definition 15.4.2) and integrates sc-differential forms over it: Stokes' theorem (Theorem 15.4.3) and the invariant on de Rham cohomology (Corollary 15.4.4). This Part retells the formal proofs in the book's order: §15.1 in §§XV.1–XV.11, §15.2 in §§XV.12–XV.16, §15.3 in §§XV.17–XV.33 (the definitions in §§XV.17–XV.20, Theorem 15.3.7 in §§XV.21–XV.26, Theorem 15.3.9 in §§XV.27–XV.29, Corollary 15.3.10 in §§XV.30–XV.33), §15.4 in §§XV.34–XV.37; then what is not formalized (§XV.38) and what the formalization taught us (§XV.39).

**What is proved, and what is not.** *§15.1.* *Theorem 15.1.1* (narrower) is proved in its substance in a stronger form: the formal sum $\sum_{f(x) = s_i(x)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(x)$ does not depend on the local sc⁺-section structure or on the chart, at every level-$1$ point, for any sc-smooth $f$, with no use of positivity of the weights or of the functoriality of $\Lambda$ (§§XV.2, XV.7); the value $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is Definition 15.1.7 (§XV.8); the functor property $T(f, \Lambda)(t(\varphi)) = \varphi_*T(f, \Lambda)(s(\varphi))$ holds at all level-$1$ points, with no added hypothesis (§XV.9, point G.144 settled). The row is narrower for one reason: the summands are proved sc-Fredholm in the chart reading and for $f$ with Part I's tame germ data (§XV.11, point G.145 settled). *Remark 15.1.2* and *Definition 15.1.7* are restated (§§XV.2, XV.8). *Lemmas 15.1.4 and 15.1.5* are proved in a stronger, purely topological form (§§XV.4, XV.5), *Remark 15.1.6* with "slightly" made explicit (§XV.6). *Proposition 15.1.3* is proved with the added hypothesis that the base point is not isolated; at an isolated point, for instance for the one-point local model of a zero-dimensional $X$, it does not hold as we read it, since no sequence $x_k \ne x_0$ converges to $x_0$. The case is degenerate and does not reach Theorem 15.1.1, whose formal proof does not use the proposition (§§XV.3, XV.7). The proof of Theorem 15.1.1 is read in two places: the separation and the limit run in the level-$1$ topology, the "weak topology" being pointwise convergence of operators; and the final sum is presumably over the $i$ with $f(0) = s_i(0)$ (§XV.7).

*§15.2.* Definitions 15.2.1 and 15.2.4 are restated, the first with "nonzero" and "sc-Fredholm" automatic (§§XV.12, XV.15). Theorems 15.2.2, 15.2.5 and Remark 15.2.3 (narrower) are proved with Part I's tame germ data for $f$, inherited from Part I's Theorem 3.6.8, on which they rest; this is the only hypothesis they add. Their "tame" submanifolds and tame branched ep⁺-subgroupoids (Definition 9.3.6) are proved (§§XV.14, XV.16, point G.150 settled). "The ep-groupoid $U(x)$" of Remark 15.2.3 is read as the translation groupoid $G_x \ltimes U(x)$; on it the statements hold as printed, the paracompactness of the support included (§XV.14, point G.152 settled). Their global forms on $X$ take a paracompact orbit space of the support, Definition 9.1.2 (1), which is new in 2021 and which the remarks do not address. The closing sentence of Theorem 15.2.5, under general position, is proved for tame $X$ (§XV.16, point G.151 settled). Remark 15.2.6 is prose.

*§15.3.* The paragraph before Definition 15.3.1, Definition 15.3.3 with its example, and the first sentence of Remark 15.3.6 are proved as stated; Definitions 15.3.1 and 15.3.5 are restated (§§XV.17, XV.18, XV.20). The rest of Remark 15.3.6 is proved at every point of the support, not only on $\partial X$, with the inherited tame germ data and, for the branched ep⁺-subgroupoid, a paracompact support (§XV.20, point G.161 settled). The products remark after Definition 15.3.3 and Remark 15.3.4 are proved as printed: product ep-groupoids and strong bundles, $f \times f'$ sc-Fredholm with tame germ data, $\Lambda \times \Lambda'$ admissible for $(\max(N, N'), U \times U')$, and the domain-support inclusion, which is sharp (§XV.19, point G.160 settled). General position and transversality are invariant under local strong bundle isomorphisms and equivalences (extra, §XV.20). *Theorem 15.3.7* (explicit) is proved with the text's hypotheses, the inherited tame germ data being the only one not printed, with no good system and no paracompactness of $|X|$, as Remark 15.3.8 says; the addendum "structurable" needs a good system, which is necessary for it and which exists for paracompact $|X|$; its "in particular" holds with "tame" (§§XV.21–XV.26). *Theorem 15.3.9* (explicit) is proved with the text's hypotheses, the inherited tame germ data again the only one not printed: step (a) from the tame strong bundle charts that every strong bundle over a tame ep-groupoid has, step (b) Theorem 14.1.9 as printed (Part XIV), steps (c)–(d) with the relative local families (§§XV.27–XV.29; points G.177, G.178 settled). Its face condition, asked for every sc⁺ extension, is equivalent to the condition for one (point G.179 settled). Its witnesses have boundary and non-zero fibres, one with a non-zero boundary datum. *Corollary 15.3.10* (1) (explicit) follows from Theorem 15.3.7 for paracompact $|X|$, with bump functions and the tame germ data, the tameness of $X$ derived from $d_X \equiv 0$ (§§XV.30, XV.31, point G.183 settled); (2) is proved with an explicit interpolated auxiliary norm (§XV.31); (3) (explicit) is proved with the hypotheses of (1), by an explicit structurable extension of the boundary data and a collar norm, with no partitions of unity (§XV.33). The text's route through Theorem 15.3.9 on $[0, 1] \times X$ is proved as well, with partitions of unity on $X$: those of the cylinder, the structurability and the face condition of the glued boundary data are proved (§XV.32; points G.162, G.180, G.181, G.189, G.193 settled). The last paragraph of the proof of the corollary is proved as printed (§XV.33). Remarks 15.3.2 and 15.3.11 are prose.

*§15.4.* Theorem 15.4.1 (narrower) is proved in Chapter 9's $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}(X)$, with the standing hypotheses of §§12.5 and 15.2–15.3 and one index of $f$ on the support, which Assumption 15.4 does not fix (the reason for the verdict): the canonical lift is an orientation of $\Lambda \circ f$, given by the kernels of the operators $(f - s_i)'(y)$ oriented by $o$; Definition 15.4.2 is restated there, the canonical orientation being unique (§XV.34, point G.159 settled). The kernel orientation depends on the operator, not only on its kernel, so the local structures are those of $\Lambda$ (§XV.34). Theorem 15.4.3 (explicit) is proved with the canonical orientation, adding the partitions of unity of Part IX's Stokes theorem (§XV.35). Corollary 15.4.4 and its effective version (narrower) are proved for the canonically oriented regular perturbations with the text's hypotheses and one standing hypothesis, sc-smooth partitions of unity on $X$ for Stokes, whose removal is the text's own question (Remark 9.5.19, point G.153); a canonically oriented regular perturbation in general position exists (§XV.37); the oriented cobordism between two regular perturbations is a theorem, with $e \wedge o$ invariant on the cylinder and the weights and signs on the ends proved (points G.182, G.190 settled); for index $0$ the frame convention is read in a signed form; the local unique continuation property on the cylinder follows from that on $X$ (point G.191 settled) (§§XV.36, XV.37). Remark 15.4.5 is prose.

*Points:* all settled: G.144, G.145, G.150–G.152, G.159–G.162, G.177–G.183, G.189–G.191, G.193 (§XV.38). Proposition 15.1.3 at an isolated point is the one statement of the chapter that does not hold as we read it, a degenerate case; no statement of §§15.2–15.4 was found not to hold as we read it.

**The decisions that shape the chapter.** (1) *Formal sums are finitely supported functions.* $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ is the type of finitely supported functions from the linear maps $T_xX \to W_x$ to $\mathbb Q^{\ge 0}$, defined at every level-$1$ point ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.GrF⟧; that the operators are sc-Fredholm is proved for the operators that occur, not built into the type. On a strong bundle model the operators are $T_zO \to F_0$ ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.GrFModel⟧. (2) *The separation argument is pure topology.* Proposition 15.1.3, Lemmas 15.1.4, 15.1.5 and Remark 15.1.6 are proved for finitely many continuous maps into a Hausdorff space and applied on the level-$1$ subspace of the model, where the sections and their derivatives are continuous. (3) *Pairs, not operators.* The proof of Theorem 15.1.1 compares finite formal sums of pairs (value, derivative) over all branches, $\mathrm{wsum}(a, \sigma)(q) = \sum_{i : a_i = q}\sigma_i$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum⟧ with masses of sets ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.mass⟧ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.mass_eq_of_wsum_eq⟧; the sum of the theorem is the part with first entry $f(y)$. (4) *A branch is a section problem of Part I.* A local sc⁺-section $s_i$ on an open $O$ is a global sc⁺-section of the restricted bundle $W|_O$, and $(f - s_i)|_O$ is an sc-Fredholm section there (§XV.10); every analytic statement of §§15.2–15.4 is Part I's theorem applied branch by branch. The price is Part I's hypothesis: *tame germ data* for $f$, inherited from Part I's Theorems 3.1.18 and 3.6.8, which are proved for it; with the untamed notion of 2021 Theorem 3.6.8 does not hold as we read it (Part III). (5) *Operators are read in charts.* $T_xX$ and $W_x$ carry no sc-structure in the project; "sc-Fredholm", "onto on $T^R_xX$" and "$\in \mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$" are read in a strong bundle chart, through the identifications that define the intrinsic operators, and shown independent of the chart where it matters. (6) *The cylinder is a product with Part I's cube.* $[0, 1] \times X$ is built as $[0, 1]^n \times X$ with $n = 1$, so that Part I's parametrized Fredholm theory applies to $\tilde f$ (§XV.30). (7) *Boundary data have non-empty index sets.* A multisection over $\partial X$ has non-empty index sets, as Definition 14.1.3 prints it; so it charges every boundary fibre, which Theorem 15.3.9 needs (§XV.27). (8) *Steps are named propositions.* Where the proof of a theorem has a step of its own (for instance the relative local families of Theorem 15.3.9, the oriented end identities of Corollary 15.4.4), the step is a named statement, the theorem is proved from it, and the statement is proved; the forms with the step as a hypothesis remain as intermediate declarations.

**Map.**

- *The linearization (§15.1).* `Chapter14/SeparationLemmas`: formal sums of points, Proposition 15.1.3 with its witness and the isolated point, Lemmas 15.1.4, 15.1.5, Remark 15.1.6, the limit step and the core lemma (§§XV.1, XV.3–XV.7). `LinearizationModel`: $\mathrm{GrF}$ on a strong bundle model, level-$1$ continuity and locality of derivatives, Theorem 15.1.1 and Remark 15.1.2 on the model (§§XV.2, XV.7). `Linearization`: principal parts and counts in a strong bundle chart, Theorem 15.1.1 in a chart, the chart form of Definition 15.1.7 (§§XV.7, XV.8). `LinearizationIntrinsic`: intrinsic operators, the change of chart, Theorem 15.1.1 with intrinsic operators, Definition 15.1.7 (§§XV.1, XV.7, XV.8). `LinearizationFunctor`: the action of morphisms on $\mathrm{GrF}$ and the functor property (§XV.9).
- *Branches as section problems, and §15.2.* `LocalScPlus`, `LocalIFT`: local sc⁺-sections on $W|_O$, the sections $f - s_i$, the chart linearization, local solution sets (§XV.10). `FredholmSummands`: the sc-Fredholm summands (§XV.11). `Transversality`: Definition 15.2.1, Theorem 15.2.2, Remark 15.2.3 (§§XV.12–XV.14). `BoundaryPerturbation`: Definition 15.2.4, Theorem 15.2.5 (§§XV.15, XV.16). `InteriorPerturbationWitness`, `GoodPositionWitness`, `TameWitness`: witnesses for §15.2. `TameBranched`: tame local solution sets and the tame forms of Remarks 15.2.3, 15.3.6, Theorems 15.2.5, 15.3.7 (§§XV.14, XV.16, XV.20, XV.21). `RestrictedEp`: the translation groupoid $G_x \ltimes U(x)$ and the statements on it (§§XV.14, XV.16). `GeneralPositionLocal`: general position at a point, its relation to good position and to Part I, Theorem 15.2.5 under general position (§XV.16).
- *Definitions of §15.3 and products.* `ControllingPairs`: Definitions 15.3.1, 15.3.3, 15.3.5, the paragraph before Definition 15.3.1, the example after Definition 15.3.3, the first sentence of Remark 15.3.6 (§§XV.17, XV.18, XV.20). `ProductEp`: product ep-groupoids (§XV.19). `ProductStrongBundle`, `ProductBundle`, `ProductBundleLevels`, `ProductBundleMap`, `ProductEpBundle`, `ProductAuxNorm`: product strong bundles, their levels and sections, the product over $X \times X'$, $\max(N, N')$ (§XV.19). `ProductMultisection`, `ProductGerms`, `ProductBasicGerm`, `ProductFredholm`: $\Lambda \times \Lambda'$, Remark 15.3.4, $f \times f'$ sc-Fredholm (§XV.19). `LinearizationLocalIso`, `GeneralPositionEquivalence`, `TransversalityEquivalence`: general position and transversality along local isomorphisms and equivalences (§XV.20). `GoodPositionBranches`, `GeneralPositionBranched`: the rest of Remark 15.3.6, the statement of Theorem 15.3.7 and its "in particular" (§§XV.20, XV.21).
- *Theorem 15.3.7.* `ParamFamily`: linear families, (15.1), sums of families, norm and support bounds (§XV.22). `GeneralPositionAssembly`: the assembly from the local families and the parametric step (§XV.22). `ParamOntoTransport`, `LocalFamilies`, `LocalSeeds`: (15.1) with vectors and along morphisms, seeds, the local families (§XV.23). `ParamOntoBridge`: (15.1) and Part I's conditions (i)–(ii) (§XV.24). `LocalSlice`, `BranchChart`, `ParametricGeneralPosition`: the local Sard step, a branch as a Part I parametrized section, achieving global transversality (§XV.25). `GeneralPositionPointwise`, `GeneralPositionGoodSystem`, `GeneralPositionExistence`: the theorem with the text's hypotheses, the structurable form, Remark 15.3.8 (§XV.26).
- *Theorem 15.3.9.* `TransversalExtension`: the face condition and the reduction to the steps of the proof (§§XV.27, XV.29). `BoundaryExtensionIndependence`: the face condition for one extension (§XV.27). `Chapter10/TameBundleCharts`, `CutoffFunctor`: tame strong bundle charts, step (a) (§XV.28). `RelativeFamilies`, `RelativeBranch`, `RelativeParametric`, `RelativeReduction`, `RelativeSeeds`, `RelativeSeedFamilies`, `RelativeStepBump`: relative (15.1), relative families and seeds, the relative parametric step (§XV.29). `RelativeLocalFamilies`: the relative local families, Theorem 15.3.9, Corollary 15.3.10 (1) (§§XV.27, XV.29, XV.31). `ChargedExtensionFibres`: step (a) and the forms with charging (§§XV.27–XV.29). `TransversalExtensionWitness`, `TransversalExtensionBoundaryDatum`, `SingleSectionMultisection`: the half-plane witnesses (§§XV.27, XV.29, XV.31).
- *The cylinder and Corollary 15.3.10.* `CubeEp`: $[0, 1]^n \times X$, $\widetilde W$, $\tilde f$ (§XV.30). `Chapter7/DegeneracyZeroTame`: $d_X \equiv 0$ implies tame (§XV.30). `CubeAuxNorm`: Corollary 15.3.10 (2) (§XV.31). `RegularPerturbations`, `Cylinder`: regular perturbations, Corollary 15.3.10 (1), the orbit space of the cylinder (§§XV.30, XV.31, XV.33). `CylinderGlue`: the glued boundary data, the last paragraph of the proof (§§XV.32, XV.33). `Chapter6/CubePartitions`, `CylinderPartitions`: partitions of unity of the cylinder (§XV.32). `CubeLift`, `GlueStructurable`: lifted structures and the structurability of the glued data (§XV.32). `CubeLiftScPlus`: lifted sc⁺-sections on the faces, structurability of the glued data (§XV.32). `CylinderFaceOnto`, `CylinderSliceKernels`: the face condition of the glued data (§XV.32). `CylinderPullStructurable`, `CylinderCollarNorm`, `CylinderExtensionBump`: the explicit extension, the collar norm, Corollary 15.3.10 (3) (§XV.33). `FaceOntoCorollaries`, `TransversalExtensionCorollaries`, `ChargedExtensionCylinder`: the text's route and the intermediate forms (§XV.33).
- *§15.4.* `OrientedLinearizations`, `CanonicalLift`: oriented formal sums and the canonical lift at a chart point (§XV.34). `BranchKernelTangent`, `KernelBranching`, `KernelBranchingIndex`, `KernelChartChange`, `KernelFramesPerturbed`, `CanonicalOrientation`, `CanonicalLiftIndependence`, `CanonicalLiftNaturality`, `CanonicalLiftContinuity`, `Chapter6/TangentOrientationFamily`: Theorem 15.4.1 and Definition 15.4.2 in $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}(X)$ (§XV.34). `CanonicalOrientationOrientedSection`, `CanonicalOrientationLocal`, `CanonicalOrientationEquivalence`, `CanonicalOrientationPullback`: further forms (§XV.34). `PerturbedStokesInvariants`, `CanonicalOrientationStokes`: Theorem 15.4.3, the cobordism argument (§§XV.35, XV.36). `CylinderEnds`, `CylinderEndWeights`, `GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight`, `CylinderCobordism`, `CylinderCobordismReduced`, `CubeLUC`: the ends, their weights, the boundary measure, the local unique continuation property on the cylinder (§XV.37). `CylinderProductOrientation`, `CylinderOrientationInvariance`, `Chapter12/IntervalProductLocalIso`, `CylinderCanonicalOrientation`: $e \wedge o$ and Theorem 15.4.1 on the cylinder (§XV.37). `CylinderEndBranching`, `CylinderEndStructures`, `CylinderEndOrientationSign`, `BoundaryFrameCriterion`, `CylinderFaceIntegration`, `CylinderEndLocalization`, `CylinderEndCanonical`, `CylinderEndCorollary`, `CylinderEndEffectiveMatching`: the end identities (§XV.37). `CollarCobordismCorollary`: Corollary 15.4.4 and its effective version (§XV.37). `CylinderPointFrames`, `CylinderPointEnds`, `Chapter16/IndexZeroCylinder`: index $0$ (§XV.37).

Throughout, $(P : W \to X, \mu)$ is a strong bundle over an ep-groupoid, $f$ an sc-Fredholm section functor, and $\Lambda$ an sc⁺-multisection with local sc⁺-section structures $(s_i, \sigma_i)$, the local representations of Definition 13.2.1 on open sets ⟦Chapter11/MultisectionLocalStructure · SBLocalization.IsLocalRepOn⟧ (Part XIII). "Part I's" refers to Part I of the book (Chapters 1–6, M-polyfolds), whose statements Parts I–VI of this book retell.

### XV.1 The preamble of §15.1: $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ and finite formal sums

The text (p. 683) fixes, at a smooth point $x$, the set of sc-Fredholm operators $L : T_xX \to W_x$, and lets $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ consist of the finite formal sums $\sum\sigma_L \cdot L$ with non-negative rational coefficients; a morphism $\varphi : x \to y$ acts by $\varphi_*L \circ T\varphi = \mu(\varphi, L(\cdot))$, and $\mathrm{GrF}(X) \to X_\infty$ becomes a category over $X$. Three objects of the formalization stand for this preamble.

**$\mathrm{GrF}(x)$** (restated) ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.GrF⟧. For a level-$1$ point $x$, $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ is the type of finitely supported functions from the linear maps $T_xX \to W_x$ to $\mathbb Q^{\ge 0}$, where $T_xX$ is the project's tangent fibre of the object M-polyfold (Part II of the book, with the linear structure of Chapter 2) and $W_x = P^{-1}(x)$ is the fibre with its vector space structure. Two departures from the text are deliberate. First, the point ranges over $X_1$, not only over $X_\infty$: the linearization of a section at a zero is defined at level-$1$ points already, and everything proved below holds there. Second, the operators are all linear maps, not only the sc-Fredholm ones. The sc-Fredholm property is a property of the operators that actually occur, proved for them in §XV.11; it is not built into the type, so that $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ is a plain free commutative monoid on which sums, push-forwards and supports behave without side conditions. The text's $\mathrm{GrF}(X)$ and the category structure are not built as such; the action of morphisms is defined in §XV.9, where it is needed.

**On a strong bundle model** ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.GrFModel⟧. On a strong bundle model $U \triangleleft F$ with retraction $R$ and base retraction $r$, $O = r(U)$, the same object at a level-$1$ point $z$ is the type of finitely supported functions on the bounded operators $T_zO \to F_0$, where $T_zO = \operatorname{range} Dr(z)$ is the tangent space of the retract, as in Part I's linearization in a chart ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationCharts · linAt⟧. This is the form in which the analysis is done.

**Intrinsic operators from chart operators.** An operator of a strong bundle chart $c = (\varphi, \Phi)$ around $x$ becomes an element of $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ in two steps. First $L : E_0 \to F_0$ is read along $T_xX$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.chartOp⟧: $\alpha \mapsto L(T\varphi\,\alpha)$, where $T\varphi$ identifies $T_xX$ with the tangent space $T_{\varphi(x)}O$ of the model ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.chartOp_apply⟧. Then the values are pulled back to $W_x$ through the fibre part $\Phi_x : W_x \to F_0$ of the chart, which is linear and injective ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.toIntr⟧: if every value $L(T\varphi\,\alpha)$ lies in the range of $\Phi_x$, the operator is $\Phi_x^{-1} \circ L \circ T\varphi : T_xX \to W_x$, and otherwise it is $0$. The default value is never used: at a zero of a section that is sc-smooth in the chart, the chart derivative takes values in $\Phi_x(W_x)$ (§XV.7, step (f)), and then $\Phi_x \circ (\text{intrinsic operator}) = L \circ T\varphi$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.fiberLin_toIntr⟧.

**Finite formal sums of points.** The proof of Theorem 15.1.1 compares formal sums of *points*, not of operators, and the formalization makes these first-class. For finitely many points $a_i$ of a set $Q$ with weights $\sigma_i$ in a commutative monoid, the coefficient of $q$ in $\sum\sigma_i \cdot a_i$ is
$$\mathrm{wsum}(a, \sigma)(q) = \sum_{i : a_i = q}\sigma_i$$
⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum⟧, and the mass of a set $N \subseteq Q$ is $\mathrm{mass}(a, \sigma)(N) = \sum_{i : a_i \in N}\sigma_i$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.mass⟧. The mass is the sum of the coefficients over any finite set $S$ containing all $a_i$, restricted to $N$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.mass_eq_sum_wsum⟧ (exchange the two finite sums), so two families with the same coefficients have the same mass on every set: take $S$ the union of the two finite images ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.mass_eq_of_wsum_eq⟧. The coefficient of $q'$ in the image $\sum\sigma_i \cdot \psi(a_i)$ is the mass of $\psi^{-1}(q')$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum_map⟧, so equal formal sums have equal images under every map ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum_map_eq_of_wsum_eq⟧. This last remark is the formal content of "select the terms with $s_i(y) = f(y)$, then forget the value": both are images, and images respect equality.

### XV.2 Theorem 15.1.1 (2017: 15.1) and Remark 15.1.2 (2017: 15.1): the statements as formalized

**The text.** For a strong bundle $(P : W \to X, \mu)$ over an ep-groupoid, an sc-Fredholm section $f$ and an sc⁺-multisection $\Lambda$, there is a section functor $T(f, \Lambda)$ of $\mathrm{GrF}(X) \to X_\infty$, uniquely characterized by (1) $T(f, \Lambda)(x) = 0$ off $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$, and (2) for $x$ in the support and any local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)_{i \in I}$ describing $\Lambda$ around $x$, $T(f, \Lambda)(y) = \sum_{\{i : f(y) = s_i(y)\}}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(y)$ at smooth $y$ near $x$ (p. 684). The substance is that the right side of (2) does not depend on the structure; existence and uniqueness then follow from the covering of $X$ by structures. A local sc⁺-section structure is the local representation of Definition 13.2.1 (Part XIII) on an open set: sc⁺-sections $s_1, \dots, s_k$ on $O$ with positive rational weights summing to $1$ such that $\Lambda(w) = \sum_{i : w = s_i(P(w))}\sigma_i$ for $w$ over $O$ ⟦Chapter11/MultisectionLocalStructure · SBLocalization.IsLocalRepOn⟧, with $s_i$ an sc⁺-section on $O$ ⟦Chapter11/MultisectionLocalStructure · SBLocalization.IsScPlusSectionOn⟧, and an sc⁺-multisection is a functor $W \to \mathbb Q^{\ge 0}$ with such a structure on the domain of a natural representation around every object ⟦Chapter11/MultisectionLocalStructure · SBLocalization.IsScPlusMultisection⟧.

The formalization states the theorem three times, on three levels of the reduction "without loss of generality $X = O$", and then once more for the value of Definition 15.1.7 (§XV.8) and for the functor property (§XV.9).

**On a strong bundle model** (stronger than the text's local statement) ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.theorem_15_1_1_model⟧. Let $U \triangleleft F$ be a strong bundle model ($U$ relatively open in a partial quadrant, $R$ a strong bundle retraction, $O = r(U)$), and let $\hat f$, $\hat s_1, \dots, \hat s_k$, $\hat t_1, \dots, \hat t_l$ be principal parts $E_0 \to F_0$ with $\hat f \circ r$, $\hat s_i \circ r$, $\hat t_j \circ r$ sc-smooth on $U$. The formal sum at a level-$1$ point $z$ is
$$\sum_{i : \hat s_i(z) = \hat f(z)}\sigma_i \cdot (\hat f - \hat s_i)'(z) \in \mathrm{GrF}_{\mathrm{model}}(z),\qquad (\hat f - \hat s_i)'(z) = D((\hat f - \hat s_i) \circ r)(z)|_{T_zO}$$
⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.linSumModel⟧, with its coefficient of an operator $\ell$ the sum of the $\sigma_i$ with $\hat s_i(z) = \hat f(z)$ and $(\hat f - \hat s_i)'(z) = \ell$ ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.linSumModel_apply⟧. If the two structures give the same weighted count $\sum_{i : \hat s_i(y) = v}\sigma_i = \sum_{j : \hat t_j(y) = v}\tau_j$ of every value $v$ at every $y \in O \cap N$, $N$ open, then the two formal sums agree at every level-$1$ point of $O \cap N$. The same holds after replacing each operator $D((\hat f - \hat s_i) \circ r)(z)$ by its image under any map $\psi$ of operators ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.linSumModelMap⟧ ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.linSumModelMap_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.theorem_15_1_1_model_map⟧; the map that matters is the passage to intrinsic operators. The statement is stronger than what the proof of the text needs in two ways: all level-$1$ points instead of the smooth ones, and no condition on $\Lambda$ at all, only that the two structures have the same counts. Neither positivity of the weights nor functoriality is used.

**In a strong bundle chart** (stronger) ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.theorem_15_1_1⟧. For an sc-Fredholm section functor $f$ and two local sc⁺-section structures $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ on $O$ and $(t_j, \tau_j)$ on $O'$ of the same $\Lambda$, the formal sums $\sum_{i : f(y) = s_i(y)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(y)$, read in a strong bundle chart $c$ with domain in $O \cap O'$ ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.linSumChart⟧, agree at every level-$1$ point of the chart. The same holds for any sc-smooth section $f$ ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.linSumChart_eq⟧; this is what the proof uses.

**With intrinsic operators** (narrower, as the row of the theorem; stronger in its substance) ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.theorem_15_1_1_intrinsic⟧. The formal sum in $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ through a chart $c$,
$$\sum_{i : f(x) = s_i(x)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(x),\qquad (f - s_i)'(x) = \Phi_x^{-1} \circ D((\hat f - \hat s_i) \circ r)(\varphi(x)) \circ T\varphi$$
⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linSumIntr⟧, is the same for two structures of $\Lambda$ on open $O$, $O'$, computed through any chart $c$ with domain in $O$ and any chart $c'$ with domain in $O'$, at every $x \in X_1$ in both domains. This is the text's (2) with the operators in their intrinsic form, and the formulation in which Definition 15.1.7 is made.

*Why the row is narrower.* What Theorem 15.1.1 claims beyond the independence is (a) that $T(f, \Lambda)$ is a *section functor*, which is proved at all level-$1$ points in §XV.9, and (b) that its values lie in $\mathrm{GrF}$, i.e. that the summands are sc-Fredholm. (b) is proved in §XV.11 only in a chart reading, since $T_xX$ and $W_x$ carry no sc-structure of their own in the project, and only for $f$ with Part I's tame germ data, the hypothesis of Part I's implicit function theorem on which the proof rests. That inherited hypothesis is the only reason the row is narrower; the independence itself is proved in a stronger form. The text's "uniquely characterized" is the remark that (1) and (2) determine the value at every point, which in the formalization is Definition 15.1.7 with $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ equal to the sum of *any* local data (§XV.8).

**Remark 15.1.2** (restated). The text observes that $(f - s_i)'(y)$ is undefined where $f(y) \ne s_i(y)$ but is not counted, and that with the empty sum equal to $0$, (1) follows from (2). In the formalization the operator $(\hat f - \hat s_i)'(z)$ is defined for every $i$, and the sum is filtered by $\hat s_i(z) = \hat f(z)$; so the first half of the remark is the definition of the formal sum. The second half is proved on each level. *On the model* ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.remark_15_1_2_model⟧: if the weights are positive and the weighted count of $\hat f(z)$ is $0$, then each term of that count is $0$, so no $i$ has $\hat s_i(z) = \hat f(z)$ (a term with $\hat s_i(z) = \hat f(z)$ would contribute $\sigma_i > 0$), every summand of the formal sum is the empty case, and the formal sum is $0$. The same counting gives the description of the support used by Definition 15.2.1: with positive weights an operator $\ell$ has a nonzero coefficient iff $\ell = (\hat f - \hat s_i)'(z)$ for some $i$ with $\hat s_i(z) = \hat f(z)$ ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.mem_support_linSumModel_iff⟧. *In a chart* ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.remark_15_1_2⟧: where $\Lambda(f(y)) = 0$, the weighted count of $\hat f(\varphi(y))$ in the chart is $\Lambda(\Phi^{-1}(\varphi(y), \hat f(\varphi(y)))) = \Lambda(f(y)) = 0$ (the count formula of §XV.7, step (e)), so the model remark applies. *Intrinsically*, Theorem 15.1.1 (1) ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linearization_eq_zero⟧: $T(f, \Lambda)(x) = 0$ where $\Lambda(f(x)) = 0$, by the same count read through the data that define $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ (§XV.8).

### XV.3 Proposition 15.1.3 (2017: 15.1): separating sequences

**The separation property** at a point $z$ for a partition $e : I \to \{1, \dots, p\}$ of the index set ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.IsSeparatedAt⟧: (1) $s_i(z) = s_{i'}(z)$ iff $e(i) = e(i')$, and (2) if $e(i) = e(i')$ then $s_i = s_{i'}$ on a neighbourhood of $z$. This is the conclusion of the proposition at one point $x_k$.

**Statement** (narrower) ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.proposition_15_1_3_hyp⟧. Let $Z$ be a first countable $T_1$ space, $s_i$, $i \in I$ finite, maps continuous on an open $V \ni x_0$ with values in a Hausdorff space, and suppose that $x_0$ is not isolated. Then there are $x_k \in V$ with $x_k \ne x_0$ and $x_k \to x_0$, and one surjective partition $e : I \to \{1, \dots, p\}$, such that the separation property holds at every $x_k$. The text states it on the local model $O$, with the metric of $E_0$, $x_0 = 0$ and the sections of a local sc⁺-section structure. The formal statement is more general in its setting (topological, no sc-structure, the maps only continuous) and narrower by one hypothesis.

**Proof.** *Step 1, points near $x_0$ where the maps are dichotomous.* Call $z$ *locally dichotomous* for the family if every pair of the maps either coincides on a neighbourhood of $z$ or differs at $z$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.IsLocDich⟧. Every nonempty open set on which the maps are continuous contains such a point ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_isLocDich_mem⟧: Lemma 15.1.5 in its topological form (§XV.5) gives a nonempty open $W$ inside it on which each pair either coincides or differs everywhere, and any point of $W$ will do (coincidence on $W$ is coincidence near the point, since $W$ is open). Now take a decreasing countable basis $(u_k)$ of neighbourhoods of $x_0$, which exists by first countability. For each $k$ the set $\operatorname{int}(u_k) \cap V \setminus \{x_0\}$ is open, because $Z$ is $T_1$ so that $\{x_0\}$ is closed; and it is nonempty, because it belongs to the punctured neighbourhood filter of $x_0$, which is not trivial since $x_0$ is not isolated. So it contains a locally dichotomous point $z_k$; $z_k \ne x_0$, $z_k \in V$, and $z_k \to x_0$ since $z_k \in u_k$ and the $u_k$ form a decreasing basis.

*Step 2, the subsequence* ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_isSeparatedAt_subseq⟧. At each $z_k$ the *pattern* of coincidences is the relation $\{(i, j) : s_i(z_k) = s_j(z_k)\}$ on $I \times I$. There are finitely many such relations, so one of them, $P$, occurs for infinitely many $k$ (a map from $\mathbb N$ to a finite set has an infinite fibre), and an extraction $k \mapsto \phi(k)$, strictly increasing, picks those $k$. The partition $e$ is taken from the values at $z_{\phi(0)}$: $e(i) = e(j)$ iff $s_i(z_{\phi(0)}) = s_j(z_{\phi(0)})$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_fin_partition⟧, the classes of equal values numbered by a finite set. Since the pattern is $P$ at every $z_{\phi(k)}$, (1) holds at every point of the subsequence. For (2): if $e(i) = e(j)$ then $s_i(z_{\phi(k)}) = s_j(z_{\phi(k)})$, and since $z_{\phi(k)}$ is locally dichotomous, the pair cannot differ there, so it coincides near $z_{\phi(k)}$. The sequence of the proposition is $x_k = z_{\phi(k)}$.

*How this departs from the text's proof.* The text picks $y_k \to 0$, $y_k \ne 0$, applies Lemma 15.1.5 on small balls around $y_k$ to get $z_k$ with partitions $I = I^k_1 \sqcup \dots \sqcup I^k_{p_k}$, and then takes successive subsequences making $I^k_1$, $I^k_2$, … constant. The formal proof takes a single subsequence on which the whole pattern is constant, by the pigeonhole principle on the finite set of relations on $I$; the result is the same. The points come from a neighbourhood basis rather than from a sequence chosen in advance, so no metric is needed.

**The added hypothesis, and the isolated point.** That $x_0$ is not isolated is not derived from the text; it is what makes the punctured neighbourhoods nonempty in Step 1. Its witness is two maps $z \mapsto 0$ and $z \mapsto z$ on $\mathbb R$ at $0$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.proposition_15_1_3_hyp_witness⟧, where all hypotheses hold and the proposition applies. **At an isolated point, Proposition 15.1.3 does not hold as we read it.** If $\{x_0\}$ is open, no sequence $x_k \ne x_0$ converges to $x_0$: convergence would put some $x_k$ in the open set $\{x_0\}$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.not_proposition_15_1_3_of_isolated⟧. This happens for the one-point local model $O = \{0\}$ of a zero-dimensional $X$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.not_proposition_15_1_3_point⟧. The case is degenerate, and nothing depends on it: the formal proof of Theorem 15.1.1 does not use the proposition, because its core lemma needs separated points in every neighbourhood of $x_0$, and $x_0$ itself may be one of them (§XV.7). At an isolated point the theorem therefore holds without change.

### XV.4 Lemma 15.1.4 (2017: 15.1): finite closed covers of a ball

**Statement** (stronger) ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.lemma_15_1_4⟧. In any pseudo-metric space, if a ball $B_\varepsilon(y)$, $\varepsilon > 0$, is the union of finitely many sets $A_i$ that are closed in $B_\varepsilon(y)$, then one of the $A_i$ has nonempty interior. The text states it for $O$ with the metric of $E_0$ (p. 685); the formal statement holds in every pseudo-metric space.

**Proof.** *The topological core* ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.interior_biUnion_eq_empty⟧: in any topological space, a finite union of closed sets with empty interiors has empty interior. By induction over the finite index set: the empty union is empty; and if $A$ is closed with empty interior and $B$ (the union so far) has empty interior, then $A \cup B$ has empty interior, Mathlib's fact for the union of a closed set and a set with empty interior. *The cover form* ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_nonempty_interior_of_finite_closed_cover⟧: a nonempty open set $V$ covered by finitely many closed sets meets the interior of one of them. Otherwise all interiors are empty, so the union has empty interior by the core; but $V$ is open and contained in the union, so every point of $V$ lies in the interior of the union. *The lemma* is the cover form in the subspace $B_\varepsilon(y)$, which is nonempty (it contains $y$) and open in itself: the preimages of the $A_i$ in the subspace are closed by hypothesis and cover it, so one of them has a point $x$ in its interior in the subspace. The image of that interior under the inclusion is open in the ambient space, because the ball is open, and it lies in $A_i$; so $x$ lies in the interior of $A_i$.

*How this departs from the text's proof.* The text moves a point $z$ step by step, each step adding to the set of indices $i$ with $z \notin \Sigma_i$, until $z$ lies in the interior of some $\Sigma_i$. The formal proof is the induction on the index set above. Neither uses the Baire property, which a finite union does not need. The lemma is not used by the formal proof of Lemma 15.1.5 (§XV.5); it is formalized as stated.

### XV.5 Lemma 15.1.5 (2017: 15.2): local dichotomy of finitely many maps

**Statement** (stronger) ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.lemma_15_1_5⟧. In a pseudo-metric space, let $s_i$, $i \in I$ finite, be maps continuous on a ball $B_\varepsilon(y)$, $\varepsilon > 0$, with values in a Hausdorff space. Then there are $z$ with $d(z, y) < \varepsilon$, $\delta > 0$ and a surjective partition $e : I \to \{1, \dots, p\}$ such that (1) $B_\delta(z) \subseteq B_\varepsilon(y)$, (2) $s_i = s_{i'}$ on $B_\delta(z)$ when $e(i) = e(i')$, and (3) $s_i(w) \ne s_j(w)$ for $w \in B_\delta(z)$ when $e(i) \ne e(j)$. The text (pp. 685–686) states it for the sections of a local sc⁺-section structure on $O$; the formal maps need only be continuous, with no linear or sc-structure.

**The topological form** ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_isOpen_dichotomy⟧: for finitely many maps continuous on a nonempty open set $V$ of a topological space, with values in a Hausdorff space, there is a nonempty open $W \subseteq V$ on which every pair of maps either coincides everywhere or differs everywhere.

*Proof.* By induction over a finite set $S$ of pairs $(i, j)$, the claim being that some nonempty open $W \subseteq V$ has the dichotomy for the pairs in $S$. For $S = \emptyset$ take $W = V$. For the step, let $W$ serve the pairs of $S$ and add the pair $(i, j)$. The map $w \mapsto (s_i(w), s_j(w))$ is continuous on $W$, and the complement of the diagonal is open because the target is Hausdorff, so
$$W' = \{w \in W : s_i(w) \ne s_j(w)\}$$
is open. If $W'$ is empty, then $s_i = s_j$ on $W$, and $W$ serves $S \cup \{(i, j)\}$. If $W'$ is nonempty, then $W'$ serves the new pair (the maps differ everywhere on it) and still serves the pairs of $S$, since coincidence or difference everywhere on $W$ passes to the subset $W'$. Applying this to the set of all pairs gives the claim.

*The lemma from the topological form.* Apply it to the ball, open and nonempty: it gives $W$ and a point $z \in W$; $d(z, y) < \varepsilon$ since $W$ lies in the ball. As $W$ is open, some $B_\delta(z) \subseteq W$, which gives (1). The partition is that of the values at $z$ ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_fin_partition⟧: $e(i) = e(j)$ iff $s_i(z) = s_j(z)$. For (2), if $e(i) = e(i')$ the pair cannot differ everywhere on $W$ (it agrees at $z$), so it coincides on $W \supseteq B_\delta(z)$. For (3), if $e(i) \ne e(j)$ the pair cannot coincide on $W$ (it differs at $z$), so it differs everywhere on $W$.

*How this departs from the text's proof.* The text builds the pieces one at a time: it fixes $i_1$, considers the closed sets $\Sigma_j = \{s_j = s_{i_1}\}$, uses Lemma 15.1.4 when they cover the ball, moves the point slightly, and repeats with $i_2 \notin I_1$ on a ball of a quarter of the radius. The formal proof goes through the pairs instead of the pieces and shrinks the open set at each step to where the new pair differs, if that set is not empty. The argument for a single pair is the one observation that the set where two continuous maps into a Hausdorff space differ is open; if it is empty on an open set, the maps agree there. So Lemma 15.1.4 is not needed. The same form gives the locally dichotomous points used in §§XV.3, XV.6 and XV.7 ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_isLocDich_mem⟧.

### XV.6 Remark 15.1.6: modifying the sequence

The remark, new in the 2021 edition (p. 686), makes two observations: a sequence with the properties of Proposition 15.1.3 can be obtained from any sequence $x'_k \to 0$ by modifying it "slightly" and passing to a subsequence; and modifying such a sequence slightly keeps the properties. Both are formalized, with "slightly" made explicit (restated).

**First part** ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.remark_15_1_6⟧. In a metric space, for finitely many maps continuous on an open $V$ with values in a Hausdorff space and any sequence $x'_k \to x_0$ in $V$, there are $x''_k \in V$ with $d(x'_k, x''_k) < 1/(k + 1)$ and $x''_k \to x_0$, a strictly increasing $\phi$, and a partition $e$ with the separation property at every $x''_{\phi(k)}$. *Proof.* For each $k$ the set $B_{1/(k+1)}(x'_k) \cap V$ is open and nonempty (it contains $x'_k$), so it contains a locally dichotomous point $x''_k$ (§XV.3, Step 1). Then $d(x''_k, x_0) \le d(x''_k, x'_k) + d(x'_k, x_0)$, and both terms tend to $0$, the first being below $1/(k + 1)$; by squeezing, $x''_k \to x_0$. The subsequence and the partition come from the pigeonhole step of §XV.3 ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.exists_isSeparatedAt_subseq⟧. "Slightly" is the bound $1/(k + 1)$, which gives the text's $d(x'_k, x''_k) \to 0$. The modified points need not differ from $x_0$; if $x'_k \ne x_0$ is wanted as well, one removes $x_0$ from the open sets as in §XV.3.

**Second part** ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.IsSeparatedAt.eventually⟧. If the separation property holds at $z$ for a partition $e$ and the maps are continuous at $z$, it holds at every $z'$ in a neighbourhood of $z$. *Proof*, pair by pair, finitely many conditions each holding on a neighbourhood. For a pair in one piece, $s_i = s_j$ on a neighbourhood $N$ of $z$; on the open interior of $N$ both clauses hold at every point, (1) because the values agree and (2) because the germs agree. For a pair in different pieces, $s_i(z) \ne s_j(z)$, so $(s_i(z), s_j(z))$ lies off the diagonal, which is closed in a Hausdorff space; by continuity of $w \mapsto (s_i(w), s_j(w))$ at $z$, the values differ on a neighbourhood, and (1) holds there while (2) is vacuous for the pair. So small moves of the points of a separating sequence keep it separating, which is the text's second observation; "slightly" means "inside this neighbourhood".

### XV.7 Proof of Theorem 15.1.1: the core lemma, the model, the chart, the change of chart

The proof follows the text's reduction to a local model and its limit argument, with three changes: the separation runs in the level-$1$ topology, the two structures are separated together without halving weights, and the formal sums compared are of pairs (value, derivative) over all branches. The steps are (a) a limit lemma for finite formal sums, (b) the comparison at a separated point, (c) the core lemma in a topological space, (d) the model, (e) the chart, (f) the change of chart and the intrinsic statement.

**(a) The limit step** ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum_eq_of_frequently⟧. Let $a_i(z)$, $b_j(z)$ be finitely many points of a Hausdorff space $Q$, depending on $z$ in a topological space and continuous at $x_0$, with weights $\sigma_i$, $\tau_j$. If the formal sums $\sum\sigma_i \cdot a_i(z)$ and $\sum\tau_j \cdot b_j(z)$ agree for $z$ frequently near $x_0$ (that is, in every neighbourhood of $x_0$ there is such a $z$), they agree at $x_0$. *Proof.* Fix $q \in Q$. For each point $p \ne q$ choose disjoint neighbourhoods $N_p \ni q$ and $V_p \ni p$ (Hausdorff); for $p = q$ take $N_q = V_q = Q$. Let $N_0$ be the intersection of the finitely many $N_p$ for $p$ among the values $a_i(x_0)$, $b_j(x_0)$: a neighbourhood of $q$. For $z$ near $x_0$, and each $i$: if $a_i(x_0) = q$, then $N_0$ is a neighbourhood of $a_i(x_0)$ and $a_i(z) \in N_0$ by continuity; if $a_i(x_0) = p \ne q$, then $a_i(z) \in V_p$, which is disjoint from $N_p \supseteq N_0$. So eventually $a_i(z) \in N_0 \iff a_i(x_0) = q$, for all $i$, and likewise for the $b_j$. Choose $z$ with this property at which the two formal sums agree. Then the mass of $N_0$ under the first sum at $z$ is the coefficient of $q$ in the first sum at $x_0$, the mass under the second is that of the second, and the two masses are equal because the formal sums at $z$ are (§XV.1) ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.mass_eq_of_wsum_eq⟧. This is the text's convergence "in the weak topology" of the formal sums: the points cluster near their limits, and a neighbourhood that separates one limit from the others carries the limit's coefficient.

**(b) At a locally dichotomous point** ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum_pair_eq_at_of_isLocDich⟧. Let the branches $c_\alpha$, $\alpha \in I \sqcup K$, of two families with weights $\sigma$ on $I$ and $\tau$ on $K$ give the same weighted count of every value at $u$, let $u$ be locally dichotomous for the combined family, and let the "derivatives" $D_\alpha(u)$ depend only on germs: $c_\alpha = c_\beta$ near $u$ implies $D_\alpha(u) = D_\beta(u)$. Then the formal sums of the pairs $(c_\alpha(u), D_\alpha(u))$ over $I$ and over $K$ agree. *Proof.* Fix a pair $(v, L)$. Two branches through $v$ at $u$ cannot differ at $u$, so by dichotomy they coincide near $u$, and by locality they have the same $D$. Hence there is one $L_v$ with $D_\alpha(u) = L_v$ for every branch through $v$ (any $L$ if there is none). For such a family, the coefficient of $(v, L)$ is the coefficient of $v$ if $L = L_v$ and $0$ otherwise ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum_pair_of_const⟧: the pairs with first entry $v$ are exactly $(v, L_v)$. Applying this to both families, with the same $L_v$ since it was chosen for the combined family, the coefficients are the counts of $v$, which agree.

**(c) The core lemma** ⟦Chapter14/SeparationLemmas · Linearization15.wsum_pair_eq_of_isLocal⟧. Let both families be continuous on an open $V$, with equal weighted counts of every value at every point of $V$, and let the $D_\alpha$ be continuous on $V$ and depend only on germs at every point of $V$. Then at every $x_0 \in V$ the formal sums $\sum\sigma_i \cdot (c_i, D_i)(x_0)$ and $\sum\tau_j \cdot (c_j, D_j)(x_0)$ agree. *Proof.* The maps $z \mapsto (c_\alpha(z), D_\alpha(z))$ are continuous at $x_0$, so the limit step (a) applies once the sums agree frequently near $x_0$. Given a neighbourhood $N$ of $x_0$, the set $\operatorname{int}(N) \cap V$ is open and contains $x_0$, so it contains a point $u$ locally dichotomous for the combined family (§XV.3, Step 1); by (b) the sums agree at $u$. *Departure from the text.* The text forms the union of the two structures with halved weights, applies Proposition 15.1.3 to it, and compares multiplicities piece by piece along the sequence. Here the dichotomy is applied to the combined family $I \sqcup K$ directly, which needs no new structure and no weights. The points $u$ may include $x_0$ itself, so no sequence $x_k \ne x_0$ is needed and isolated points are covered; this is why Proposition 15.1.3 is not used and its failure at isolated points (§XV.3) does not reach the theorem.

**(d) On the model.** Two facts of the sc-calculus at level $1$ come first. *Continuity of values* ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.continuousOn_comp_toBase_one⟧: an sc⁰ map is continuous on $U_1$ in the level-$1$ topology, read in $F_0$ (it is continuous $U_1 \to F_1$, and $F_1 \to F_0$ is continuous). *Continuity of derivatives* ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.continuousOn_scDeriv_coe⟧: for an sc¹ map $g$, $u \mapsto Dg(u)$ is continuous on $U_1$ (level-$1$ topology) into the operators $E_0 \to F_0$ with the topology of pointwise convergence. This is Definition 1.1.15 (3), the continuity of $(u, h) \mapsto Dg(u)h$ on $U_1 \times E_0$, read one $h$ at a time. *Locality of the derivative* ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.scDeriv_eq_of_eventuallyEq_lvl⟧: if two sc¹ maps $g$, $g'$ on an sc-domain agree near a level-$1$ point $x$ in the level-$1$ topology, then $Dg(x) = Dg'(x)$. Both derivatives are Fréchet derivatives of $g \circ \iota$, $g' \circ \iota$ within $U_1$ at $x$; the two composites agree near $x$ within $U_1$, so they have the same derivatives there, and within an sc-domain the derivative within $U_1$ is unique; finally an operator on $E_0$ is determined by its values on the dense $E_1$.

*The comparison on the model* ⟦Chapter14/LinearizationModel · Linearization15.wsum_model_eq⟧. Let the two structures have the same counts at every $y \in O \cap N$. Work on the space $Z = U_1$, the level-$1$ points of $U$ with the level-$1$ topology, and on its open subset $V = \{u : r(u) \in N\}$, open because $u \mapsto r(u)$ is continuous on $U_1$ (r is sc-smooth, so sc⁰). The combined family has branches $c_\alpha(u) = \hat s_\alpha(r(u))$ (the $\hat s_i$ and the $\hat t_j$ together) and derivatives $D_\alpha(u) = D((\hat f - \hat s_\alpha) \circ r)(u)$, regarded as functions $E_0 \to F_0$. The hypotheses of the core lemma hold. Continuity: the $c_\alpha$ by the first fact, since $\hat s_\alpha \circ r$ is sc-smooth; the $D_\alpha$ by the second, since $(\hat f - \hat s_\alpha) \circ r$ is sc-smooth, a difference of sc-smooth maps. Counts: at $u \in V$ they are the counts at $r(u) \in O \cap N$, equal by hypothesis. Locality: if $c_\alpha = c_\beta$ near $u$ in $U_1$, then $(\hat f - \hat s_\alpha) \circ r$ and $(\hat f - \hat s_\beta) \circ r$ agree near $u$ in $U_1$ (both are $\hat f \circ r$ minus the branch), so their derivatives agree at $u$ by the locality fact. The core lemma gives equal formal sums of pairs at the point $z$, which lies in $U_1$ because it lies over $O \subseteq U$, and in $V$ because $r(z) = z$ for $z \in O$.

*Theorem 15.1.1 on the model*, from the pairs. The coefficient of an operator $\ell : T_zO \to F_0$ in the formal sum of a structure is the mass, under the formal sum of pairs, of the set
$$N_\ell = \{(v, D) : v = \hat f(z),\ D(h) = \ell(h)\ \text{for all } h \in T_zO\}.$$
Indeed, the summand $i$ contributes $\sigma_i$ iff $\hat s_i(z) = \hat f(z)$ and $(\hat f - \hat s_i)'(z) = \ell$, and the latter, an equality of operators on $T_zO$, says that the full derivative $D((\hat f - \hat s_i) \circ r)(z)$ agrees with $\ell$ on $T_zO$, which is the membership of the pair in $N_\ell$ (with $r(z) = z$). Equal formal sums of pairs give equal masses (§XV.1), so the coefficients of $\ell$ agree for the two structures; this proves `theorem_15_1_1_model`. The form with an arbitrary map $\psi$ of operators is the same argument with the set $\{(v, D) : v = \hat f(z),\ \psi(L) = q'\ \text{for every operator } L \text{ with } L = D\}$: the derivative is recorded as a function, and $\psi$ is applied to the operator it determines.

*Two readings of the text's proof* (Findings, Chapter 15, items 1 and 2). (a) The text takes the points $x_k$ of Proposition 15.1.3 in the metric of $E_0$ and lets $T(f - s_i)(x_k)$ converge "in the weak topology". The derivative of an sc¹ map is continuous only on level $1$, and there only pointwise as an operator $E_0 \to F_0$. So the separation is run in the level-$1$ topology, where the branches are continuous and the separation lemmas hold equally (they are topological), and the "weak topology" is read as pointwise convergence of operators, which is the topology in which the derivatives are continuous. (b) The last display of the text sums $\sigma_i \cdot T(f - s_i)(0)$ over all $i$ and then uses $f(0) = s_i(0)$; this holds only for some $i$, and the sum is presumably over $\{i : f(0) = s_i(0)\}$. The formal proof compares the formal sums of the pairs $(s_i, (f - s_i)')$ over all $i$, where no such equation is needed, and selects the first entry $f(0)$ only at the end, by the mass of $N_\ell$.

**(e) In a strong bundle chart.** Let $c$ be a strong bundle chart of $P$ with base chart $\varphi$, model $O$ and fibre model $F$, and let $s$ be a section over $O_X \supseteq \operatorname{dom}(c)$. Its principal part $\hat s$ is the fibre component of $c \circ s \circ \varphi^{-1}$: $c(s(\varphi^{-1}(z))) = (z, \hat s(z))$ for $z \in O$ ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.apply_localSection⟧. *Principal parts are sc-smooth.* A local sc⁺-section on $O_X$ has $\hat s \circ r$ sc-smooth on $U$ in every chart of the atlas with domain in $O_X$ ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.IsScPlusSectionOn.isScSmooth_principal_comp_r⟧. The sc⁺-section is an sc-smooth map into $W_{0,1}$; composed with the sc-smooth inclusion $W_{0,1} \to W$ it is sc-smooth into $W$ on $O_X$; read in the chart, $z \mapsto (z, \hat s(z))$ is an sc-smooth retract map $O \to K$; composed with the base retraction $r : U \to O$ and projected to the second factor, $\hat s \circ r$ is sc-smooth. The same holds for an sc-smooth global section ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.isScSmooth_principal_comp_r⟧. *Counts in the chart* ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.IsLocalRepOn.count_eq⟧: for a local section structure of $\Lambda$ on $O_X \supseteq \operatorname{dom}(c)$ and $y \in O$, the weighted count $\sum_{i : \hat s_i(y) = v}\sigma_i$ equals $\Lambda(\Phi^{-1}(y, v))$ if $(y, v) \in K$, and $0$ otherwise. If $(y, v) \in K$, let $w = \Phi^{-1}(y, v)$, which lies over $\varphi^{-1}(y) \in O_X$; the defining formula of the structure gives $\Lambda(w) = \sum_{i : w = s_i(P w)}\sigma_i$, and $w = s_i(\varphi^{-1}(y))$ iff $\hat s_i(y) = v$, by injectivity of the chart and the formula for $\hat s_i$. If $(y, v) \notin K$, no branch can have $\hat s_i(y) = v$, since $(y, \hat s_i(y))$ is the chart image of $s_i(\varphi^{-1}(y))$ and lies in $K$. *The statement in a chart* ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.linSumChart_eq⟧: for two structures of $\Lambda$ on $O$ and $O'$ and a chart with domain in $O \cap O'$, both counts at every $y$ of the model equal $\Lambda(\Phi^{-1}(y, v))$ or $0$, so the model theorem applies with $N$ the whole space; Theorem 15.1.1 in a chart is this for sc-Fredholm section functors, which are sc-smooth ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.theorem_15_1_1⟧. Every object lies in such a chart ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.exists_chart_isLocalRepOn⟧: an sc⁺-multisection has a local section structure on the domain $U(x)$ of a natural representation around $x$, and any atlas chart around $x$ restricted to its domain intersected with $U(x)$ is a chart with domain in $U(x)$.

**(f) The change of chart, and the statement with intrinsic operators.** *Independence of the structure in $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$* ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linSumIntr_eq_of_isLocalRepOn⟧: in one chart with domain in $O \cap O'$, the intrinsic formal sums of two structures agree. This is the model theorem with the map $\psi$ that sends a chart operator to its intrinsic operator (§XV.1), at the level-$1$ point over $\varphi(x)$, with the counts of (e).

*The text's "without loss of generality $X = O$"* is, formally, the independence of the intrinsic operator $(f - s)'(x)$ from the chart. The proof makes $f - s$ a global section to which Part I's chain rule applies. *The section $f - s$ on $O_X$* ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.sectionSubOn⟧: at $y \in O_X$ the difference $f(y) - s(y)$ in the fibre, and $f(y)$ elsewhere; a section ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.isSection_sectionSubOn⟧ whose principal part in a chart with domain in $O_X$ is $\hat f - \hat s$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.principal_sectionSubOn⟧, because the chart is linear on fibres. In such a chart $z \mapsto (z, \hat f(z) - \hat s(z))$ is an sc-smooth retract map $O \to K$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.isScSmoothRetractMap_sectionSubOn⟧: for every sc-smooth retraction $R'$ onto $O$, $\hat f \circ R'$ and $\hat s \circ R'$ are sc-smooth (as in (e)), and so is the pair $(R', \hat f \circ R' - \hat s \circ R')$; this is the chart-wise form of sc-smoothness that Part I's statements take. The derivative of $(\hat f - \hat s) \circ r$ at a level-$1$ point equals that of the principal part of $f - s$ composed with $r$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.scDeriv_sub_eq_sectionSubOn⟧, since the two maps agree on $U$ (derivatives of sc¹ maps agreeing on a relatively open set agree on its level-$1$ points).

*The values lie in the fibre* ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.chartOp_mem_range⟧. At a zero $x$ of a global section $g$ whose principal part is sc-smooth in the chart, every value $D(\hat g \circ r)(z)(T\varphi\,\alpha)$ lies in $\Phi_x(W_x)$. Part I's lemma on the derivative at a zero ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationCharts · rho_scDeriv_comp_r⟧ says that the bundle projection $\rho(z)$ onto the fibre $K_z$ fixes $D(\hat g \circ r)(z)$ on $T_zO$ when $\hat g(z) = 0$; so the values lie in $K_z = \Phi_x(W_x)$. Hence the default branch of the intrinsic operator is never taken at the points of interest.

*The chain rule* ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.toIntr_chart_independent⟧. At a zero $x \in X_1$ of a section $g$ whose principal parts are sc-smooth in two charts $c$, $c'$ around $x$, the intrinsic operators through $c$ and through $c'$ are equal. Part I's chain rule with the operators named ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationChartChange · linAt_chart_change_key⟧ provides a retraction $r_W$ onto the base overlap and the identity
$$D(\hat g' \circ r')(z')\big(D(\tau \circ r_W)(z)v\big) = \Gamma(z)\big(D(\hat g \circ r)(z)v\big),$$
with $\tau$ the base transition and $\Gamma(z)$ the fibre transition operator. Two further identities translate this to intrinsic operators: the chart vector of $\alpha \in T_xX$ in $c'$ is $D(\tau \circ r_W)(z)$ applied to its chart vector in $c$ (the transition of tangent fibres), and the fibre part of $c'$ is $\Gamma(z)$ composed with that of $c$. With these, $\Phi'_x$ applied to the operator through $c'$ equals $\Phi'_x$ applied to the operator through $c$, and $\Phi'_x$ is injective. This is the argument of Part I's chart independence of $f'(x)$ ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationIntrinsic · StrongBundle.linIntr_chart_independent⟧, with chart-wise hypotheses only, so that it applies to $f - s$, which is sc-smooth only over $O_X$.

*The formal sum does not depend on the chart* ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linSumIntr_chart_independent⟧. For one structure on $O_X$ and two charts with domains in $O_X$, the formal sums agree term by term. The selection condition is the same through both charts: $\hat s_i(\varphi(y)) = \hat f(\varphi(y))$ iff $s_i(y) = f(y)$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.principal_eq_iff⟧, by injectivity of the chart on the fibre. For a selected $i$, the operator $(f - s_i)'(x)$ through either chart is the intrinsic operator of the global section $f - s_i$ at its zero $x$ (by the derivative identity above), and the chain rule gives equality.

*Proof of the intrinsic statement.* Given structures on $O$, $O'$ and charts $c$, $c'$ with domains in $O$, $O'$ around $x \in X_1$, let $c''$ be $c$ restricted to $\operatorname{dom}(c) \cap O \cap O'$, a chart around $x$ with domain in $O \cap O'$. Then: the sum of $(s, \sigma)$ through $c$ equals that through $c''$ (chart independence, structure $s$); through $c''$ it equals the sum of $(t, \tau)$ (independence of the structure); and that equals the sum of $(t, \tau)$ through $c'$ (chart independence, structure $t$). Only the sc-smoothness of $f$ enters; the theorem is stated for sc-Fredholm section functors, as in the text.

### XV.8 Definition 15.1.7 (2017: 15.1): the linearization $T(f, \Lambda)$

**Definition** (restated) ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linearization⟧. *Local data* at $x \in X_1$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.LocalLinData⟧ are an open set $O$, a local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)_{i < k}$ of $\Lambda$ on $O$, and a strong bundle chart $c$ of the atlas around $x$ with domain in $O$; their formal sum is the intrinsic sum through $c$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.LocalLinData.sum⟧. $T(f, \Lambda)(x) \in \mathrm{GrF}(x)$ is the sum of some local data, chosen, and $0$ if there are none. The text calls $T(f, \Lambda)$ the linearization of $\Lambda \circ f$ "at the points in $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$" (p. 687); the formal definition is made at every level-$1$ point, with value $0$ off the support (§XV.2).

**Well-defined** ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.LocalLinData.sum_eq⟧: any two local data at $x$ give the same sum, by the intrinsic Theorem 15.1.1 (§XV.7 (f)). **Data exist** ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.nonempty_localLinData⟧ at every level-$1$ point of an sc⁺-multisection, from the chart of §XV.7 (e). **Theorem 15.1.1 (2)** ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linearization_eq⟧: $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is the sum of *any* local data at $x$; so the value at $y$ may be computed from any structure around $x$ and any chart around $y$ with domain inside it, which is the text's (2) for all $y$ near $x$, smooth or of level $1$. **Theorem 15.1.1 (1) and Remark 15.1.2** ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linearization_eq_zero⟧: where $\Lambda(f(x)) = 0$, $T(f, \Lambda)(x) = 0$. *Proof.* Take the chosen data $(O, s, \sigma, c)$. The point $(\varphi(x), \hat f(\varphi(x)))$ lies in $K$, and $\Phi^{-1}$ of it is $f(x)$; so by the count formula (§XV.7 (e)) the weighted count of $\hat f(\varphi(x))$ is $\Lambda(f(x)) = 0$. With positive weights, no branch has $\hat s_i(\varphi(x)) = \hat f(\varphi(x))$, and every summand of the formal sum vanishes.

**In a chart.** The formalization also keeps the chart-level definition: in a chart $c$, the formal sum of any structure on an open set containing the domain of $c$, and $0$ if there is none ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.linearizationIn⟧, equal to the sum of every such structure at every level-$1$ point ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.linearizationIn_eq⟧, and $0$ where $\Lambda(f(y)) = 0$ ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.remark_15_1_2⟧. It is the form used where an argument stays inside one chart.

The definition is the value of a section; that this section is a *functor* is the next subsection.

### XV.9 Theorem 15.1.1: the functor property

The text calls $T(f, \Lambda)$ a section functor of $\mathrm{GrF}(X) \to X_\infty$, i.e. $T(f, \Lambda)(t(\varphi)) = \varphi_*T(f, \Lambda)(s(\varphi))$ for every morphism, and gives no separate argument for it. The formalization proves it at every level-$1$ point (stronger than the text's smooth points), with no added hypothesis.

**The action of a morphism on $\mathrm{GrF}$.** For $\varphi : x \to y$, $\mu(\varphi, \cdot) : W_x \to W_y$ is a linear map ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.muLin⟧ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.muLin_apply⟧, linear because the action of a strong bundle over an ep-groupoid is linear on fibres. If $x$ is of level $1$ then so is $\varphi$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.mem_levelSet_one_of_src⟧ (a morphism lies in $\mathbf X_m$ iff its source lies in $X_m$), and then Chapter 7's tangent map $T\varphi : T_xX \to T_yX$ is a bounded linear isomorphism ⟦Chapter9/TangentMorphismLinear · EpGroupoid.tangentMorCLE⟧. An operator $L : T_xX \to W_x$ is carried to
$$\varphi_*L = \mu(\varphi, \cdot) \circ L \circ (T\varphi)^{-1} : T_yX \to W_y$$
⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.conjOp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.conjOp_apply⟧, which is the text's $\varphi_*L \circ T\varphi = \mu(\varphi, L(\cdot))$, and a formal sum to the push-forward of its coefficients along $L \mapsto \varphi_*L$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.grfPush⟧.

**Statement** (stronger, for this clause) ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.theorem_15_1_1_functor⟧. For an sc-Fredholm section functor $f$, an sc⁺-multisection $\Lambda$, a morphism $\varphi$ and level-$1$ points $x = s(\varphi)$, $y = t(\varphi)$: $T(f, \Lambda)(y) = \varphi_*T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ in $\mathrm{GrF}(y)$.

**Proof.** Since $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ may be computed from *any* local data at $y$ (§XV.8), it suffices to carry local data at $x$ to local data at $y$ and to compare the two sums. The carrying uses the local strong bundle isomorphism attached to a morphism by its sheet, built in Chapter 12 (Part XII) ⟦Chapter12/MorphismIso · EpStrongBundle.morIso⟧: over the image $t(\text{sheet})$ of the sheet of $\varphi$ it consists of the base sc-diffeomorphism $\phi = s \circ (t|_{\text{sheet}})^{-1}$ onto $s(\text{sheet})$, with $\phi(y) = x$, and the fibre maps $\Phi$, $\Psi$ given by the action $\mu$ of the morphisms of the sheet.

*Step 1, data at $x$ inside the sheet.* Take local data $(O, s, \sigma, c)$ at $x$, restrict the chart to $\operatorname{dom}(c) \cap s(\text{sheet})$ and the structure to $O \cap s(\text{sheet})$ (a local structure restricts to open subsets ⟦Chapter11/MultisectionLocalStructure · SBLocalization.IsLocalRepOn.mono⟧). Call the result $(O, s, \sigma, c)$ again.

*Step 2, carrying the sections* ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.transSec⟧. A section $s$ near $x$ becomes $z \mapsto \Psi(s(\phi(z)))$ on $t(\text{sheet}) \cap \phi^{-1}(O)$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.transSet⟧, an open set ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.isOpen_transSet⟧ (the preimage of an open set under the continuous $\phi$), and the carried section is a section there ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.P_transSec⟧ because $\Psi$ covers $\phi^{-1}$. An sc⁺-section stays sc⁺ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.isScPlusSectionOn_transSec⟧: its level-$1$ lift is the composite of the lift of $s$ with $\phi$ and with the level-$1$ part of $\Psi$, all sc-smooth. A local section structure of a *functor* $\Lambda$ is carried to one of $\Lambda$, with the same weights ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.isLocalRepOn_transSec⟧: for $w$ over the new domain, $\Lambda(w) = \Lambda(\Phi(w))$ because $\Phi$ acts by $\mu$ of a morphism and $\Lambda$ is a functor ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.isWFunctor_morIso_Φ⟧; the formula of the structure at $\Phi(w)$, which lies over $\phi(P(w)) \in O$, counts the $i$ with $\Phi(w) = s_i(\phi(P w))$; and this holds iff $w = \Psi(s_i(\phi(P w)))$, since $\Phi$ and $\Psi$ are inverse on the relevant fibres.

*Step 3, carrying the chart.* The chart $c$ at $x$ is carried to the transported chart $c \circ \Phi$ at $y$ ⟦Chapter5/FredholmPullbackIso · StrongBundle.LocalIso.transportChart⟧, with base chart $\varphi \circ \phi$ and the same model; it is a chart of the atlas ⟦Chapter5/FredholmPullbackIso · StrongBundle.LocalIso.isChart_transportChart⟧. In it the principal parts of the carried sections are those of the given sections in $c$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.principal_transportChart_transSec⟧ (unwind $\Phi \circ \Psi = \mathrm{id}$ and $\phi \circ \phi^{-1} = \mathrm{id}$), and the principal part of $f$ is that of $f$ in $c$ ⟦Chapter5/FredholmPullbackIso · StrongBundle.LocalIso.principal_transportChart⟧, because a functorial section satisfies $f = \Psi \circ f \circ \phi$ on $t(\text{sheet})$ ⟦Chapter12/MorphismIso · EpStrongBundle.eqOn_morIso⟧, which is $f(t(\psi)) = \mu(\psi, f(s(\psi)))$ for the morphisms $\psi$ of the sheet.

*Step 4, the chain rule in the carried chart.* Two identities at the point. $T\varphi$ is the identity on chart vectors ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.chartVec_transport⟧: the chart vector of $T\varphi(\beta)$ in $c \circ \Phi$ equals the chart vector of $\beta$ in $c$. Indeed $T\varphi = T\hat\varphi$ at $x$ (Chapter 7's tangent map of a morphism is that of the local diffeomorphism $\hat\varphi = t \circ (s|_{\text{sheet}})^{-1}$), $T(\phi) \circ T(\hat\varphi) = \mathrm{id}$ near $x$ since $\phi \circ \hat\varphi = \mathrm{id}$, and the chart vector of $Tg(\alpha)$ in $c$ is that of $\alpha$ in $c \circ g$. And $\mu(\varphi, \cdot)$ followed by the fibre part of the carried chart at $y$ is the fibre part of $c$ at $x$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.fiberLin_transport_muLin⟧, because on $W_x$ the fibre map $\Psi$ is $\mu(\varphi, \cdot)$ and $\Phi$ undoes it; also the fibre part of the carried chart at $y$ takes values in that of $c$ at $x$ ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.fiberLin_transport_mem_range⟧. Together ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.toIntr_transportChart⟧: for every chart operator $L$, the intrinsic operator through the carried chart is $\mu(\varphi, \cdot) \circ (\text{intrinsic operator through } c) \circ (T\varphi)^{-1}$. If the values of $L \circ T\varphi$ lie in the fibre for $c$, those through the carried chart lie in the fibre (the preimages are the $\mu(\varphi, \cdot)$-images), and the two sides agree after applying the injective fibre part; if not, neither do the carried ones, and both sides are $0$.

*Step 5, the sums correspond* ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationFunctor · SBLocalization.linSumIntr_transportChart⟧. The level-$1$ point of the carried chart over $y$ is the level-$1$ point of $c$ over $x$, since $\phi(y) = x$. Term by term: the selection condition is the same (the principal parts agree), and the derivative $D((\hat f - \hat s_i) \circ r)(z)$ is the same in both charts, the composites agreeing on $U$. So each term of the sum at $y$ is $\varphi_*$ of the corresponding term at $x$ by Step 4, and push-forward of formal sums is additive. With the carried data at $y$ and the given data at $x$, §XV.8 identifies both sides with $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ and $\varphi_*T(f, \Lambda)(x)$.

*Not formalized.* That $\varphi \mapsto \varphi_*$ is itself functorial, $(\psi\varphi)_* = \psi_*\varphi_*$ and $1_* = \mathrm{id}$, which the text uses to speak of $\mathrm{GrF}(X)$ as a category, is not formalized and not needed for the theorem; it would follow from Chapter 7's functoriality of $T$ and the composition law of $\mu$, in about 60–100 lines.

### XV.10 Local sc⁺-sections, the sections $f - s_i$ on $W|_O$, and local solution sets (prerequisite for §15.2)

The proofs of Theorems 15.2.2 and 15.2.5 and of the sc-Fredholm property of the summands (§XV.11) apply Part I's implicit function theorem to $f - s_i$ near $x$, where $s_i$ is defined only on an open $O$. The formalization makes $f - s_i$ a section problem of Part I on the restricted strong bundle $W|_O$ (extra).

**Local sc⁺-sections in compatible charts.** Chapter 11 defines a local sc⁺-section on $O$ as a section over $O$ that is sc-smooth into $W_{0,1}$. Two chart facts are needed for charts that are only compatible with the atlas, such as restricted charts. In every such chart with domain in $O$, the principal part takes values in $F_1$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.IsScPlusSectionOn.principal_mem_range_of_isChart⟧: compare with an atlas chart around $s(x)$, in which this holds by Chapter 13's local form ⟦Chapter13/ScPlusSectionOn · SBLocalization.IsScPlusSectionOn.isScSmoothRetractMap_localRepOne⟧, through the transition, which preserves level $1$ in the fibre. And the level-$1$ representative $z \mapsto (z, \hat s_1(z))$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.localRepOne_eq_of_sub⟧ is an sc-smooth retract map $O \to K[1]$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.IsScPlusSectionOn.isScSmoothRetractMap_localRepOne_of_isChart⟧. The proof is local: near each point, the representative is the composite of the base transition into an atlas chart $c'$ around $s(x)$, the representative in $c'$ (sc-smooth by Chapter 13), and the level-$1$ part of the bundle transition back to $c$; the composite agrees with the representative on the overlap.

**Restriction to $O$** ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.IsScPlusSectionOn.restrictOpen⟧. A local sc⁺-section on $O$ is a global sc⁺-section of the restricted strong bundle $W|_O$, Part I's pull-back along the inclusion $O \to X$ ⟦Chapter5/RestrictOpen · StrongBundle.restrictOpen⟧: the charts of $W|_O$ are restrictions of charts with domain in $O$, and in them the two chart facts above are the conditions of an sc⁺-section. (The restricted bundle needs the paracompactness of $O$ and of the pulled-back total space as type-class data; they hold for open subsets of M-polyfolds and are supplied in the proofs.)

**The section $f - s$** ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.subSec⟧: $f + (-1)\cdot\bar s$, where $\bar s$ is $s$ extended by $0$ off $O$; a global section ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.isSection_subSec⟧ with principal part $\hat f - \hat s$ in every chart with domain in $O$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.principal_subSec⟧ and with zeros in $O$ exactly where $f = s$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.isZeroAt_subSec_iff⟧. Its restriction to $W|_O$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.subRestrict⟧ is a tame sc-Fredholm section when $f$ has Part I's tame germ data ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.isScFredholmSectionTame_subRestrict⟧. *Proof.* Restriction to an open set keeps tame germ data (Part I), so $f|_O$ is tame sc-Fredholm on $W|_O$; $-\bar s$ is an sc⁺-section on $O$ (Chapter 13: sc⁺-sections on $O$ are closed under constant multiples), hence a global sc⁺-section of $W|_O$ by the restriction above; and an sc⁺-perturbation of a tame sc-Fredholm section is one, by Part I's Theorem 3.1.18 in its tame form ⟦Chapter3/TameGermsAddG · theorem_3_1_18_tame⟧. Finally, the restriction of a sum of sections is the sum of the restrictions.

**The linearization in a chart.** At a level-$1$ point $z$ of a chart with domain in $O$, the chart operator of $(f - s)'$ is $D((\hat f - \hat s) \circ r)(z) : E_0 \to F_0$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.subDeriv⟧, the derivative of an sc¹ map ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.isSc1_sub_comp_r⟧, and "onto" means that it maps $T_zO$ onto the fibre $K_z = \{k : (z, k) \in K\}$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.ChartOnto⟧, Part I's "$f'(x)$ onto" read in the chart. *The intrinsic and the chart readings agree* ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.surjective_toIntr_subDeriv_iff⟧: at $x \in X_1$ with $f(x) = s(x)$, the intrinsic operator $(f - s)'(x) : T_xX \to W_x$ of §XV.1 is onto iff the chart operator maps $T_zO$ onto $K_z$, $z$ over $\varphi(x)$. The chart operator equals that of the global section $f - s$, whose values at its zero lie in the fibre (§XV.7 (f)); the fibre part of the chart maps $W_x$ bijectively onto $K_{\varphi(x)}$, and $T\varphi$ maps $T_xX$ onto $T_zO$, so surjectivity transfers in both directions. *And they are Part I's surjectivity on $W|_O$.* In the restricted chart of $W|_O$ the principal part of $(f - s)|_O$ composed with $r$ has the same derivative as $(\hat f - \hat s) \circ r$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.scDeriv_restrictTo_subRestrict⟧; so chart surjectivity gives Part I's surjective linearization of $(f - s)|_O$ at $x$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.isLinSurjAt_subRestrict⟧, and conversely, at a zero, Part I's surjectivity gives chart surjectivity in every chart with domain in $O$ and at every level-$1$ point over $\varphi(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.chartOnto_of_isLinSurjAt_subRestrict⟧, by the chart independence of Part I's notion.

**Local solution sets.** *At a point of good position* ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.exists_solutionSet_of_isGoodPosition⟧. If $(f - s)|_O$ is in good position at $x$ in the sense of Part I's Definition 3.6.6 in a tame chart, there is an open $V \ni x$ inside the domain of $c$ such that $\{y \in V : f(y) = s(y)\}$ is a smooth finite-dimensional submanifold of $X$ (Definition 4.2.1), and at each of its points $y$: $y$ is smooth, $(f - s)|_O$ is in good position at $y$, and the chart operator is onto at every level-$1$ point over $\varphi(y)$. *Proof.* Part I's Theorem 3.6.8 ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyOntoG · theorem_3_6_8'⟧ for the tame sc-Fredholm section $(f - s)|_O$ at its zero $x$ gives an open $V_x \subseteq O$ on which every zero is in good position; on $W = V_x \cap \operatorname{dom}(c)$ the solution set is a smooth submanifold of $O$ by Part I's lemma on solution sets in good position ⟦Chapter5/GeneralPosition · StrongBundle.isSmoothSubmanifold_solutionSet_inter⟧, and its image under the sc-diffeomorphism $O \to X$ (the inclusion) is a smooth submanifold of $X$. Zeros of an sc-Fredholm section are smooth points; good position implies surjective linearization; and the converse direction above gives chart surjectivity. *At an interior point* ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.exists_solutionSet_interior⟧: if $d_X(x) = 0$, $f(x) = s(x)$ and the chart operator is onto at $x$, the same conclusion holds without "good position" in it. The restriction $(f - s)|_O$ has degeneracy index $0$ at $x$ (the index of an open subset is that of $X$), it has surjective linearization at $x$ by the transfer above, and at a point of index $0$ Part I's surjectivity gives good position ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTIndexG · StrongBundle.isGoodPositionGerm_of_isLinSurjAt⟧; then the first case applies.

All of this rests on Part I's Theorem 3.6.8 for tame germ data; with the 2021 untamed notion that theorem does not hold as we read it (Part III), which is why $f$ carries the tame germ data here and in §15.2 (§XV.11).

### XV.11 Theorem 15.1.1 (2): the summands are sc-Fredholm
