The text puts sc-Fredholm operators into $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ and asserts that $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is an element of it: each $(f - s_i)'(x)$ with $f(x) = s_i(x)$ is sc-Fredholm, because $f - s_i$ is an sc⁺-perturbation of an sc-Fredholm section. The formalization proves this for the operators that occur, in a chart (narrower).

**The chart operator is sc-Fredholm** ⟦Chapter15/FredholmSummands · SBLocalization.subDeriv_isScFredholm⟧. Let $f$ have Part I's tame germ data, $s$ be sc⁺ on an open $O$ containing the domain of the chart $c$, $f(x) = s(x)$, and $z$ a level-$1$ point over $\varphi(x)$ in $U$. Then $D((\hat f - \hat s) \circ r)(z)$ is an sc-operator, $T_zO$ and $K_z$ are sc-subspaces, the operator maps $T_zO$ into $K_z$, and its restriction $T_zO \to K_z$ is sc-Fredholm, Fredholm on every level with the index of level $0$. *Proof.* By §XV.10, $(f - s)|_O$ is a tame sc-Fredholm section of $W|_O$ with a zero at $x$, and the restricted chart of $c$ is a chart of $W|_O$. Part I's statement that the linearization of an sc-Fredholm section at a zero is sc-Fredholm in every chart ⟦Chapter3/LinearizationChartsScG · StrongBundle.linAt_isScFredholm⟧ gives all the conclusions for the derivative of the restricted principal part composed with $r$, which is $D((\hat f - \hat s) \circ r)(z)$ by the derivative identity of §XV.10.

**Theorem 15.1.1 (2), the summands** (restated, with the inherited hypothesis) ⟦Chapter15/FredholmSummands · SBLocalization.linearization_summand_isScFredholm⟧. For $x \in X_1$ and any local data $(O, s, \sigma, c)$ (§XV.8), every operator $L$ in the support of $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is $(f - s_i)'(x)$, the intrinsic operator of $D((\hat f - \hat s_i) \circ r)(z)$, for some $i$ with $f(x) = s_i(x)$, and that chart operator is sc-Fredholm as above. *Proof.* $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is the sum of the given data (§XV.8); an operator in the support of that sum is the intrinsic operator of a branch through $f(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_support_linSumIntr_iff⟧ (positive weights, as in Remark 15.1.2); and the chart statement applies to that branch.

**Reading.** $T_xX$ and $W_x$ carry no sc-structure in the project: they are a tangent fibre and a fibre with their linear structures. "$(f - s_i)'(x) : T_xX \to W_x$ is sc-Fredholm" is therefore read in a chart, through the identifications $T_xX \cong T_zO$ and $W_x \cong K_z$ by which the intrinsic operator is defined. The sc-structure on $T_zO$ and $K_z$ is that of sc-subspaces of $E$ and $F$; that it does not depend on the chart is not formalized, and not needed, since the statement holds in every chart. $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ is not cut down to sc-Fredholm operators (§XV.1); the lemma says what lies in the support.

**The hypothesis taken.** *Tame germ data for $f$* (Part I's notion, an sc-Fredholm section whose germs are conjugate to basic germs in tame charts) is not stated in §15.1, where $f$ is "an sc-Fredholm section". It is not new: it is inherited from Part I's Theorems 3.1.18 and 3.6.8, which are proved for tame germ data and on which the proof rests; with the 2021 untamed notion, Theorem 3.6.8 does not hold as we read it ⟦Chapter4/UntamedBundleTheorem · UntamedCorner.theorem_3_6_8_untamed_not⟧ (Part III). The same hypothesis is carried by all of §15.2 (§§XV.12–XV.16) and by the cylinder of Corollary 15.3.10 (§XV.30). Its witness is the graph section of $0$ over $\mathbb R^n \times \mathbb R^0 \to \mathbb R^n$, which has tame germ data by Part I ⟦Chapter4/NonVacuityG · isScFredholmSectionTame_graphSection⟧, with the zero multisection; there all hypotheses of the §15.2 theorems hold together ⟦Chapter15/InteriorPerturbationWitness · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_2_hyp_witness⟧. The witness has zero fibres, so it shows that the hypotheses are consistent, not that the summands are non-trivially sc-Fredholm; the sc-Fredholm statement itself is a theorem for every $f$ with tame germ data.

### XV.12 Definition 15.2.1 (2017: Definition 15.2): transversality

§15.2 (pp. 688–691) is the local theory: what $T(f, \Lambda)$ says about $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$ near one point. Its tool throughout is the passage of §XV.10 from a branch $s_i$ of a local sc⁺-section structure, a section defined only on an open $O$, to the sc-Fredholm section $(f - s_i)|_O$ of the restricted strong bundle $W|_O$, to which Part I's implicit function theorems apply. The definitions come first.

**Statement** (restated). Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section functor and $\Lambda$ an sc⁺-multisection. At a level-$1$ point $x$, *$T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is onto* if every operator in the support of the formal sum $T(f, \Lambda)(x) \in \mathrm{GrF}(x)$ (Definition 15.1.7, §XV.8) is surjective onto $W_x$ ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.IsOntoAtLevel⟧. For an arbitrary object $x$ the same is said with the clause $x \in X_1$ built in, since $T(f, \Lambda)$ is defined only on $X_1$ ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.IsOntoAt⟧. $(f, \Lambda)$ is *transversal at $x$* if $\Lambda(f(x)) > 0$ and $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is onto ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.IsTransversalAt⟧ (the text's (2)), and *transversal* if it is transversal at every point of $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$ ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.IsTransversal⟧ (the text's (1)).

The text phrases the condition twice: as "a nonzero combination of surjective sc-Fredholm operators", and, after "i.e.", as the surjectivity of all $(f - s_i)'(x)$ with $f(x) = s_i(x)$ for a local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i)$. The formal definition is the first phrasing minus the two words that turn out to be automatic; the two lemmas below recover the second phrasing and the word "nonzero", and §XV.11 the word "sc-Fredholm".

**The operators of the formal sum.** Fix local data at $x$ (§XV.8): a local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)_{i \in I}$ of $\Lambda$ on an open $O$, with positive weights, and a strong bundle chart $c$ around $x$ with domain in $O$. Then an operator $L : T_xX \to W_x$ lies in the support of $\sum_{i : f(x) = s_i(x)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(x)$ iff $L = (f - s_i)'(x)$ for some $i$ with $s_i(x) = f(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_support_linSumIntr_iff⟧. *Proof.* The formal sum through $c$ is, by definition, the push-forward of the chart-level sum, whose filter is "the principal parts of $s_i$ and $f$ agree at $\varphi(x)$" (§XV.7). For sections over the chart domain this is the same as $s_i(x) = f(x)$, the chart being injective on fibres; this rewrites the filter. The coefficient of $L$ is then $\sum_i [s_i(x) = f(x) \wedge (f - s_i)'(x) = L]\,\sigma_i$, a finite sum of non-negative rationals; it is nonzero iff some term is nonzero, and a term is nonzero iff its condition holds, because $\sigma_i > 0$. So positivity of the weights is exactly what makes "occurs in the sum" the same as "is the derivative of some branch through $f(x)$".

**The text's reading** ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.isOntoAtLevel_iff⟧: for $f$ an sc-Fredholm section functor and any local data at $x \in X_1$, $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is onto iff every $(f - s_i)'(x)$ with $s_i(x) = f(x)$, computed through the chart of the data, is surjective. *Proof.* By Definition 15.1.7, $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is the formal sum of these data, whichever data are taken ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linearization_eq⟧ (this is Theorem 15.1.1, §XV.7); the support is described by the previous lemma; the two directions are then immediate. The text's "if $(s_i)$ is a local sc⁺-section structure" is thereby read "for any", which Theorem 15.1.1 makes the same as "for some".

**"Nonzero" is automatic** ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.linearization_ne_zero⟧: at $x \in X_1$ with $\Lambda(f(x)) > 0$, $T(f, \Lambda)(x) \neq 0$. *Proof.* Local data exist at every level-$1$ point ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.nonempty_localLinData⟧. The count formula of the local structure, evaluated at the point $f(x)$ of the fibre over $x$, gives $\Lambda(f(x)) = \sum_{i : s_i(x) = f(x)}\sigma_i$; if no branch passed through $f(x)$ the sum would be empty and $\Lambda(f(x)) = 0$. So some $i$ has $s_i(x) = f(x)$, the operator $(f - s_i)'(x)$ lies in the support of the formal sum, and a formal sum with nonempty support is not $0$.

**"sc-Fredholm" is automatic** as well, for $f$ with Part I's tame germ data: every operator of the support is, read in the chart, sc-Fredholm (§XV.11). So Definition 15.2.1 asks only for surjectivity, and that is what the formal definition says.

**Where the support lives.** $T(f, \Lambda)$ is defined on $X_1$, so the formal definition carries "$x \in X_1$". Under the tame germ data this costs nothing: every point of the support is smooth. *One branch* ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_smoothSet_of_eq⟧: if $s$ is sc⁺ on an open $O \ni y$ and $f(y) = s(y)$, then $y$ is a zero of the section $(f - s)|_O$ of $W|_O$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.isZeroAt_subSec_iff⟧, which is a tame sc-Fredholm section (§XV.10) ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.isScFredholmSectionTame_subRestrict⟧; zeros of sc-Fredholm sections are smooth points (Part I) ⟦Chapter3/TameGermsG · StrongBundle.IsScFredholmSection.mem_smoothSet_of_isZeroAt⟧, and a point of the open M-polyfold $O$ is smooth there iff it is smooth in $X$. *The support* ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_smoothSet_of_pos⟧: if $\Lambda(f(y)) > 0$, the count formula of a local structure around $y$ produces a branch with $f(y) = s_i(y)$, as in the previous proof, and the one-branch lemma applies. The same fact without tame germ data, $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f) \subset X_1$, is proved for Definition 15.3.5 in §XV.20 ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.mem_levelSet_one_of_pos⟧.

**A witness.** Over the trivial ep-groupoid of $\mathbb R^n$, the strong bundle $\mathbb R^n \times \mathbb R^0 \to \mathbb R^n$ with the graph section of $0$ and the trivial multisection $\Lambda_0$ (Chapter 13; $\Lambda_0(w) = 1$ on the zero vectors, $0$ elsewhere) is transversal, with $\Lambda_0(f(x)) = 1$ at every point ⟦Chapter15/InteriorPerturbationWitness · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_2_hyp_witness⟧. The fibres are $\{0\}$, so every operator into a fibre is onto. The example has zero fibres: it shows that the definition is satisfiable with nonempty support, not more.

### XV.13 Theorem 15.2.2 (2017: Theorem 15.2): interior local perturbation

**Statement** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_2_hyp⟧. Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section functor *with Part I's tame germ data*, $\Lambda$ an sc⁺-multisection, $d_X(x) = 0$, and $(f, \Lambda)$ transversal at $x$. Then there are a natural representation $U(x)$ of $G_x$ (Theorem 7.1.13), a local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)_{i \in I}$ of $\Lambda$ on an open $O$, and a strong bundle chart $c$ with $U(x) \subset \operatorname{dom}(c) \subset O$, such that, with $M_i = \{y \in U(x) : f(y) = s_i(y)\}$:

1. $d_X(y) = 0$ for all $y \in U(x)$;
2. $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ is onto at every $y \in U(x)$ with $\Lambda(f(y)) > 0$;
3. (a) every $M_i$ is a smooth finite-dimensional submanifold of $X$ (Definition 4.2.1); (b) the inclusion $M_i \hookrightarrow U(x)$ is a proper map; (c) $\{y \in U(x) : \Lambda(f(y)) > 0\} = \bigcup_iM_i$; (d) $\Lambda(f(y)) = \sum_{i : f(y) = s_i(y)}\sigma_i$ on $U(x)$; (e) $T(f, \Lambda)(y) = \sum_{i : f(y) = s_i(y)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(y)$, the operators read in $c$, at every level-$1$ point $y$ of $\operatorname{dom}(c)$;

and, in addition, a branch meets $f$ in $U(x)$ only if it passes through $f(x)$: $f(y) = s_i(y)$ for some $y \in U(x)$ implies $s_i(x) = f(x)$.

*Narrower* only by the tame germ data. The text's hypothesis $x \in \operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$ is part of transversality at $x$.

**Proof.** The text's proof is four sentences: choose $U'(x)$ and a local structure on it; by assumption $f(x) = s_i(x)$ for all $i$; apply the implicit function theorem to each $f - s_i$; intersect. The Lean proof follows it, with two additions: the branches that do not pass through $f(x)$ are handled separately, and every step is read through one chart inside the domain of the structure, so that §§XV.8 and XV.10 apply.

*Step 1, a structure and a chart inside it.* Since $\Lambda$ is an sc⁺-multisection, there is a natural representation $U_0(x)$ with a local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)_{i \in I}$ on $U_0(x)$ (Definition 13.2.5 in its 2021 reading, Part XIII). Choose any strong bundle chart $c_0$ around $x$ in the atlas and restrict it to $\operatorname{dom}(c_0) \cap U_0(x)$, which is open; the restriction is a chart of the atlas, and its domain lies in the domain of the structure. Call it $c$. The structure and $c$ are local data at $x$ in the sense of Definition 15.1.7 ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.LocalLinData⟧.

*Step 2, from transversality to the chart.* Transversality at $x$ gives $x \in X_1$ and $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ onto; by the text's reading (§XV.12) ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.isOntoAtLevel_iff⟧, every intrinsic $(f - s_i)'(x)$ with $s_i(x) = f(x)$ is surjective. The intrinsic operator is onto $W_x$ iff the chart operator $D((\hat f - \hat s_i) \circ r)(z)$ maps $T_zO$ onto the fibre $K_z$ at the level-$1$ chart point $z$ over $\varphi(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.surjective_toIntr_subDeriv_iff⟧ (§XV.10).

*Step 3, one branch* ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.exists_branch_interior⟧. For each $i$ there is an open $V_i \ni x$ inside $\operatorname{dom}(c)$ such that $\{y \in V_i : f(y) = s_i(y)\}$ is a smooth submanifold, every point $y$ of it is smooth and has the chart operator of $f - s_i$ onto at every chart point over $\varphi(y)$, and, if some $y \in V_i$ has $f(y) = s_i(y)$, then $s_i(x) = f(x)$. Two cases.

- *The branch passes through $f(x)$.* Then the chart operator is onto at $x$ (Step 2), and the interior local solution lemma of §XV.10 ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.exists_solutionSet_interior⟧ gives $V_i$: it is Part I's implicit function theorem (Theorem 3.6.8) for the tame sc-Fredholm section $(f - s_i)|_O$ of $W|_O$ at its zero $x$, where $d_X(x) = 0$ makes the good position automatic, read back in $X$ through the inclusion $O \to X$. The last clause holds trivially.
- *The branch does not pass through $f(x)$.* Then $V_i = \{y \in \operatorname{dom}(c) : f(y) \ne s_i(y)\}$. It is open ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.isOpen_sep_ne⟧: $f$ is continuous, $s_i$ is continuous on $O$ ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.IsScPlusSectionOn.continuousOn⟧ (an sc⁺-section is the composite of a continuous lift to $W_{0,1}$ with the continuous inclusion $W_{0,1} \to W$), and the set where two continuous maps into a Hausdorff space differ is the preimage of the complement of the closed diagonal. It contains $x$. The solution set in $V_i$ is empty, so it is a submanifold, and the remaining clauses hold vacuously.

The second case is where the formal statement departs from the text's proof, which says "by assumption, $f(x) = s_i(x)$ for $i \in I$". That holds only for the branches through $f(x)$; presumably the text means those, the others having empty solution sets near $x$. The formal statement keeps the whole index set, and the last clause of the theorem says that the other branches contribute nothing on $U(x)$.

*Step 4, the neighbourhood.* The set $\operatorname{dom}(c) \cap \bigcap_iV_i \cap \{d_X = 0\}$ is open, the last set being open because the degeneracy index is upper semicontinuous, and it contains $x$. A natural representation can be shrunk into any open neighbourhood of $x$ ⟦Chapter7/NaturalRep · EpGroupoid.exists_naturalRep_shrink⟧ (Theorem 7.1.13); this gives $U(x) \subset U_0(x)$. Property (1) holds by construction.

*Step 5, the count.* For $y \in U(x)$ the count formula of the structure at the point $f(y)$ gives (d), $\Lambda(f(y)) = \sum_{i : f(y) = s_i(y)}\sigma_i$. Since the weights are positive, $\Lambda(f(y)) > 0$ iff some branch passes through $f(y)$; this gives (c).

*Step 6, (2).* Let $y \in U(x)$ with $\Lambda(f(y)) > 0$. By Step 5 some $i$ has $f(y) = s_i(y)$; then $y$ lies in the solution set of Step 3 for that branch, hence is smooth and in particular at level $1$. The same structure with the chart $c$ is local data at $y$, so by the text's reading it suffices that each $(f - s_j)'(y)$ with $s_j(y) = f(y)$ is onto; Step 3 for the branch $j$ gives the chart operator onto at $y$, and §XV.10 turns this into the intrinsic surjectivity ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.surjective_toIntr_subDeriv_iff⟧.

*Step 7, (a) and (b).* $M_i = \{y \in V_i : f(y) = s_i(y)\} \cap U(x)$, the intersection of a smooth submanifold with an open set, which is again one. For (b), an inclusion of a subset is a proper map iff the subset is closed; $M_i$ is the set of points of $U(x)$ where the two continuous maps $f$ and $s_i$ agree, which is closed in $U(x)$.

*Step 8, (e).* At a level-$1$ point $y$ of $\operatorname{dom}(c)$, the structure and $c$ are local data at $y$, and Definition 15.1.7 gives $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ as their formal sum ⟦Chapter15/LinearizationIntrinsic · SBLocalization.linearization_eq⟧. The last clause is the last clause of Step 3.

**Readings.** (i) "$d_{M_i} \equiv 0$ and therefore a natural (classical) smooth manifold structure" in (a) is read as Definition 4.2.1 inside $\{d_X = 0\}$, which (1) provides; Definition 4.2.1 allows boundary and corners and here has none. (ii) The text's (e) "for $y \in U(x)$" uses the structure on $U(x)$; the formal (e) is read in the chart $c$, which is no restriction, since the formal sum does not depend on the chart (§XV.7). (iii) The text's "$f_i(y) = s_i(y)$" in (e) is read $f(y) = s_i(y)$. (iv) The index set is that of the structure; $M_i = \emptyset$ for the branches not through $f(x)$.

**The added hypothesis.** *Tame germ data for $f$* (Part I's sc-Fredholm sections with germ data in tame charts), not stated in §15.2, which says only "$f$ an sc-Fredholm section functor". It is inherited, not new: the proof is Part I's Theorem 3.6.8 applied to $(f - s_i)|_O$, and Part I proves that theorem for tame germ data; with the untamed 2021 notion of an sc-Fredholm germ Theorem 3.6.8 does not hold as we read it ⟦Chapter4/UntamedBundleTheorem · UntamedCorner.theorem_3_6_8_untamed_not⟧ (Part III). The restriction to $O$ keeps tame germ data, and the sc⁺-perturbation by $-s_i$ keeps them by Part I's Theorem 3.1.18 (§XV.10). No ep-level counterexample to the untamed §15.2 was built. *Witness* of all hypotheses together ⟦Chapter15/InteriorPerturbationWitness · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_2_hyp_witness⟧: the example of §XV.12, where the graph section of $0$ has tame germ data by Part I ⟦Chapter4/NonVacuityG · isScFredholmSectionTame_graphSection⟧, $\Lambda_0$ is an sc⁺-multisection, $d_X \equiv 0$ on $\mathbb R^n$, $\Lambda_0(f(x)) = 1$ and $(f, \Lambda_0)$ is transversal everywhere. The example has zero fibres: it witnesses consistency, and the solution sets are all of $\mathbb R^n$.

### XV.14 Remark 15.2.3 (2017: Remark 15.2): $\Lambda \circ f$ is locally branched; tame; the ep-groupoid $U(x)$

The remark says that on the ep-groupoid $U(x)$ the functor $z \mapsto \Lambda(f(z))$ is a branched, tame ep⁺-subgroupoid with support $M_{U(x)}$. The formalization proves it in three forms: as a local branching structure at a point of $X$, as a global statement on $X$, and on the ep-groupoid $U(x)$ itself, each also with "tame".

**Local form** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_hyp⟧. With tame germ data for $f$: at a point $y$ with $d_X(y) = 0$ where $(f, \Lambda)$ is transversal, $z \mapsto \Lambda(f(z))$ has a local branching structure at $y$ in the sense of Definition 9.1.2 (2)–(4) (Part IX): an open $U \ni y$, finitely many smooth submanifolds $M_j \subset U$ through $y$ with proper inclusions, and positive rational weights $\sigma_j$ with $\Lambda(f(z)) = \sum_{j : z \in M_j}\sigma_j$ on $U$. *Proof.* Theorem 15.2.2 at $y$ gives $U(y)$, the structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)_{i \in I}$, the sets $M_i$ and (a)–(d). The index set of the branching structure is the set of branches through $f(y)$, $\{i \in I : s_i(y) = f(y)\}$, so that every $M_j$ contains $y$, as Definition 9.1.2 asks; the submanifold property and properness are (a) and (b); the weights are positive. The count is computed in three rewrites: by (d), $\Lambda(f(z)) = \sum_{i \in I}[f(z) = s_i(z)]\,\sigma_i$; by the last clause of Theorem 15.2.2 every branch with $f(z) = s_i(z)$ for some $z \in U(y)$ passes through $f(y)$, so the sum may be restricted to the branches through $f(y)$; and that sum is the sum over the index set of the structure, after reindexing by the subtype.

**Global form** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_isBranchedEpSubgroupoid_hyp⟧. With tame germ data: if $(f, \Lambda)$ is transversal, $d_X = 0$ on $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$, and the orbit space $|\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)|$ is paracompact, then $\Lambda \circ f$ is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid of $X$ (Definition 9.1.2). *Proof*, field by field. The values are non-negative rationals. Functoriality: for a morphism $\varphi$, $f(t(\varphi)) = \mu(\varphi, f(s(\varphi)))$ because $f$ is a section functor, and $\Lambda(\mu(\varphi, w)) = \Lambda(w)$ because $\Lambda$ is a functor on $W$; so $\Lambda(f(t(\varphi))) = \Lambda(f(s(\varphi)))$. The support consists of smooth points (§XV.12) ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_smoothSet_of_pos⟧. The paracompactness of the orbit space of the support, Definition 9.1.2 (1), is the hypothesis. The local branching structures at the support points are the local form. *Narrower* also by that hypothesis: Definition 9.1.2 (1) is new in the 2021 edition, and the remark, being local, does not address it. *Witness* ⟦Chapter15/InteriorPerturbationWitness · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_isBranchedEpSubgroupoid_hyp_witness⟧: the example of §XV.12, with the paracompactness of the orbit space of any subset of the trivial ep-groupoid of $\mathbb R^n$ from Part IX ⟦Chapter9/Propositions · EpGroupoid.paracompactSpace_orbitSupp_trivial_rn⟧.

**"Tame".** A tame local branching structure (Definition 9.3.6) is a local branching structure whose domain is a natural representation with the properness property (Definition 7.5.8) and whose submanifolds $M_j$ have tame induced structures (Definition 2.5.18). At an interior point of transversality $(f, \Lambda)$ is in good position (Definition 15.2.4; the lemma is in §XV.15), and at a point of good position Theorem 15.2.5 produces a tame local branching structure (§XV.16). Hence *the local tame form* (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_tame_hyp⟧: at $y$ with $d_X(y) = 0$ and $(f, \Lambda)$ transversal at $y$, $z \mapsto \Lambda(f(z))$ has a tame local branching structure at $y$; and *the global tame form* (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_isTameBranched_hyp⟧: under the hypotheses of the global form, $\Lambda \circ f$ is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid and tame, by the global form of Theorem 15.2.5 (§XV.16) applied with good position at every support point. *Witness* ⟦Chapter15/TameWitness · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_isTameBranched_hyp_witness⟧: the example of §XV.12, with support all of $\mathbb R^n$.

**The ep-groupoid $U(x)$.** *Reading.* "The ep-groupoid $U(x)$" is read as the translation groupoid $G_x \ltimes U(x)$ of the natural action of the isotropy group on a natural representation, whose objects are the points of $U(x)$ and whose morphisms are the pairs $(g, y)$ from $y$ to $g * y$ ⟦Chapter7/NaturalAction · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.translationEpGroupoid⟧. Part VII proves that it is an ep-groupoid and that the functor $\Gamma : G_x \ltimes U(x) \to X$ is fully faithful onto the full subcategory of $X$ on $U(x)$ (Theorem 7.1.13 (3)), with the same orbit space (Proposition 7.1.19). So the full subcategory on $U(x)$ is an ep-groupoid in this form. The restriction of a functor $\Theta$ on $X$ to it is $\Theta_U(y) = \Theta(y)$, and "with support $M_{U(x)}$" is then the definition of the support of $\Theta_U$.

**The generic step** (extra) ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.isTameBranched_translation⟧. For any ep-groupoid $X$, natural representation $U(x)$ and functor $\Theta \ge 0$ on $X$ with closed support, such that every point of $\operatorname{supp}\Theta \cap U(x)$ is smooth and carries a tame local branching structure of $\Theta$, the restriction $\Theta_U$ is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid of $G_x \ltimes U(x)$, and tame. Unlike the global form on $X$, no paracompactness is assumed: on $G_x \ltimes U(x)$ it is proved. *Proof*, field by field.

- *Functoriality.* A morphism $(g, y)$ of the translation groupoid is carried by $\Gamma$ to a morphism of $X$ with source $y$ and target $g * y$, so $\Theta(g * y) = \Theta(y)$ by functoriality of $\Theta$ on $X$.
- *Smoothness.* A point of the open sub-M-polyfold $U(x)$ is smooth there iff it is smooth in $X$.
- *Paracompactness of the orbit space of the support* ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.paracompactSpace_orbitSupp_translation⟧. Let $S = \operatorname{supp}\Theta_U$, closed in $U(x)$, hence paracompact, and let $p : S \to |S|$ be the orbit map restricted to it, continuous and onto. Two facts about the orbit map $\pi$ of $G_x \ltimes U(x)$. It is closed ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.isClosedMap_translation_π⟧: every closed subset $C$ of $U(x)$ is a proper subset of the translation groupoid in the sense of Definition 7.5.8 ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.translation_isProperSubset⟧, because Part VII proves that the restriction of the source–target map to the morphisms over the closure of any subset is proper ⟦Chapter7/NaturalAction · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.translation_isProperMap⟧, and the orbit of a proper subset is closed (Proposition 7.5.10 (1)) ⟦Chapter7/ProperSubsets · EpGroupoid.proposition_7_5_10_one⟧. Its fibres are finite ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.finite_fiber_translation_π⟧: two points of $U(x)$ have the same orbit iff they differ by the action of an element of $G_x$, so a fibre is the image of the finite group $G_x$ under $g \mapsto g * y$. Restricted to the closed set $S$, $p$ is again closed with finite fibres. Now Michael's criterion (Lemma 2.7.3) ⟦Chapter7/Michael · paracompactSpace_of_closed_refinement⟧: a regular space is paracompact if every open cover has a locally finite closed refinement that covers. Given an open cover of $|S|$, pull it back to $S$, take a locally finite closed precise refinement there ⟦Chapter7/Michael · exists_closed_precise_refinement⟧, and push it forward by $p$. The images are closed ($p$ closed), cover ($p$ onto), refine the cover, and form a locally finite family by the next lemma.
- *Closed maps with finite fibres* ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · locallyFinite_image_of_isClosedMap⟧: a closed map $p$ with finite fibres carries a locally finite family $(v_i)$ to a locally finite family $(p(v_i))$. *Proof.* Fix $y$. Each point $z$ of the finite fibre $p^{-1}(y)$ has a neighbourhood $N_z$ meeting only finitely many $v_i$. The union $W$ of the interiors of the $N_z$ is open and contains the fibre, so $y \notin p(W^c)$, and $(p(W^c))^c$ is an open neighbourhood of $y$, $p$ being closed. If $p(v_i)$ meets it, at a point $p(z)$ with $z \in v_i$, then $z \notin W^c$, so $z$ lies in some $N_{z'}$ and $v_i$ meets $N_{z'}$; there are finitely many such $i$ for each of the finitely many $z'$.
- *Tame local branching structures move into $U(x)$* ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.nonempty_tameBranching_translation⟧. Let $C$ be a tame local branching structure of $\Theta$ at $y \in U(x)$, with domain $C.U$. Theorem 7.1.13 for the translation groupoid ⟦Chapter7/NaturalRep · EpGroupoid.theorem_7_1_13⟧ gives a natural representation $U'$ of $G_x \ltimes U(x)$ at $y$ inside the preimage of $C.U$; its image in $X$ is open and inside $C.U \cap U(x)$. The structure $C$ is restricted to this image (the $M_j$ intersected with an open set stay submanifolds with tame induced structures), and moved into the M-polyfold $U(x)$ by the inclusion $U(x) \to X$, an sc-diffeomorphism onto its open image ⟦Chapter8/TangentLocal · MPolyfold.isScDiffeoOn_subtypeVal⟧; images of tame induced structures under sc-diffeomorphisms are tame. The properness property of $U'$ inside the translation groupoid holds for every open subset of $U(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · EpGroupoid.NaturalRep.translation_hasPropernessProperty⟧, since every closed subset is proper there.

**Remark 15.2.3 on the ep-groupoid $U(x)$** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_restricted_hyp⟧. With tame germ data: at $x$ with $d_X(x) = 0$ and $(f, \Lambda)$ transversal at $x$, there is a natural representation $U(x)$ with $d_X \equiv 0$ on it such that $z \mapsto \Lambda(f(z))$ is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid of $G_x \ltimes U(x)$, and tame. *Proof.* Theorem 15.2.2 at $x$ gives $U(x)$ with (1) and (2). Apply the generic step to $\Theta = \Lambda \circ f$: it is $\ge 0$ and a functor ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBranched · SBLocalization.isFunctorial_comp⟧; its support is closed in $X$ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBranched · SBLocalization.isClosed_supp_comp⟧, being locally the finite union of the closed sets $\{f = s_i\}$ (§XV.21); its support points are smooth (§XV.12); and at a support point $y \in U(x)$, $d_X(y) = 0$ by (1) and $(f, \Lambda)$ is transversal at $y$ by (2), so the local tame form above gives a tame local branching structure at $y$. *No paracompactness* is assumed: the remark holds on $U(x)$ exactly as stated, with the inherited tame germ data. *Witness* ⟦Chapter15/TameWitness · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_restricted_hyp_witness⟧: all hypotheses together at $0 \in \mathbb R^1$ in the example of §XV.12, with the conclusion.

### XV.15 Definition 15.2.4 (2017: Definition 15.3): good position

At a point with $d_X(x) \ge 1$ transversality is not enough: the kernels must also sit well against the boundary. The text calls Definition 15.2.4 "a version of Definition 3.6.6"; the formalization makes it exactly that, branch by branch.

**Statement** (restated). *One branch* ⟦Chapter15/BoundaryPerturbation · SBLocalization.IsBranchGoodPosition⟧: for $s$ sc⁺ on an open $O \ni x$, the branch $f - s$ is in good position at $x$ if the section $(f - s)|_O$ of $W|_O$ is in good position at $x$ in a tame chart, Part I's Definition 3.6.6 in its tame germ reading: germ data at $x$ in a tame chart of $W|_O$, a linearization at $x$ that is onto, and a kernel in good position to the partial quadrant (Definition 3.1.24). *The pair* ⟦Chapter15/BoundaryPerturbation · SBLocalization.IsGoodPositionAt⟧: $(f, \Lambda)$ is in good position at $x$ if $\Lambda(f(x)) > 0$, $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is onto (Definition 15.2.1), and there is a local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ of $\Lambda$ on an open $O \ni x$ such that every branch with $s_i(x) = f(x)$ is in good position at $x$.

**Readings.** (i) The text's (1), "$X$ is tame near $x$", and (3), "the kernels of the $L_i$ are in good position to $C_xX$", are read together per branch as Part I's good position of $(f - s_i)|_O$, which carries a tame chart at $x$ (giving (1)) and the kernel condition (giving (3)). (ii) The condition is asked for one local structure; the text does not say whether it depends on the structure chosen. Theorem 15.2.5 below propagates it with the same structure. (iii) The text assumes $x \in \partial X$; the formal definition does not, and everything below holds at any point where the condition holds.

**At an interior point, transversality is good position** (extra) ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.isGoodPositionAt_of_interior⟧: if $d_X(x) = 0$ and $(f, \Lambda)$ is transversal at $x$ ($f$ with tame germ data), then $(f, \Lambda)$ is in good position at $x$. *Proof.* Take the structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ on a natural representation $U_0(x)$ and a chart $c$ inside it, as in Step 1 of §XV.13. For a branch through $f(x)$, transversality and the text's reading give the chart operator onto at $x$ (Step 2 of §XV.13); this is Part I's surjectivity of the linearization of $(f - s_i)|_{U_0(x)}$ at $x$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.isLinSurjAt_subRestrict⟧ (§XV.10). The degeneracy index of $x$ in the open M-polyfold $U_0(x)$ is that in $X$, so $0$. Part I: at a zero with $d = 0$, an onto linearization is good position in a tame chart ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTIndexG · StrongBundle.isGoodPositionGerm_of_isLinSurjAt⟧, since at an interior point the partial quadrant is the whole space and the kernel condition is empty. So every branch through $f(x)$ is in good position, which is Definition 15.2.4.

This lemma is what lets the tame forms of Remark 15.2.3 (§XV.14) be derived from those of Theorem 15.2.5 (§XV.16).

**Witness** ⟦Chapter15/GoodPositionWitness · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_hyp_witness⟧: in the example of §XV.12, $(f, \Lambda_0)$ is in good position at every point of $\mathbb R^n$. *Proof.* There every pair is in general position (Definition 15.3.5), the fibres being $0$ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBranched · SBLocalization.isInGeneralPosition_epSEP_zero⟧, and general position gives good position at every point of the support (Remark 15.3.6, §XV.20) ⟦Chapter15/GoodPositionBranches · SBLocalization.remark_15_3_6_goodPosition_hyp⟧. Zero fibres: a consistency witness.

### XV.16 Theorem 15.2.5 (2017: Theorem 15.3) and Remark 15.2.6: boundary local perturbation, tame, and under general position

**Statement** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/BoundaryPerturbation · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_hyp⟧. Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section functor with tame germ data, $\Lambda$ an sc⁺-multisection, and $(f, \Lambda)$ in good position at $x$. Then there are a natural representation $U(x)$, a local sc⁺-section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ on an open $O$ and a chart $c$ with $U(x) \subset \operatorname{dom}(c) \subset O$ such that, with $M_i = \{y \in U(x) : f(y) = s_i(y)\}$: every $M_i$ is a smooth submanifold (Definition 4.2.1); (1) the inclusions $M_i \hookrightarrow U(x)$ are proper; $\{y \in U(x) : \Lambda(f(y)) > 0\} = \bigcup_iM_i$; (2) $(f, \Lambda)$ is in good position, with the same structure, at every $y \in U(x)$ with $\Lambda(f(y)) > 0$, so $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ is onto with the branches in good position; (3) $\Lambda(f(y)) = \sum_{i : y \in M_i}\sigma_i$ on $U(x)$; $T(f, \Lambda)(y) = \sum_{i : f(y) = s_i(y)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(y)$ in $c$; and only the branches through $f(x)$ meet $f$ in $U(x)$. "Tame submanifolds" and "tame branched" are the tame form below. *Narrower* by the inherited tame germ data; $x \in \partial X$ is not required.

**Proof.** The text: "along the lines of the proof of Theorem 15.2.2", with Part I's implicit function theorem in good position. The Lean proof is that proof with the good-position lemma in place of the interior one.

*Step 1.* Good position at $x$ supplies the structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ on an open $O \ni x$ in which the branches through $f(x)$ are in good position. Take a chart $c_0$ around $x$ and restrict it to $\operatorname{dom}(c_0) \cap O$: a chart $c$ with domain in $O$. A natural representation $U_0(x)$ is taken from the multisection property of $\Lambda$; only its existence is used, for the shrinking in Step 3.

*Step 2, one branch* ⟦Chapter15/BoundaryPerturbation · SBLocalization.exists_branch_goodPosition⟧: for each $i$ an open $V_i \ni x$ in $\operatorname{dom}(c)$ with $\{y \in V_i : f(y) = s_i(y)\}$ a smooth submanifold, each of its points smooth, with the branch in good position there and the chart operator onto there, and only meeting $f$ if $s_i(x) = f(x)$. If the branch passes through $f(x)$ this is the good-position local solution lemma of §XV.10 ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.exists_solutionSet_of_isGoodPosition⟧: Part I's Theorem 3.6.8 for $(f - s_i)|_O$ at $x$ in good position, which gives an open set of the zeros of which each is in good position, read back in $X$; the chart surjectivity at those zeros comes from the good position there. If it does not, $V_i = \{y \in \operatorname{dom}(c) : f(y) \ne s_i(y)\}$, as in §XV.13 ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.isOpen_sep_ne⟧.

*Step 3.* Shrink $U_0(x)$ into the open set $\operatorname{dom}(c) \cap \bigcap_iV_i$ ⟦Chapter7/NaturalRep · EpGroupoid.exists_naturalRep_shrink⟧. No condition on $d_X$ enters.

*Step 4.* The count (3), the description of the support, the submanifolds $M_i$, the properness (1) and the formula for $T(f, \Lambda)$ are proved word for word as Steps 5, 7 and 8 of §XV.13.

*Step 5, (2).* At $y \in U(x)$ with $\Lambda(f(y)) > 0$: some branch passes through $f(y)$, so $y$ is smooth, hence at level $1$, by Step 2. The structure on $O$ and the chart $c$ are local data at $y$; every branch $j$ through $f(y)$ has its chart operator onto at $y$ (Step 2), so $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ is onto by the text's reading of §XV.12 ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.surjective_toIntr_subDeriv_iff⟧; and every branch $j$ through $f(y)$ is in good position at $y$ (Step 2). These are the three clauses of Definition 15.2.4 at $y$, with the same structure.

**Tame local solution sets** (extra) ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.exists_tame_solutionSet_of_isBranchGoodPosition⟧. If a branch $f - s$ with $f(x) = s(x)$ is in good position at $x$, there is an open $V \ni x$ inside $O$ such that $\{y \in V : f(y) = s(y)\}$ is a smooth submanifold of $X$ with a tame induced structure (Definition 2.5.18). *Proof.* Part I's Theorem 3.1.26, the implicit function theorem in the boundary case, for the tame sc-Fredholm section $(f - s)|_O$ at its zero $x$ in good position ⟦Chapter3/GlobalIFTNearbyOntoG · theorem_3_1_26'⟧, gives an open $U \subset O$ around $x$ whose zeros are smooth and in good position and whose zero set is a sub-M-polyfold with a tame induced structure. The zero set in $U$ is a smooth submanifold of $O$ ⟦Chapter5/GeneralPosition · StrongBundle.isSmoothSubmanifold_solutionSet_inter⟧, the zeros of $(f - s)|_O$ being the points with $f = s$ ⟦Chapter14/LocalScPlus · SBLocalization.isZeroAt_subSec_iff⟧. Both properties are carried to $X$ by the sc-diffeomorphism $O \to X$ onto the open set $O$ ⟦Chapter8/TangentLocal · MPolyfold.isScDiffeoOn_subtypeVal⟧; $V$ is the image of $U$.

**Theorem 15.2.5, tame** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_tame_hyp⟧. In good position at $x$, $z \mapsto \Lambda(f(z))$ has a tame local branching structure at $x$ (Definition 9.3.6): a natural representation $U(x)$ with the properness property, the sets $M_j = \{z \in U(x) : f(z) = s_j(z)\}$ over the branches through $f(x)$, each a smooth submanifold with a tame induced structure and a proper inclusion, the weights $\sigma_j$, and the count. *Proof.* For every branch choose $V_i$: the tame local solution set above if the branch passes through $f(x)$, and $\{y \in O : f(y) \ne s_i(y)\}$ otherwise. Every object has a neighbourhood $Q$ with the properness property (Definition 7.5.8) ⟦Chapter7/EpGroupoid · EpGroupoid.exists_hasPropernessProperty⟧. Theorem 7.1.13 ⟦Chapter7/NaturalRep · EpGroupoid.theorem_7_1_13⟧ gives a natural representation $U(x)$ inside the open set $Q \cap O \cap \bigcap_iV_i$; it has the properness property, its closure lying in that of $Q$. The $M_j$ are the intersections of the tame solution sets with the open $U(x)$, which keeps both the submanifold property and the tame induced structure; properness and the count are as in the local form of Remark 15.2.3 (§XV.14), with the index set the branches through $f(x)$. This is the text's "by definition $\Lambda \circ f$ … is a tame branched ep⁺-subgroupoid, since it is represented by tame submanifolds".

**The global tame form** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_isTameBranched_hyp⟧: if $(f, \Lambda)$ is in good position at every point of the support and $|\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)|$ is paracompact, then $\Lambda \circ f$ is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid of $X$ and tame; the fields are those of the global form of Remark 15.2.3 (§XV.14), with functoriality ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBranched · SBLocalization.isFunctorial_comp⟧ and the tame local branching structures just proved. The same assembly gives the global tame form of Remark 15.2.3 (§XV.14), and, through good position from general position (Remark 15.3.6), the tame forms of Remark 15.3.6 ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.remark_15_3_6_isTameBranched_hyp⟧ and of the "in particular" of Theorem 15.3.7 ⟦Chapter15/TameBranched · SBLocalization.theorem_15_3_7_in_particular_tame_hyp⟧ (§§XV.20–XV.21).

**On the ep-groupoid $U(x)$** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/RestrictedEp · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_restricted_hyp⟧: in good position at $x$, there is a natural representation $U(x)$ on whose translation groupoid $G_x \ltimes U(x)$ the functor $z \mapsto \Lambda(f(z))$ is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid, and tame. *Proof.* Theorem 15.2.5 gives $U(x)$ with good position at every support point in it; the generic step of §XV.14 applies, the tame local branching structures at those points coming from the tame form above. No paracompactness is assumed.

**The closing sentence: general position.** The text: the same conclusion holds if (2) is replaced by the requirement that the restrictions of $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ to the reduced tangent spaces $T^R_yX$ are surjective, pointing ahead to Definition 15.3.5.

*Definition 15.3.5 at one point* ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.IsReducedOntoAtLevel⟧ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.IsInGeneralPositionAt⟧: $(f, \Lambda)$ is in general position at $x$ if $x \in X_1$ and every operator $L$ of $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$, restricted to $T^R_xX$, is onto $W_x$, with $T^R_xX$ read in every chart around $x$ and every presentation of its partial quadrant (Part I's reduced tangent fibre). Definition 15.3.5 (§XV.20) is this condition at every point of the support ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.isInGeneralPosition_iff_forall_at⟧, the support lying in $X_1$ ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.mem_levelSet_one_of_pos⟧.

*Reading.* "(2) replaced by general position" is read: the hypothesis "good position at $x$" is replaced by "general position at $x$", and the conclusion (2) by "general position at every point of $M_{U(x)}$". Good position at $x$ and on $M_{U(x)}$ still holds, so the formal conclusion gives both forms of (2).

Four lemmas connect general position of the pair to Part I's general position of the branches (Definition 5.3.9 for $(f - s_i)|_O$: the linearization at the zero is onto, with kernel transversal to $T^R$).

- *Part I, in every chart* (extra) ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · StrongBundle.linKer_sup_reduced_eq_of_isGeneralPositionAt⟧: if a smooth submanifold $A$ of the solution set of a section $f$ is in general position at $y$ (Definition 4.2.10), then in every strong bundle chart around $y$, at every level-$1$ point $z$ over $\varphi(y)$ and for every presentation of the partial quadrant, $\ker\mathrm{Lin}(z) + T^R_zO = T_zO$. *Proof.* General position of $A$ gives, in the chart, a splitting $T_zO = N \oplus B$ with $N$ the tangent space of $A$ and $B \subset T^R_zO$. The tangent space of a submanifold of zeros lies in the kernel of the linearization, so $N \subset \ker\mathrm{Lin}(z)$; hence $T_zO = N + B \subset \ker\mathrm{Lin}(z) + T^R_zO \subset T_zO$. This is the converse of Part I's chart criterion ⟦Chapter5/CylinderPerturbation · isGeneralPositionGerm_of_sup_eq⟧, and it holds in every chart, not only in the chart of the germ data.
- *From the pair to the branches* ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.isGeneralPositionGerm_branch_at⟧: if $(f, \Lambda)$ is in general position at $x$, every branch $(f - s_i)|_O$ through $f(x)$ of any local structure on an open $O \ni x$ is in Part I's general position at $x$. *Proof.* The point $x$ is smooth (§XV.12) ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_smoothSet_of_eq⟧, so the tame germ data of $f$ provide a tame chart centred at $x$; restrict it to $O$. With the structure it is local data at $x$, so $(f - s_i)'(x)$ through this chart lies in the support of $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_support_linSumIntr_iff⟧ and is onto on $T^R_xX$ by hypothesis. The chart step of Remark 15.3.6 (§XV.20) ⟦Chapter15/GoodPositionBranches · SBLocalization.isGeneralPositionGerm_subRestrict⟧ turns this into Part I's general position of $(f - s_i)|_O$: in the transported chart of $W|_O$ the intrinsic operator is the chart derivative of the branch, onto from $T_0O \cap T^R_0$ means onto with $\ker + T^R_0O = T_0O$, which is Part I's chart criterion.
- *Good position from general position* ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.isGoodPositionAt_of_isInGeneralPositionAt⟧: general position at $x$ with $\Lambda(f(x)) > 0$ gives good position at $x$. *Proof.* $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ is onto, because each operator is already onto on the subspace $T^R_xX$, read in some chart with a presentation ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.exists_chart_presentation⟧. Take any local structure on an open $O \ni x$ ⟦Chapter13/MultisectionSupport · SBLocalization.IsScPlusMultisection.exists_isLocalRepOn⟧; each branch through $f(x)$ is in Part I's general position (previous lemma), hence in good position in a tame chart, by Part I's Lemma 5.26 ⟦Chapter5/Transversality · StrongBundle.IsGeneralPositionGerm.isGoodPositionGermTame⟧, the branch being a tame sc-Fredholm section of $W|_O$ with $x$ a zero.
- *From the branches back to the pair* ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.isReducedOntoAtLevel_of_isGeneralPositionGerm⟧: if, for a local structure on $O \ni y$ and a chart $c$ around $y$ with domain in $O$, every branch through $f(y)$ is in Part I's general position at $y$, then $(f, \Lambda)$ is in general position at $y$. *Proof.* The reduced tangent fibre does not depend on the chart and presentation ⟦Chapter2/ReducedTangentIntrinsic · reducedTangentFiber_eq⟧, so it suffices to work in $c$ with one presentation. An operator of the support is $(f - s_j)'(y)$ for a branch $j$ through $f(y)$ (§XV.12). Its reduced restriction is onto ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.surjective_reduced_toIntr_of_isGeneralPositionGerm⟧, by the chart lemma: *every vector $k$ of the fibre $K_z$ has a preimage under the chart operator in $T^R_zO \cap T_zO$* ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.exists_reduced_preimage_of_isGeneralPositionGerm⟧. For this lemma, Part I's general position of $(f - s_j)|_O$ gives good position, hence a smooth solution submanifold in general position at $y$ ⟦Chapter5/GeneralPosition · exists_isGeneralPositionGerm_iff⟧; the first lemma gives $\ker + T^R = T$ in the restricted chart; the linearization is onto, so $k$ has a preimage $v = a + b$ with $a$ in the kernel and $b \in T^R$, and $b$ is a preimage; and the derivative of the restricted chart is that of $c$ ⟦Chapter15/LocalIFT · SBLocalization.scDeriv_restrictTo_subRestrict⟧. Passing from the chart operator to the intrinsic one has two cases, as the intrinsic operator is defined by cases: if the chart operator takes values in the image of the fibre identification, the preimage $b$ is carried to a reduced tangent vector with the right image; otherwise the intrinsic operator is $0$, and the full operator being onto (§XV.10) forces $W_y = 0$, where any operator is onto.

**Theorem 15.2.5, closing sentence** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionLocal · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_generalPosition_hyp⟧. Let $X$ be tame, $f$ with tame germ data, $\Lambda(f(x)) > 0$ and $(f, \Lambda)$ in general position at $x$. Then all conclusions of Theorem 15.2.5 hold on a natural representation $U(x)$, and $(f, \Lambda)$ is in general position and in good position at every $y \in U(x)$ with $\Lambda(f(y)) > 0$. *Proof.* *Step 1.* General position at $x$ gives good position at $x$, and Theorem 15.2.5 gives $U_1(x)$, the structure on $O$, the chart and all conclusions. *Step 2, openness of general position.* $O$ is tame, an open subset of the tame $X$ ⟦Chapter11/FaceIntersectionCounterexample · MPolyfold.isTame_restrictOpen⟧. For each branch through $f(x)$, Part I's general position of $(f - s_i)|_O$ at $x$ (the second lemma) is open along the solution set: Part I's Theorem 4.2.11 (3), in the form that a zero in general position of a tame sc-Fredholm section over a tame M-polyfold has an open neighbourhood $W_i$ at whose zeros the section is in general position ⟦Chapter5/GeneralPosition · exists_isOpen_isGeneralPositionGerm⟧. For the other branches $W_i = X$. *Step 3.* Shrink $U_1(x)$ into $\bigcap_iW_i$ ⟦Chapter7/NaturalRep · EpGroupoid.exists_naturalRep_shrink⟧; the conclusions of Theorem 15.2.5 survive the shrinking, as in Step 7 of §XV.13. *Step 4.* At a support point $y \in U(x)$, every branch through $f(y)$ passes through $f(x)$ (last clause of Theorem 15.2.5) and $y \in W_i$, so it is in Part I's general position at $y$; the fourth lemma gives general position of the pair at $y$, and good position is clause (2) of Theorem 15.2.5. *The hypothesis of tameness* is that of Definition 15.3.5, which the text states over tame ep-groupoids, and it is what Part I's openness of general position needs. *Witness* ⟦Chapter15/GoodPositionWitness · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_generalPosition_hyp_witness⟧: the example of §XV.12, where the trivial ep-groupoid of $\mathbb R^n$ is tame ⟦Chapter7/EpGroupoid · EpGroupoid.isTame_trivial_rn⟧ and every pair is in general position ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBranched · SBLocalization.isInGeneralPosition_epSEP_zero⟧. Zero fibres: a consistency witness.

**Witnesses for Theorem 15.2.5.** The hypotheses in good position hold together at every point of the example of §XV.12 (§XV.15) ⟦Chapter15/GoodPositionWitness · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_hyp_witness⟧, and the conclusion is obtained there, with good position on all of $U(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/GoodPositionWitness · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_hyp_applies⟧. The example has no boundary and zero fibres, so it does not exercise the corner geometry for which Theorem 15.2.5 is made; Part I's Theorem 3.1.26, on which the tame form rests, has its own witnesses in Part III.

**Remark 15.2.6** (pp. 690–691; nothing to formalize). Prose: with an oriented $(f, o)$ the branches $M_i$ inherit orientations $o_{i,y}$ from the surjective oriented $(f - s_i)'(y)$, recorded in the formal sum $\hat T_{((f,o),\Lambda)}(y) = \sum\sigma_i \cdot (T_yM_i, o_{i,y})$. The local data it needs are in place: the $M_i$ of Theorems 15.2.2 and 15.2.5 and the surjective $(f - s_i)'(y)$ of (2) with the text's reading of §XV.12. The orientations themselves are Theorem 15.4.1 and Definition 15.4.2, formalized at a single point in §XV.34.

### XV.17 The paragraph before Definition 15.3.1, Definition 15.3.1 and Remark 15.3.2 (2017: Definition 15.4, Remark 15.4)

§15.3 opens (p. 691) with the standing data of a perturbation problem: a strong bundle $(P : W \to X, \mu)$ over an ep-groupoid, an sc-Fredholm section functor $f$, and an auxiliary norm $N$ (Definition 12.2.1, which is Definition 8.3.5). An auxiliary norm is defined on $W_{0,1}$; the text extends it by $N(h) = +\infty$ off $W_{0,1}$, so that "$N(f(x)) \le 1$" makes sense for every $x$. The formalization does not extend $N$. It keeps $N$ on the level-$(0,1)$ part $W[1]$ and reads "$N(h) \le r$" as Part I's relation $\mathrm{AuxLe}$: $h \in W_{0,1}$ and the value of $N$ at $h$ is at most $r$ ⟦Chapter5/Compactness · StrongBundle.AuxLe⟧. This is exactly the text's convention, a point with $N(h) = +\infty$ satisfying no inequality $N(h) \le r$.

**Definition 15.3.1** (restated) ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.ControlsCompactness⟧. $(N, U)$ *controls the compactness of* $f$ if

1. $N$ is an auxiliary norm of $(W, \mu)$ (Definition 8.3.5: an auxiliary norm of the strong bundle, invariant under $\mu$);
2. $U \subset X$ is open;
3. $U$ is saturated;
4. $S_f = \{x : f(x) = 0_x\} \subset U$;
5. the closure in $|X|$ of $\pi(\{x \in U : N(f(x)) \le 1\})$ is compact, $\pi : X \to |X|$ the orbit map.

*Readings.* The orbit space "of a set" $A \subset X$ is its image $\pi(A)$. The text places the definition over an ep-groupoid with paracompact $|X|$. Paracompactness is not part of the formal definition, which is therefore more general; the statements that use paracompactness (the structurable addendum of Theorem 15.3.7, Theorem 15.3.9, Corollary 15.3.10) carry it as a hypothesis of their own. The text calls $f$ an sc-Fredholm section functor; the formal predicate takes any $f$, and the theorems that use it supply the Fredholm property.

*Monotonicity* ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.ControlsCompactness.mono⟧. If $(N, U)$ controls the compactness of $f$ and $U' \subset U$ is a smaller saturated open set still containing $S_f$, then $(N, U')$ controls it. *Proof.* Clauses (1)–(4) for $U'$ are the hypotheses. For (5): $\{x \in U' : N(f(x)) \le 1\} \subset \{x \in U : N(f(x)) \le 1\}$, so the closure of its image lies in the closure of the larger image, a compact set; a closed subset of a compact set is compact. The lemma is used wherever a proof shrinks the controlling neighbourhood.

**The paragraph before Definition 15.3.1** (same) ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.exists_controlsCompactness⟧. *Statement.* For an sc-Fredholm section functor $f$ with $|S_f| = \pi(S_f)$ compact and any auxiliary norm $N$, there is a $U$ with $(N, U)$ controlling the compactness of $f$. The text says this follows from "the discussion in Section 12.4".

*Proof.* It is Theorem 12.4.2, the implication (2) $\Rightarrow$ (3), of Part XII: an sc-Fredholm section functor with compact solution set is proper$_{<\infty}$, that is, for every auxiliary norm $N$ there is a saturated open $U \supset S_f$ such that $\operatorname{cl}_{|X|}\pi(\{x \in U : N(f(x)) \le C\})$ is compact for every $0 < C < \infty$ ⟦Chapter12/CompactnessEp · EpStrongBundle.isProperLtInftyFunctor_of_hasCompactSolutionSetEp⟧ ⟦Chapter12/CompactnessEp · EpStrongBundle.IsProperLtInftyFunctor⟧. The formal proof then takes $C = 1$. The proof of the implication, as Part XII retells it, runs in four steps, and we recall it because it is the whole content of the paragraph.

*Step 1, the local step.* At each zero $x$ of $f$ the local step of Theorem 5.2.2 gives an open $V(x) \ni x$ such that $\operatorname{cl}\{y \in V(x) : N(f(y)) \le C\}$ is compact for every $C > 0$ ⟦Chapter12/CompactnessEp · exists_nhds_isCompact_closure_auxLe⟧; this uses only that $f$, as a section of the object M-polyfold, is sc-Fredholm.

*Step 2, a finite cover.* The orbit map is open (Proposition 7.1.8, Part VII) ⟦Chapter7/Basic · EpGroupoid.proposition_7_1_8⟧, so the sets $\pi(V(x))$, $x \in S_f$, are an open cover of the compact $|S_f|$; finitely many, $\pi(V(x_1)), \dots, \pi(V(x_m))$, suffice. Let $O$ be their union and $U = \pi^{-1}(O)$: it is open (continuity of $\pi$), saturated (a preimage under $\pi$), and contains $S_f$.

*Step 3, invariance.* If $y \in U$ with $N(f(y)) \le C$, then $\pi(y) = \pi(y')$ for some $y' \in V(x_i)$, that is, there is a morphism $\varphi : y \to y'$. Since $f$ is a section functor and $N$ is $\mu$-invariant, $N(f(y')) = N(\mu(\varphi, f(y))) = N(f(y)) \le C$ ⟦Chapter12/CompactnessEp · EpStrongBundle.auxLe_tgt_of_auxLe_src⟧.

*Step 4, compactness.* Hence $\pi(\{y \in U : N(f(y)) \le C\})$ lies in $K = \bigcup_i\pi(\operatorname{cl}\{y \in V(x_i) : N(f(y)) \le C\})$, a finite union of continuous images of compact sets, compact, and closed because $|X|$ is Hausdorff. The closure of the image lies in $K$ and is compact.

*Departure from the text.* The text's proof of Theorem 12.4.2 shrinks to neighbourhoods with the natural action and uses $\operatorname{cl}_{|X|}|U| = |\operatorname{cl}_XU|$; neither is needed, because the closure lies in the finite union $K$. The paracompactness of $|X|$, which Theorem 12.4.2 assumes, is not used ⟦Chapter12/CompactnessEp · EpStrongBundle.theorem_12_4_2⟧.

**Remark 15.3.2** (nothing to formalize). The remark says that the construction of perturbations needs sc-smooth bump functions in general, and that geometric constructions may replace them at the price of checking compactness by hand. It is a comment. In the formalization bump functions enter as a hypothesis exactly where perturbations are built: Theorem 15.3.7 assumes that $X$ admits sc-smooth bump functions in the pointwise reading (§XV.21), and the cylinder $[0, 1] \times X$ of Corollary 15.3.10 inherits them from $X$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeEp · EpGroupoid.admitsBumpFunctionsPointwise_cubeProd⟧ (§XV.30). The sentence after Definition 15.3.1, "we also assume that $X$ admits sc-smooth bump functions", is likewise read as a hypothesis of the theorems that construct perturbations, not as part of any definition.

### XV.18 Definition 15.3.3 (2017: 15.5) and the example after it

**Definition 15.3.3** (same) ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.IsAdmissible⟧. An *$(N, U)$-admissible perturbation* for $f$ is a function $\Lambda : W \to \mathbb Q^+$ such that

1. $\Lambda$ is an sc⁺-multisection (Definition 13.2.1, in the 2021 form with local section structures, Part XIII);
2. $N(\Lambda)(x) \le 1$ for every $x \in X$, where $N(\Lambda)(x)$ is the pointwise norm of Definition 13.2.3, the largest value of $N$ on the finite set $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda)(x) = \{w \in W_x : \Lambda(w) > 0\}$ ⟦Chapter13/MultisectionSupport · SBLocalization.pointwiseNorm⟧;
3. $\{x \in X : \exists t \in [-1, 1],\ (t\Lambda)(f(x)) > 0\} \subset U$, where $t\Lambda$ is the scalar multiple of Definition 13.2.10: $(0\Lambda)(w) = 1$ if $w$ is a zero vector and $0$ otherwise, and $(t\Lambda)(w) = \Lambda(w/t)$ for $t \ne 0$ ⟦Chapter13/MultisectionAlgebra · SBLocalization.multisectionSmul⟧.

*Readings.* The text's standing data, $f$ sc-Fredholm with compact $|S_f|$ and $(N, U)$ controlling compactness, are not part of the predicate; they are hypotheses of the theorems that use it (Theorem 15.3.9, the boundary conditions of Corollary 15.3.10). The pointwise norm is defined as a supremum; for an sc⁺-multisection it is attained, the support over a point being finite and nonempty, and the points of the support lie in $W_{0,1}$, where $N$ is defined (Part XIII). Condition (3) with $t = 0$ asks that $S_f \subset U$, since $(0\Lambda)(f(x)) > 0$ exactly when $f(x) = 0_x$; with $t = \pm 1$ it asks that the support of $\Lambda \circ f$ and of $(-\Lambda) \circ f$ lie in $U$. The interval $[-1, 1]$ is what the cobordism argument of Corollary 15.3.10 (3) needs: perturbations $t\Lambda$ along a path stay inside $U$.

**The example after Definition 15.3.3** (same) ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.isAdmissible_of_domSupp_subset⟧. *Statement.* Let $f$ be a section with $S_f \subset U$, and $\Lambda$ an sc⁺-multisection with $N(\Lambda) \le 1$ whose domain support lies in $U$. Then $\Lambda$ is $(N, U)$-admissible. The domain support is $\operatorname{dom\text{-}supp}(\Lambda) = \operatorname{cl}_X\{x : \exists w \in W_x \setminus \{0_x\},\ \Lambda(w) > 0\}$ (Definition 13.2.2 (1)) ⟦Chapter13/MultisectionSupport · SBLocalization.domSupp⟧.

*Proof.* Conditions (1) and (2) are hypotheses; condition (3) is the content, and it goes in three steps.

*Step 1, off the domain support $\Lambda$ sees only the zero vector* ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.eq_zero_of_not_mem_domSupp⟧. If $x \notin \operatorname{dom\text{-}supp}(\Lambda)$ and $w \in W_x$ is not $0_x$, then $\Lambda(w) = 0$: otherwise $x$ would lie in the set whose closure is the domain support.

*Step 2, the same for every scalar multiple* ⟦Chapter15/ControllingPairs · SBLocalization.eq_fiberZero_of_multisectionSmul_pos⟧. If $P(w) \notin \operatorname{dom\text{-}supp}(\Lambda)$ and $(t\Lambda)(w) > 0$ for some real $t$, then $w$ is the zero vector of its fibre. For $t = 0$ this is the definition of $0\Lambda$. For $t \ne 0$, $(t\Lambda)(w) = \Lambda(t^{-1}w)$, with $t^{-1}w$ computed in the fibre; if $w \ne 0$ then $t^{-1}w \ne 0$ (multiply back by $t$), so $\Lambda(t^{-1}w) = 0$ by Step 1, a contradiction.

*Step 3.* Let $x \notin U$ with $(t\Lambda)(f(x)) > 0$ for some $t \in [-1, 1]$. Since $\operatorname{dom\text{-}supp}(\Lambda) \subset U$, $x \notin \operatorname{dom\text{-}supp}(\Lambda)$, and $P(f(x)) = x$ because $f$ is a section; by Step 2, $f(x) = 0_x$, so $x \in S_f \subset U$, a contradiction.

The text's argument is Step 1 and Step 3 for $t = 1$; Step 2 makes the argument cover every $t \in [-1, 1]$, including $t = 0$, where $0\Lambda$ is the multisection of the zero section and the claim is $S_f \subset U$. Only the section property of $f$ and $S_f \subset U$ are used: neither the Fredholm property nor the control of compactness enters. Witness of the definition: the zero multisection, whose domain support is empty and whose pointwise norm is $0$ (§XV.21), is admissible for every controlling pair by this example.

### XV.19 The products remark after Definition 15.3.3, and Remark 15.3.4: product ep-groupoids and product strong bundles

After Definition 15.3.3 (pp. 692–693) the text observes that admissible perturbations "behave well under products": for two problems $(W, f, N, U, \Lambda)$ and $(W', f', N', U', \Lambda')$ it takes the product $f \times f'$ as an sc-Fredholm section of $W \times W' \to X \times X'$, the auxiliary norm $(N \times N')(h, h') = \max\{N(h), N'(h')\}$, the product multisection $(\Lambda \times \Lambda')(h, h') = \Lambda(h)\Lambda'(h')$, and checks that $\Lambda \times \Lambda'$ is admissible for $(N \times N', U \times U')$. Remark 15.3.4 adds that the domain support of $\Lambda \times \Lambda'$ lies in $(U \times X') \cup (X \times U')$, not necessarily in $U \times U'$. The text takes the product ep-groupoid, the product strong bundle and its structure map for granted. The formalization builds them: the product ep-groupoid (extra, row V15.17), the product strong bundle of Part I (restated, V15.53), products of sections (same, V15.54), the product over the product ep-groupoid with $\mu \times \mu'$ (same, V15.55), and $N \times N'$ as an auxiliary norm (same, V15.56). The rest of the remark, the sc-Fredholm property of $f \times f'$ and the multisection $\Lambda \times \Lambda'$ with its admissibility and domain support, is proved as printed (same, V15.57), and Remark 15.3.4's inclusion is the best possible. The product ep-groupoid is also the base of the cylinder $[0, 1]^n \times X$ of Corollary 15.3.10 (§XV.30).

**The product ep-groupoid** (extra) ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.prod⟧. *Statement.* For ep-groupoids $X$, $X'$, the groupoid with objects $X \times X'$ and morphisms $\mathbf X \times \mathbf X'$, all structure maps (source, target, composition, unit, inverse) taken componentwise ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · GroupoidStr.prod⟧, and the product M-polyfold structures on objects and morphisms (Exposition Lemma 4.D, Part II), is an ep-groupoid (Definition 7.1.3, Part VII). The groupoid axioms hold componentwise. *Proof* of the four conditions of Definition 7.1.3.

*(1)–(2), the étale property and the smooth structure maps.* Products of sc-smooth maps are sc-smooth on products of open sets: $f \times g = (f \circ \mathrm{pr}_1, g \circ \mathrm{pr}_2)$, a pair of composites with the sc-smooth projections ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · MPolyfold.IsScSmoothOn.prodMap⟧. Hence a product of sc-diffeomorphisms between open sets is one, with the product of the inverses as inverse ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · MPolyfold.IsScDiffeoOn.prodMap⟧, and a product of local sc-diffeomorphisms is one: at $(p_1, p_2)$ take the product of the two local inverses ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · MPolyfold.IsLocalScDiffeo.prodMap⟧. So $s \times s'$ and $t \times t'$ are local sc-diffeomorphisms, and the unit and inverse maps, products of sc-smooth maps on $\mathrm{univ} \times \mathrm{univ}$, are sc-smooth.

*(3), properness.* At $(x, x')$ take the neighbourhoods $V \ni x$, $V' \ni x'$ of condition (3) for $X$ and $X'$, with $t : s^{-1}(\operatorname{cl}V) \to X$ and $t' : s'^{-1}(\operatorname{cl}V') \to X'$ proper. For $V \times V'$, the closure of a product is the product of the closures, so $(s \times s')^{-1}(\operatorname{cl}(V \times V')) = s^{-1}(\operatorname{cl}V) \times s'^{-1}(\operatorname{cl}V')$, and $t \times t'$ restricted to it is proper: a product of proper maps, composed with the homeomorphism between a product of subsets and the subset of the product ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · isProperMap_domRestrict_prod⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.prod_exists_isProperMap⟧.

*(4), sc-smooth composition.* Condition (4) asks that composition be sc-smooth on the fibred product $\mathbf X\,{}_s\!\times_t\mathbf X$; Part VII proves it equivalent to its sheet form: for every sheet $\sigma$ of $s$ (a local inverse of $s$ on an open $V$), the map $\psi \mapsto m(\sigma(t(\psi)), \psi)$ is sc-smooth on $t^{-1}(V)$ ⟦Chapter7/EpGroupoid · isScSmoothComp_iff_sheet⟧. The formal proof checks the sheet form for the product ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.prod_isScSmoothCompSheet⟧. Let $\sigma$ be a sheet of $s \times s'$ over $V$, and $\psi_0 \in (t \times t')^{-1}(V)$. Take sheets $h_1$ of $s$ near the first component of $\sigma(t(\psi_0))$, defined on $U_1$, and $h_2$ of $s'$ near the second, on $U_2$; let $O$ be the open set of $\psi$ near $\psi_0$ with $\sigma(t(\psi)) \in U_1 \times U_2$. On $O$, $s(\sigma(t(\psi))_1) = t(\psi_1)$, and since $h_1$ is a left inverse of $s$ on $U_1$, $\sigma(t(\psi))_1 = h_1(t(\psi_1))$; likewise for the second component. So on $O$ the map is $\psi \mapsto (m(h_1(t\psi_1), \psi_1), m'(h_2(t'\psi_2), \psi_2))$, a pair of maps that are sc-smooth by the sheet form of (4) for $X$ and $X'$, composed with the projections. Sc-smoothness is local, which concludes.

*Consequences.* The projections are sc-smooth functors ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.prodFst⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.prodSnd⟧. If $X$ and $X'$ are tame, so is $X \times X'$, with $d_{X \times X'}(x, x') = d_X(x) + d_{X'}(x')$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.isTame_prod⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.degIndex_prod⟧, from the M-polyfold statement ⟦Chapter2/FibredProduct · MPolyfold.isTame_prod⟧. Two objects are isomorphic iff their components are: a morphism of the product is a pair of morphisms ⟦Chapter15/ProductEp · EpGroupoid.prod_π_eq_π_iff⟧. *Reading.* The type of ep-groupoids requires paracompact object and morphism spaces; on the products these are instances that always hold, M-polyfolds being metrizable ⟦Chapter2/FibredProduct · MPolyfold.paracompactSpace_prod⟧.

**The model level: products of strong bundle maps and retracts.** A strong bundle chart of $W$ takes values in $E \triangleleft F$, pairs (base, fibre); for the product the natural model is $(E \oplus E') \triangleleft (F \oplus F')$, base first. The two are related by the swap $((a, a'), (b, b')) \mapsto ((a, b), (a', b'))$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · bundleSwap⟧, which on every level is a continuous linear isomorphism, an sc-operator ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · ScBanach.bundleSwapOp⟧. The product of local maps $\Phi$, $\Phi'$ is $\Phi \times \Phi' := \mathrm{swap} \circ (\Phi \times \Phi') \circ \mathrm{swap}$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · prodBundleMap⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · prodBundleMap_eq⟧, sc-smooth on $(U \times U') \triangleleft (F \oplus F')$ when $\Phi$, $\Phi'$ are, as a composite of the product of sc-smooth maps with two sc-operators ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · isScSmooth_prodBundleMap⟧.

*Products of strong bundle maps* (Definition 2.6.1) ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · IsStrongBundleMap.prod⟧. Each clause of the definition is checked componentwise. $\Phi \times \Phi'$ maps $(U \times U') \triangleleft$ into $(V \times V') \triangleleft$; its base part does not depend on the fibre variable; it is linear in the fibre; it preserves the double filtration, because a pair lies on level $m$ of $E \oplus E'$ iff both entries do ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · ScBanach.mem_range_prod_toBase⟧; it is sc-smooth on $\triangleleft$ by the previous paragraph. For the shifted bundle $\triangleleft[1]$ one needs that the $[1]$-version of the product is the product of the $[1]$-versions: the lift to level $1$ of a pair of level-$1$ points is the pair of the lifts ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · ScBanach.invFun_prod_toBase_one⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · shiftFiber_prodBundleMap⟧, and the $[1]$-maps are sc-smooth by the same composition.

*Retractions and retracts* (Definitions 2.6.1, 2.6.2). For strong bundle retractions $R$, $R'$, $R \times R'$ is idempotent componentwise, so it is a strong bundle retraction ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · IsStrongBundleRetraction.prod⟧; its base retraction is $r \times r'$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · baseRetraction_prod⟧, with retract the product of the base retracts ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · retract_baseRetraction_prod⟧; and its image is the swap preimage of $K \times K'$, $K = R(U \triangleleft F)$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · image_prodBundleMap⟧. Hence $\mathrm{swap}^{-1}(K \times K')$ is a local strong bundle retract of $(C \times C') \triangleleft (F \oplus F')$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · IsStrongBundleRetract.prod⟧. Sc-smooth maps between retracts multiply in the same way: $T \times T'$ is the swap, the product $T \times T'$ of retract maps, and the swap back, three sc-smooth maps between retracts ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · IsScSmoothRetractMap.prodBundleMap⟧. The $[1]$-retract of $\mathrm{swap}^{-1}(K \times K')$ is $\mathrm{swap}^{-1}(K[1] \times K'[1])$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductStrongBundle · strongShiftOne_bundleSwap_preimage⟧.

**The product strong bundle** (restated) ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundle.prod⟧. *Statement.* For strong bundles $P : W \to X$ and $P' : W' \to X'$ over M-polyfolds, $P \times P' : W \times W' \to X \times X'$ is a strong bundle (Definition 2.6.5), with the atlas of products of charts. *Construction.*

*The fibres.* The fibre over $(x, x')$ is in bijection with $P^{-1}(x) \times P'^{-1}(x')$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · prodFiberEquiv⟧; the vector space structure is transported along the bijection, which then is a topological linear isomorphism ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · prodFiberCLE⟧. A product of Banachable spaces is Banachable ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · IsBanachable.prod⟧, and Banachability passes along topological linear isomorphisms ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · IsBanachable.of_equiv⟧.

*The charts.* For charts $\Phi$ of $W$ over $\varphi$ and $\Phi'$ of $W'$ over $\varphi'$, the product chart is $y \mapsto \mathrm{swap}(\Phi(y_1), \Phi'(y_2))$ over $\varphi \times \varphi'$, with fibre model $F \oplus F'$, domain $U \times U'$ and strong bundle retraction $R \times R'$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundleChart.prod⟧; its inverse is the pair of inverses after the swap ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundleChart.prodPartialEquiv⟧, it covers $\varphi \times \varphi'$, and it is linear on fibres, componentwise. Its image is $\mathrm{swap}^{-1}(K \times K')$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundleChart.prod_K⟧. *Compatibility.* The overlap of two product charts is the swap preimage of the product of the overlaps ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundleChart.overlap_prod⟧, and the transition map is the product of the transition maps ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundleChart.transition_prod⟧. Compatibility (Definition 2.6.4) asks four sc-smoothness statements, the transition maps and their inverses on $[0]$ and on $[1]$, and two level-preservation statements. The first four are the product of retract maps of the previous paragraph, on $[1]$ after identifying the $[1]$-map of the product with the product of the $[1]$-maps; the last two hold componentwise ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundleChart.Compatible.prod⟧. *The atlas* consists of the products $\Phi \times \Phi'$ of charts of the two atlases; any two are compatible by what was just shown, and they cover $W \times W'$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundle.prodAtlas⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundle.prod_mem_charts⟧. $W \times W'$ is paracompact, $W[0]$ and $W'[0]$ being metrizable ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundle.paracompactSpace_prod⟧. *Restated*, because the text does not describe the structure; the atlas of products of charts is the one the M-polyfold product of Part II uses.

*The levels.* As M-polyfolds, $(W \times W')[0]$ and $W[0] \times W'[0]$ coincide: the identity is sc-smooth in both directions, being the swap in the charts $\Phi \times \Phi'$ and $\Phi[0] \times \Phi'[0]$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundle.isScSmoothMap_id_prod_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundle.isScSmoothMap_id_prod_zero_symm⟧. The $(0, 1)$-part is the product, $(W \times W')_{0,1} = W_{0,1} \times W'_{0,1}$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundle.mem_oneSet_prod⟧, since in a product chart the level-$1$ lift is the swap of the pair of lifts ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundleChart.liftOne_prod⟧. With their own topologies, $(W \times W')[1]$ and $W[1] \times W'[1]$ are homeomorphic ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundle.prodOneHomeomorph⟧, the right side paracompact ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundle.paracompactSpace_prod_one⟧, and the homeomorphism and its inverse are sc-smooth, again the swap in the $[1]$-charts ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundle.isScSmoothMap_prodOneHomeomorph⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleLevels · StrongBundle.isScSmoothMap_prodOneHomeomorph_symm⟧.

*Products of strong bundle maps over maps of the bases* (§8.3's strong bundle maps). Products of sc-smooth maps of total spaces are sc-smooth on $[0]$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleMap · StrongBundle.isScSmoothMap_prodMap_zero⟧ and, through the homeomorphism of the $[1]$-levels, on $[1]$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleMap · StrongBundle.isScSmoothMap_prodMap_one⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleMap · StrongBundle.oneRestrict_prodMap⟧. If $\Phi$ covers $g$ and $\Phi'$ covers $g'$, then $\Phi \times \Phi'$ is a strong bundle map covering $g \times g'$: it covers componentwise, is linear on fibres, maps $(W_1 \times W'_1)_{0,1}$ into $(W_2 \times W'_2)_{0,1}$ by the product formula for the $(0,1)$-parts, and is sc-smooth on $[0]$ and $[1]$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundleMap · StrongBundle.IsStrongBundleMap.prod⟧.

**Products of sections** (same). $f \times f'$ is a section of $P \times P'$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · IsSection.prodMap⟧. *Sc-smooth* ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundle.IsScSmoothSection.prod⟧: near any point $p$, an sc-smooth section has a chart representative: a chart $\varphi$ of the base around $p_1$, a strong bundle chart $\Phi$ around $f(p_1)$, and an open $V \ni p_1$ on which $\Phi \circ f \circ \varphi^{-1}$ is an sc-smooth map between retracts ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundle.exists_chart_rep⟧. Do the same for $f'$ at $p_2$; on $V \times V'$ the representative of $f \times f'$ in the product charts is the swap composed with the product of the two representatives, sc-smooth between retracts. *Sc⁺* ⟦Chapter15/ProductBundle · StrongBundle.IsScPlusSection.prod⟧: in a product chart the fibre values lie on level $1$ because both components do, and the representative into the $[1]$-retract $\mathrm{swap}^{-1}(K[1] \times K'[1])$ is again a swap composed with the product of the $[1]$-representatives.

**Over the product ep-groupoid** (same) ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · EpStrongBundle.prod⟧. *Statement.* For strong bundles $(W, \mu)$ over $X$ and $(W', \mu')$ over $X'$, $(W \times W', \mu \times \mu')$ is a strong bundle over $X \times X'$ (Definition 8.3.1), with $(\mu \times \mu')((\varphi, \varphi'), (w, w')) = (\mu(\varphi, w), \mu'(\varphi', w'))$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · EpStrongBundle.prod_μ⟧. *Proof.* The structure map is defined on the pull-back $(\mathbf X \times \mathbf X')\,{}_{s \times s'}\!\times_{P \times P'}(W \times W')$, a strong bundle over the morphism space; $\mu \times \mu'$ is a map on the product of the two pull-backs. The first step identifies the two. The splitting $((\varphi, \varphi'), (w, w')) \mapsto ((\varphi, w), (\varphi', w'))$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · prodPullbackSplit⟧, with inverse the joining map ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · prodPullbackJoin⟧, is a strong bundle map covering the identity of $\mathbf X \times \mathbf X'$, on $[0]$ and on $[1]$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · isStrongBundleMap_prodPullbackSplit⟧: each of its two components is sc-smooth into its pull-back by the universal property of the pull-back (a map into a pull-back is sc-smooth when its two projections are), the projections being the projections of the product composed with the identification $(W \times W')[0] = W[0] \times W'[0]$ above, and likewise on $[1]$ with the homeomorphism of the $[1]$-levels. Its inverse is sc-smooth by the same argument, so it is an sc-diffeomorphism and in particular a local sc-diffeomorphism ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · isLocalScDiffeo_prodPullbackSplit⟧. Then $\mu \times \mu' := (\mu \times \mu') \circ \mathrm{split}$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · EpStrongBundle.prodμ⟧ is: a strong bundle map covering $t \times t'$, as the composite of the splitting with the product of the strong bundle maps $\mu$, $\mu'$; surjective, by joining preimages; a local sc-diffeomorphism, as the composite of the splitting, the identity between the product strong bundle and the product M-polyfold on $[0]$, the product of the local sc-diffeomorphisms $\mu$, $\mu'$, and the identity back; and it satisfies the unit and composition laws componentwise.

*Section functors.* If $f$ and $f'$ satisfy the functor condition $f(t\varphi) = \mu(\varphi, f(s\varphi))$, so does $f \times f'$, componentwise ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · EpStrongBundle.IsFunctorialSection.prod⟧; products of sc-smooth section functors are sc-smooth section functors ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · EpStrongBundle.IsScSmoothSectionFunctor.prod⟧, and products of sc⁺-section functors are sc⁺ ⟦Chapter15/ProductEpBundle · EpStrongBundle.IsScPlusSectionFunctor.prod⟧ (Definition 8.3.4 (1), (2)). The paracompactness instances on the pull-backs and products that the types require are produced by the lemmas above; they always hold.

**$N \times N' = \max(N, N')$ is an auxiliary norm** (same). *Definition* ⟦Chapter15/ProductAuxNorm · StrongBundle.prodAuxNorm⟧: on $(W \times W')[1]$, $(N \times N')(w, w') = \max(N(w), N'(w'))$, read through the homeomorphism $(W \times W')[1] \cong W[1] \times W'[1]$; on a fibre, the level-$(0,1)$ fibre of the product is the product of the fibres ⟦Chapter15/ProductAuxNorm · StrongBundle.prodOneFiberFst⟧ ⟦Chapter15/ProductAuxNorm · StrongBundle.prodOneFiberMk⟧, and the norm is the maximum of the norms of the components ⟦Chapter15/ProductAuxNorm · StrongBundle.prodAuxNorm_toOne⟧. *Statement* ⟦Chapter15/ProductAuxNorm · StrongBundle.isAuxNorm_prodAuxNorm⟧: if $N$, $N'$ are auxiliary norms (Definition 5.1.1), so is $N \times N'$. *Proof*, clause by clause. *Continuity*: a maximum of continuous functions. *On each fibre a complete norm*: (i) if $\max(N(v_1), N'(v_2)) = 0$, both are $0$, since both are non-negative, so $v_1 = 0$, $v_2 = 0$, and $v = 0$ ⟦Chapter15/ProductAuxNorm · StrongBundle.prodOneFiber_ext⟧;
