**The norm on the boundary.** $\widetilde N(\Lambda_\partial)(0, x) \le N^0(\Lambda^0)(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.pointwiseNorm_cubeGlue_le_zero⟧ and $\widetilde N(\Lambda_\partial)(1, x) \le N^1(\Lambda^1)(x)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.pointwiseNorm_cubeGlue_le_one⟧. *Proof.* $N(\Lambda)(x)$ is the supremum of the norms of the points of the fibre over $x$ with positive weight (Definition 13.2.3). A point $w$ over $(0, x)$ with $\Lambda_\partial(w) > 0$ has $W$-component $w_2$ over $x$ with $\Lambda^0(w_2) > 0$, and its norm is at most that of $w_2$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.normW_interpAuxNorm_le⟧: on level $1$, $\widetilde N = \widetilde N^0$ over $t = 0$ and $\widetilde N^0(w) = N^0(w_2)$; off level $1$ both sides are the convention value. For an sc⁺-multisection the supremum is attained, so it is at least $N^0(w_2)$. Hence $\widetilde N(\Lambda_\partial) < 1$ on the boundary when $N^i(\Lambda^i) < 1$, which holds for regular perturbations.

**The glued data charge the boundary.** *An sc⁺-multisection charges every fibre* ⟦Chapter15/ChargedExtensionCylinder · SBLocalization.exists_ne_zero_of_isScPlusMultisection⟧: near $x$ it has a local representation by $k$ sections with positive weights summing to $1$, so $k > 0$; the first section gives $w = s_0(x)$ over $x$, and $\Lambda(w)$, the sum of the weights of the sections through $w$, is at least $\sigma_0 > 0$. So the glued data charge $\partial([0, 1] \times X)$ ⟦Chapter15/ChargedExtensionCylinder · SBLocalization.chargesBoundary_cubeGlue⟧ in the sense of Part XIV's charging condition ⟦Chapter14/EmptyIndex · SBLocalization.ChargesBoundary⟧: at a boundary point $(i, x)$ take $((i, x), v)$ with $\Lambda^i(v) \ne 0$. With the non-empty index sets of Definition 14.1.3 this holds for every boundary datum (§XV.27); here it is seen directly.

**Partitions of unity on $[0, 1] \times X$** (explicit). Theorem 15.3.9 asks for sc-smooth partitions of unity on the M-polyfold of objects; the text does not say why the cylinder has them. *Step 1, from Urysohn functions to partitions of unity* ⟦Chapter6/CubePartitions · admitsScPartitionsOfUnity_of_urysohn⟧. If any two disjoint closed sets of an M-polyfold are separated by an sc-smooth function with values in $[0, 1]$, the M-polyfold admits sc-smooth partitions of unity: this is the second half of the proof of Theorem 5.5.10. Given an open cover, a bump covering subordinate to it by such functions $f_i$ exists by a general construction in paracompact spaces (Mathlib's subordinate bump coverings with functions from a prescribed class), and $\rho_i = f_i\prod_{j < i}(1 - f_j)$ is the partition of unity; locally only finitely many factors are nontrivial, so each $\rho_i$ is locally a finite product of sc-smooth real functions, hence sc-smooth.

*Step 2, a local separation.* Let $s$, $t$ be disjoint closed sets of $[0, 1]^n \times X$ and $x \in X$. The slices $S = \{q : (q, x) \in s\}$, $T = \{q : (q, x) \in t\}$ are closed in the compact cube, hence compact, and disjoint. A smooth Urysohn function $g_x : \mathbb R^n \to [0, 1]$ is $0$ near $S$ and $1$ near $T$. The points of the cube where $g_x \ne 0$ form a compact set disjoint from $S$, and the tube lemma gives an open $V_x \ni x$ such that $g_x(q) = 0$ whenever $(q, y) \in s$ and $y \in V_x$; likewise $g_x(q) = 1$ whenever $(q, y) \in t$ and $y \in V_x$, after shrinking $V_x$.

*Step 3, gluing over $X$* ⟦Chapter6/CubePartitions · exists_cubeProd_urysohn⟧. Let $(\rho_x)$ be an sc-smooth partition of unity of $X$ subordinate to $(V_x)$, which exists by hypothesis, and
$$F(q, y) = \sum_x\rho_x(y)\,g_x(q).$$
The sum is locally finite, since $\rho$ is, and each term is sc-smooth (an sc-smooth function of $y$ times a smooth function of the cube coordinates); so $F$ is sc-smooth. On $s$, every term with $\rho_x(y) \ne 0$ has $y \in V_x$, so $g_x(q) = 0$, and $F = 0$; on $t$, every such term has $g_x(q) = 1$, so $F = \sum_x\rho_x(y) = 1$; and $F$ takes values in $[0, 1]$ as a convex combination of values in $[0, 1]$. *Conclusion.* $[0, 1]^n \times X$ admits sc-smooth partitions of unity when $X$ does ⟦Chapter6/CubePartitions · admitsScPartitionsOfUnity_cubeProd⟧, and so does the object M-polyfold of the cylinder bundle ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPartitions · SBLocalization.admitsScPartitionsOfUnity_cubeSEP⟧. Point G.181 is closed. What remains, along the text's route, is a hypothesis on $X$: sc-smooth partitions of unity, which Corollary 15.3.10 does not state and Theorem 15.3.9 on the cylinder needs; witness $\mathbb R^n$ ⟦Chapter7/LocalDiffeo · rnPolyfold_admitsScPartitionsOfUnity⟧. For $X = \mathbb R^n$ the cylinder's partitions of unity have also a direct proof, from Theorem 5.5.10 with the product charts of the cube and $\mathbb R^n$, whose finite-dimensional models have smooth partitions of unity (Proposition 5.5.11) ⟦Chapter15/Cylinder · SBLocalization.admitsScPartitionsOfUnity_cube_rn⟧.

**The glued data are structurable over the boundary** (explicit) ⟦Chapter15/CubeLiftScPlus · SBLocalization.isBoundaryStructurable_cubeGlue⟧. The text treats the data over $\{0, 1\} \times X$ as structurable because the $\Lambda^i$ are. The formalization makes this precise by lifting the structures of $\Lambda^i$ along the projection $[0, 1] \times X \to X$.

*Lifted sections.* For a section $s$ of $W$ (corrected to the zero of the fibre where $s(y)$ does not lie over $y$, a formality), the lift is $\tilde s(q, y) = ((q, y), s(y))$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLift · SBLocalization.cubeLiftSec⟧, a section of $\widetilde W$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLift · SBLocalization.P_cubeLiftSec⟧, and $\tilde s(p) = w$ iff $s(p_2) = w_2$ for $w$ over $p$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLift · SBLocalization.cubeLiftSec_eq_iff⟧. Along a morphism, $\tilde\mu$ acts on the $W$-component by $\mu$ (§XV.30), so the equivariance of lifted sections reduces to that of the sections: if $s'(t(\varphi_2)) = \mu(\varphi_2, s(s(\varphi_2)))$ then $\tilde s'(t(\varphi)) = \tilde\mu(\varphi, \tilde s(s(\varphi)))$ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.cubeLiftSec_compat⟧. The isotropy group of $z = (q, x)$ maps to that of $x$ by $(q, g) \mapsto g$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLift · SBLocalization.cubeIso⟧; it is an isomorphism, since the cube contributes only identities.

*The key step: natural representations on a connected neighbourhood* ⟦Chapter15/CubeLift · SBLocalization.cube_Γ_snd⟧. Let $V$ be a natural neighbourhood of $z$ in the cylinder (Theorem 7.1.13), with $V$ connected and lying over the domain of a natural neighbourhood $N$ of $z_2$ in $X$. Then for $g \in G_z$ and $y \in V$, the $X$-part of the morphism $\Gamma_V(g, y)$ is $\Gamma_N(g_2, y_2)$. *Proof.* The maps $a(p) = \Gamma_V(g, p)_2$ and $b(p) = \Gamma_N(g_2, p_2)$ are continuous on $V$ with values in the morphism space of $X$ and have the same source $p_2$. Two such maps agree on an open set, by the étale property ⟦Chapter13/StrongBundlePullMap · isOpen_setOf_eq_of_src⟧, and disagree on an open set, the morphism space being Hausdorff. These two open sets cover $V$; they agree at $z$, where both are $g$ (the natural representation at its centre is the given isotropy element); and $V$ is connected, so they agree on all of $V$. In particular the action of $g$ on $V$ is $\Phi_V(g)(y) = (y_1, \Phi_N(g_2)(y_2))$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLift · SBLocalization.cube_Φ_eq⟧, and the same holds for the bi-action of isotropy groups on morphisms between two such neighbourhoods ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.cube_biact_snd⟧. The text uses this compatibility of the natural representations of $[0, 1] \times X$ and of $X$ without comment; formally it is the uniqueness of continuous lifts through the source map.

*Lifting one structure* ⟦Chapter15/CubeLift · SBLocalization.liftStructure⟧. A symmetric sc⁺-section structure of $\Lambda$ on $N$ (Definition 13.2.5, Part XIII) — an index set $I$ with an action of $G_{z_2}$, sc⁺-sections $s_i$ on $N$ and weights $\sigma_i$, equivariant, representing $\Lambda$ — gives a boundary symmetric structure (Definition 14.1.3, Part XIV) of any $\widetilde\Lambda$ with $\widetilde\Lambda(w) = \Lambda(w_2)$ over $V \cap \partial$: the same index set and weights, $G_z$ acting through $G_z \cong G_{z_2}$, and the lifted sections $\tilde s_i$. Equivariance follows from that of the $s_i$ with the key step: $\tilde s_{g\cdot i}(\Phi_V(g)(y))$ has $W$-component $s_{g_2 \cdot i}(\Phi_N(g_2)(y_2)) = \mu(\Gamma_N(g_2, y_2), s_i(y_2))$, the $W$-component of $\tilde\mu(\Gamma_V(g, y), \tilde s_i(y))$. The representation: $\widetilde\Lambda(w) = \Lambda(w_2) = \sum_{i : s_i(y_2) = w_2}\sigma_i = \sum_{i : \tilde s_i(y) = w}\sigma_i$. Condition (1) of Definition 14.1.3, that the sections be sc⁺ on the local faces, is taken as given (below). The index set is that of a structure of an sc⁺-multisection, so it is not empty ⟦Chapter13/TightMultisection · SBLocalization.Decoration.nonempty_I⟧.

*The two sides.* A point $p$ of the cylinder is on the side "$t > \frac12$" or "$t < \frac12$" ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.cubeSide⟧; both sides are open ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.isOpen_sideSet⟧, every boundary point lies on its side ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.mem_sideSet_cubeSide⟧, morphisms keep the time and hence the side ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.cubeSide_src⟧, and on the boundary $\Lambda_\partial$ is $\Lambda^{\text{side}}$ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.cubeGlue_eq_side⟧. A function that is $\Lambda^{\text{side}}(w_2)$ over the boundary is a functor there when the $\Lambda^i$ are ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.isBoundaryWFunctor_of_sides⟧, since morphisms keep the side and act by $\mu$ on the $W$-component.

*The glued structure.* Let $(\mathcal U^b, S^b)$ be decorations of $\Lambda^b$ with correspondences $\tau^b$ (Definition 13.3.6, $\Lambda^b$ structurable). At a boundary point $z$ let $O(z) = \{\text{side of } z\} \times \{p : p_2 \in U^{\text{side}}(z_2)\}$ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.glueO⟧, an open set containing $z$ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.isOpen_glueO⟧ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.mem_glueO⟧. Theorem 14.1.6 (Part XIV) gives a good system for the boundary of the tame cylinder with paracompact orbit space whose neighbourhoods lie in the $O(z)$ ⟦Chapter14/BoundaryMultisection · EpGroupoid.theorem_14_1_6_hyp⟧; its neighbourhoods are connected. Two facts make everything reduce to $X$. (i) A morphism of the good system from $z$ to $z'$ stays on one side, and its $X$-part is a morphism of $\mathcal U^b(z_2, z'_2)$ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.mor_glue⟧, because its source and target lie in $O(z)$ and $O(z')$. (ii) The structure at $z$ is the lift of the structure of $\Lambda^{\text{side}(z)}$ at $z_2$ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.glueS⟧. The correspondences are $\tilde\tau_{z,z'}(\varphi) = \tau^b_{z_2, z'_2}(\varphi_2)$ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.glueTau⟧ (between different sides the full correspondence is put in, but there are no morphisms between them, so it is never used). The conditions of Definition 14.1.7 on correspondences follow from those of $\tau^b$: local constancy and finiteness of the image along the $X$-parts; symmetry under inversion; the diagonal condition at isotropy elements, by the key step $\Gamma_V(g, y)_2 = \Gamma_N(g_2, y_2)$; equivariance under the bi-action of isotropy groups, by its form for morphisms; and the compatibility with the sections, by the equivariance of lifted sections above.

*Structurability* ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.isBoundaryStructurable_of_sides⟧. For tame $X$ with $d_X \equiv 0$ and paracompact $|X|$, structurable $\Lambda^0$, $\Lambda^1$, and the analytic statement below, which holds, any $\widetilde\Lambda$ equal to $\Lambda^{\text{side}}(w_2)$ over the boundary is structurable over the boundary: functor by the side lemma, a structure at every boundary point by the lift, the decoration and correspondences as just built. In particular the glued data are, with no added hypothesis ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.isBoundaryStructurable_cubeGlue_of_cubeLiftFace⟧. Their index sets are those of the $\Lambda^i$, hence not empty, in agreement with the charging above.

*The analytic statement* ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.CubeLiftFace⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLiftScPlus · SBLocalization.cubeLiftFace_holds⟧: for an sc⁺-section $s$ on an open $U \subset X$, the lift $\tilde s$ is sc⁺ on the local faces through the points of $([0, 1] \times U) \cap \partial([0, 1] \times X)$, condition (1) of Definition 14.1.3. The text uses it without comment. *Proof.* The lift is an sc⁺-section on $[0, 1] \times U$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLiftScPlus · SBLocalization.isScPlusSectionOn_cubeLiftSec⟧: in the pulled-back chart over a chart $\psi$ of the cylinder and a strong bundle chart $c$ of $W$ over $\varphi$, the representative of $(q, y) \mapsto ((q, y), s(y))$ is $w \mapsto (w, \hat s_1(g(w)))$ with $g = \varphi \circ pr \circ \psi^{-1}$ and $\hat s_1$ the level-$1$ representative of $s$, read at the points of $\varphi(U \cap \operatorname{dom}\varphi)$ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLiftScPlus · SBLocalization.localRepOne_fst_of_mem⟧; it is sc-smooth into level $1$. A local sc⁺-section on an open set is sc⁺ on every local face inside it ⟦Chapter15/CubeLiftScPlus · SBLocalization.isFaceScPlusSectionOn_of_isScPlusSectionOn_open⟧. Point G.193 is closed.

**The face condition of the glued data** (explicit) ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOnto · SBLocalization.isBoundaryFaceOnto_cubeGlue⟧. *Statement.* For $d_X \equiv 0$, $f$ sc-Fredholm with tame germ data and $(f, \Lambda^0)$, $(f, \Lambda^1)$ transversal, every sc⁺-multisection $\Lambda'$ of $\widetilde W$ equal to $\Lambda_\partial$ over $\{0, 1\} \times X$ is in general position at the boundary points of the support; so $\Lambda_\partial$ satisfies the face condition of Theorem 15.3.9. The text does not say how this is checked. *Proof*, by slices. At $(q, y)$, $q \in \{0, 1\}$, take the product chart $\chi_q \times \varphi_c$ with $\chi_q(q) = 0$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOnto · SBLocalization.cubeChart⟧ and $c$ tame; since $d_X \equiv 0$, $T^R_yX = T_yX$ and $T^R_{(q,y)}([0, 1] \times X) = \{0\} \times T_yX$. For a branch $\tilde s_i$ of $\Lambda'$, the linearization in the direction $(0, v)$ is $(f - \tilde s_i(q, \cdot))'(v)$, by the chain rule along $u \mapsto (0, u)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOnto · SBLocalization.subDeriv_cubeChart_inr⟧. The slices $\tilde s_i(q, \cdot)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOnto · SBLocalization.cubeSecSlice⟧, with the same weights, represent $\Lambda^q$ near $y$, since $\Lambda'$ agrees with $\Lambda_\partial$ over $\{q\} \times X$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOnto · SBLocalization.isLocalRepOn_cubeSecSlice⟧. So the operators of $T(\tilde f, \Lambda')(q, y)$ on $T^R$ are operators of $T(f, \Lambda^q)(y)$, onto by transversality. Conversely, general position of $(\tilde f, \tilde\Lambda)$ at a boundary point gives general position of $(f, \Lambda^q)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderSliceKernels · SBLocalization.isInGeneralPositionAt_cubeSecSlice⟧. The argument holds for every extension, so it does not need the one-extension lemma of §XV.27. *Witness* ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOntoWitness · TrivialBundle.isBoundaryFaceOnto_cubeGlue_diagSection⟧: $\mathbb R \times \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$ with $f(x) = (x, x)$, non-zero fibres, the face condition at the boundary points $(0, 0)$, $(1, 0)$ of the support. Point G.180 is closed.

### XV.33 Corollary 15.3.10 (3): the explicit extension, the text's route, the last paragraph of the proof, and Remark 15.3.11

**Corollary 15.3.10 (3)** (explicit) ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_bump⟧. *Statement.* Let $d_X \equiv 0$, $|X|$ paracompact, $X$ admit sc-smooth bump functions, $f$ an sc-Fredholm section functor with Part I's tame germ data, and $(N^i, U^i, \Lambda^i)$, $i = 0, 1$, structurable regular perturbations of $f$. Then there are an auxiliary norm $\widetilde N$ of $\widetilde W$ equal to the lift of $N^i$ over $\{i\} \times X$, a saturated open $\widetilde U$ with $(i, x) \in \widetilde U$ iff $x \in U^i$, and $\widetilde\Lambda$ such that $(\widetilde N, \widetilde U, \widetilde\Lambda)$ is a structurable regular perturbation of $\tilde f$ with $\widetilde\Lambda = \Lambda^i$ over $\{i\} \times X$, and $\widetilde\Theta = \widetilde\Lambda \circ \tilde f$ is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid with compact $|\operatorname{supp}\widetilde\Theta|$: the text's "compact cobordism $\widetilde\Theta$ between $\Theta_0$ and $\Theta_1$". The text's (3) does not repeat the pairs; it is stated for different $(N^i, U^i)$, as in (2). The hypotheses not printed are those of (1): bump functions and the inherited tame germ data. No partitions of unity enter, and no compactness of $|S_f|$ beyond the control by $(N^i, U^i)$.

*Proof.* The text applies Theorem 15.3.9 on the cylinder; here the extension of its step (b) is written down, and the relative step of its steps (c)–(d) does the rest. $X$ is tame (§XV.30), hence so is the cylinder, and $\partial([0, 1] \times X)$ lies over $t \in \{0, 1\}$. (i) *Cut-offs.* $\beta_0(s) = \chi(1 - 6s)$ and $\beta_1(s) = \chi(6s - 5)$, $\chi$ the smooth transition function: $\beta_0 = 1$ for $s \le 0$ and $0$ for $s \ge 1/6$, $\beta_1 = 0$ for $s \le 5/6$ and $1$ for $s \ge 1$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · SBLocalization.collarCut₀⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · SBLocalization.collarCut₁⟧. (ii) *The extension* $\Lambda_{\rm ext} = (\beta_0 \circ t) \odot pr^*\Lambda^0 \oplus (\beta_1 \circ t) \odot pr^*\Lambda^1$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · SBLocalization.cylExtension⟧, $pr^*\Lambda$ being the pull-back along the projection ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPullStructurable · SBLocalization.cubePullMult⟧, structurable with $\Lambda$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPullStructurable · SBLocalization.isStructurable_cubePullMult⟧. Scaling by an sc-smooth function and sums keep structurability (Proposition 13.3.12, Lemma 13.3.14), so $\Lambda_{\rm ext}$ is structurable ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · SBLocalization.isStructurable_cylExtension⟧; over $t = i$ it is $\Lambda^i$, the other summand being trivial there, so it agrees with the glued data $\Lambda_\partial$ over the boundary. (iii) *The collar norm* $\widetilde N$, the lift of $N^0$ for $t \le 1/6$ and of $N^1$ for $t \ge 5/6$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCollarNorm · SEP.collarAuxNorm⟧, is an auxiliary norm ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCollarNorm · SEP.isAuxNorm_collarAuxNorm⟧, and with $\widetilde U$ the interpolated set of (2) ⟦Chapter15/CubeAuxNorm · SEP.interpSet⟧ it controls the compactness of $\tilde f$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCollarNorm · SEP.controlsCompactness_collar⟧. The domain support of $\Lambda_{\rm ext}$ lies in $\widetilde U$, and $\widetilde N(\Lambda_{\rm ext}) < 1$ since $N^i(\Lambda^i) < 1$, each summand being estimated on its collar ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · SBLocalization.pointwiseNorm_cylExtension_lt⟧. (iv) *General position on the boundary* from the face condition of the glued data (§XV.32) ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOnto · SBLocalization.isBoundaryFaceOnto_cubeGlue⟧. (v) *The relative step* from bump functions (§XV.29) with $h \equiv 1$ ⟦Chapter15/RelativeStepBump · SBLocalization.theorem_15_3_7_relative_of_bump⟧ gives $\widetilde\Lambda$ structurable, equal to $\Lambda_{\rm ext}$ over $\{0, 1\} \times X$, with domain support in $\widetilde U$, $\widetilde N(\widetilde\Lambda) < 1$, transversal and in general position, and $\widetilde\Lambda \circ \tilde f$ is branched with compact support (§XV.21). These are the clauses of a regular perturbation, with $\widetilde\Lambda = \Lambda^i$ over $\{i\} \times X$. The form with the collar norm and $\widetilde U$ named is ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_collar⟧. $\square$

**The text's route** ⟦Chapter15/FaceOntoCorollaries · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_regular⟧. Theorem 15.3.9 on the cylinder, applied to the glued data with the interpolated norm of (2) on the interpolated set ⟦Chapter15/CubeAuxNorm · SEP.interpAuxNorm⟧, has its hypotheses from §§XV.30–XV.32: tameness and paracompactness, the partitions of unity of the cylinder from those of $X$, the structurability and the face condition of the glued data, the boundary norm and domain support. This form takes sc-smooth partitions of unity on $X$, which Theorem 14.1.9 on the cylinder uses in step (b), and the compactness of $|S_f|$, as the text does; the explicit extension takes the place of step (b) and needs neither. *Reading.* The text's proof says only $\widetilde U = [0, 1] \times U$ and $\tilde f(t, x) = f(x)$ and does not say how the face condition of the glued data is checked; it follows from the transversality of $(f, \Lambda^i)$, since $T^R_{(i,x)}([0, 1] \times X) = \{0\} \times T_xX$ when $d_X \equiv 0$ (§XV.32).

**Intermediate forms.** Forms with steps of the proof as hypotheses remain as declarations, their hypotheses all proved: with the relative step and the face condition ⟦Chapter15/TransversalExtensionCorollaries · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_of_onto⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CubeLiftScPlus · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_of_rel_onto⟧ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_of_rel⟧; with the analytic statement on lifted sections in place of structurability ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_struct⟧ ⟦Chapter15/GlueStructurable · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_struct_charged⟧; with the charged Theorem 15.3.9 on the cylinder ⟦Chapter15/ChargedExtensionCylinder · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_charged⟧; with Theorem 15.3.9 on arbitrary boundary data ⟦Chapter15/RegularPerturbations · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_hyp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Cylinder · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_of_theorem_15_3_9⟧; for the glued data ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_glue_hyp⟧, with the partitions of unity of $X$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPartitions · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_pu⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPartitions · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_glue⟧.

*Witnesses* on $\mathbb R \times \mathbb R \to \mathbb R$ with $f(x) = (x, x)$: the fibres are non-zero, the cylinder has boundary points in the support, the regular perturbation of (1) exists, and the corollary joins it to itself ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · TrivialBundle.corollary_15_3_10_3_collar_epSEP_nonempty⟧; the two-ended form is instantiated on the same example ⟦Chapter15/CylinderExtensionBump · TrivialBundle.corollary_15_3_10_3_collar_epSEP⟧, and the text's route as well ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceOntoWitness · TrivialBundle.corollary_15_3_10_3_regular_epSEP_nonempty⟧. $X = \mathbb R$ has no boundary, as $d_X \equiv 0$ asks; the boundary that matters is $\{0, 1\} \times X$. Weak in one respect: the two ends carry the same perturbation. On $\mathbb R^n \times \mathbb R^0 \to \mathbb R^n$ with $\Lambda^0 = \Lambda^1 = 0$ the hypotheses on the boundary data hold as well, with zero fibres ⟦Chapter15/Cylinder · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_boundary_witness⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.corollary_15_3_10_3_glue_witness⟧.

**The last paragraph of the proof** (same). The text closes with: if $(N, U)$ and $(N', U')$ control compactness, so does $(N'', U'') = (\max\{N, N'\}, U \cap U')$, and a perturbation for it is controlled by both pairs, so one can connect $\Lambda$ with $\Lambda''$ and $\Lambda''$ with $\Lambda'$. *Proof.* (i) $\max\{N, N'\}$ is an auxiliary norm of $(W, \mu)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.isAuxNorm_max⟧: Part I's lemma for the maximum of two auxiliary norms, and invariance from that of $N$ and $N'$. (ii) $(N'', U'')$ controls compactness ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.controlsCompactness_max⟧: $U \cap U'$ is open, saturated (a morphism with source in both saturated sets has its target in both) and contains $S_f$; and its sublevel set $\{x \in U \cap U' : \max\{N, N'\}(f(x)) \le 1\}$ lies in the sublevel set of $(N, U)$, since $N \le \max\{N, N'\}$, whose orbit set has compact closure. (iii) For an sc⁺-multisection, $N(\Lambda) \le \max\{N, N'\}(\Lambda)$ pointwise ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.pointwiseNorm_le_max_left⟧: the supremum defining $N(\Lambda)(x)$ is attained at some $w$, and $N(w) \le \max\{N, N'\}(w) \le \max\{N, N'\}(\Lambda)(x)$. (iv) Hence a regular perturbation for $(N'', U'')$ is one for $(N, U)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.IsRegularPerturbation.of_max_left⟧ and for $(N', U')$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderGlue · SBLocalization.IsRegularPerturbation.of_max_right⟧: domain support in $U \cap U' \subset U$, norm $N(\Lambda) \le N''(\Lambda) < 1$, structurability and transversality unchanged, and the control of compactness by $(N, U)$ given. *A remark on its role.* The formal (3) takes the two regular perturbations with different pairs $(N^0, U^0)$, $(N^1, U^1)$ from the start, through the interpolated pair of (2), so it does not need this paragraph; the paragraph is proved as printed, and the two-step connection it describes, (3) applied twice, is not stated as a separate declaration.

**Remark 15.3.11** (nothing to formalize) is prose: without orientations the perturbation theory gives only rational chains, and with $f$ oriented, $\Lambda \circ \tilde f$ carries a natural orientation and gives invariants, which is §15.4 (§§XV.34–XV.37).

### XV.34 Oriented formal sums, Theorem 15.4.1 and Definition 15.4.2 (2017: Theorem 15.6, Definition 15.7)

§15.4 opens (p. 700) with a list of standing data, Assumption 15.4: a strong bundle over a tame ep-groupoid, an oriented sc-Fredholm section functor $(f, o)$ with compact $|S_f|$, and an sc⁺-multisection $\Lambda$ with $T(f, \Lambda)$ onto on $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$, kernels in good position at the boundary, and $\Theta = \Lambda \circ f$ a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid. Theorem 15.4.1 then lifts the tangent functor $T\Theta$ to oriented tangent spaces, using $o$. The text's route has two layers. The first is linear algebra at one point $y$: every summand $(f - s_i)'(y)$ of $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$ inherits an orientation $o_{i,y}$ from $o$, and the oriented formal sum $\hat T_{((f,o),\Lambda)}(y) = \sum_{f(y) = s_i(y)}\sigma_i \cdot ((f - s_i)'(y), o_{i,y})$ does not depend on the local section structure. The second layer is geometry: for $y$ in the support the operators are onto, $o_{i,y}$ orients $\ker(f - s_i)'(y)$, these orientations vary continuously along the branch $M_i = \{f = s_i\}$, and $\hat T_{(\Theta, o)}(y) = \sum\sigma_i \cdot T_y(M_i, o_i)$ is an orientation of $\Theta$ in Chapter 9's sense. The formalization proves the first layer at a chart point and the second in Chapter 9's $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}(X)$, which is Theorem 15.4.1.

**The setting: chart points and $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$.** Part I's orientation theory of sc-Fredholm sections (Chapter 6) is formalized at a *chart point*: a strong bundle chart $c$ around $x$ together with a level-$1$ point $z$ of the model over $\varphi(x)$ ⟦Chapter6/LinSet · ChartPoint⟧. There $T_xX$ is read as $T_zO = \operatorname{im}Dr(z)$ and the fibre $W_x$ as $K_z = \operatorname{im}\rho(z)$. Part I's $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ (Definition 6.2.1) is the set of operators $T_zO \to K_z$ of the form $(f + s)'(x) + a$, with $s$ an sc⁺-section through $-f(x)$ and $a$ an sc⁺-operator ⟦Chapter6/LinSet · ChartPoint.linSet⟧; for one fixed $s$ it is $(f + s)'(x) + \{\text{sc⁺-operators}\}$ ⟦Chapter6/LinSet · ChartPoint.mem_linSet_iff⟧, it is convex ⟦Chapter6/LinSet · ChartPoint.convex_linSet⟧, and at a smooth point it is not empty when sc⁺-sections pass through every smooth point of the fibre ⟦Chapter6/LinSet · ChartPoint.linSet_nonempty⟧, the standing hypothesis of §12.5. Its members are Fredholm ⟦Chapter6/LinOrientation · ChartPoint.isFredholm_of_mem_linSet⟧, so the determinant lines form a line bundle $\mathrm{DET}(f, x)$ over $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ ⟦Chapter6/LinOrientation · ChartPoint.detLin⟧, and $\mathrm{Or}^f_x$ is the set of its orientations (Definition 6.6.2) ⟦Chapter6/LinOrientation · ChartPoint.Or⟧. Since $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ is convex, hence contractible, an orientation is determined by its value at any one member: $o \mapsto o(L)$ is a bijection of $\mathrm{Or}^f_x$ with the two rays of $\det L$ ⟦Chapter6/LinOrientation · ChartPoint.orEquivRay⟧, because two orientations of a line bundle over a preconnected base that agree on one fibre agree everywhere ⟦Chapter6/LineBundleOrientation · LineOrientation.eq_of_apply_eq⟧.

**Oriented formal sums** (restated). At a chart point $p$, an *oriented member of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$* is a pair $(L, r)$ with $L \in \mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ and $r$ a ray of $\det L$ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.OrLin⟧. The text's $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}_F(x)$ (p. 701) is the set of finite formal sums of oriented operators with weights in $\mathbb Q^+$, read here as finitely supported functions on oriented members ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.GrFHat⟧, as $\mathrm{GrF}(x)$ was read in §XV.1. The forgetful map $f_F$ sends $\sum\sigma \cdot (L, r)$ to $\sum\sigma \cdot L$ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.forgetOr⟧. *The reading*: the text's oriented operators are all sc-Fredholm operators $T_xX \to W_x$; here they are the members of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$, which are exactly the operators that $o$ can orient, and all operators that occur in $T(f, \Lambda)(x)$ are of this kind (below).

*$f_F$ is 2–1* ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.exists_two_orLin⟧: over every $L \in \mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ there are exactly two oriented members. *Proof.* $\det L$ is a real line, since $L$ is Fredholm ⟦Chapter6/FredholmDet · finrank_fredholmDet⟧, and a real line has exactly two rays, a nonzero vector $v$ and $-v$ spanning them ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.exists_two_rays_of_finrank_eq_one⟧. Two oriented members over the same $L$ are equal iff their rays are.

**The canonical lift of a formal sum.** For $o \in \mathrm{Or}^f_x$ and $L \in \mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$, $(L, o(L))$ is the oriented member $L$ inherits from $o$ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orLin⟧, and $L \mapsto (L, o(L))$ is injective ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orLin_injective⟧. The canonical lift of a formal sum $w = \sum\sigma_L \cdot L$ of members of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ is its push-forward along this map, $\sum\sigma_L \cdot (L, o(L))$ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.canonLift⟧. Four facts follow, each a short computation with push-forwards of finitely supported functions.

- *$f_F$ undoes the lift*: $f_F(\mathrm{lift}_o(w)) = w$, read as a sum of operators ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.forgetOr_canonLift⟧, since $f_F \circ (L \mapsto (L, o(L)))$ is the inclusion of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$. The lift is additive and commutes with finite sums and single terms ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.canonLift_add⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.canonLift_sum⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.canonLift_single⟧.
- *Every summand carries the orientation induced by $o$* ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.snd_eq_of_mem_support_canonLift⟧: the support of a push-forward lies in the image of the support.
- *Uniqueness* ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.canonLift_unique⟧: a formal sum $W$ of oriented members whose image under $f_F$ is $w$ and all of whose summands carry the orientation induced by $o$ equals $\mathrm{lift}_o(w)$. *Proof.* On the support of $W$ the identity agrees with $q \mapsto (q_1, o(q_1))$, so $W$ is the push-forward of itself along that map; this push-forward factors as the push-forward along the first projection, which is $w$, followed by the lift.
- *The lift sees $o$* ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.canonLift_injective_o⟧: two orientations $o$, $o'$ that give the same lift of a nonzero sum $w$ are equal. *Proof.* Take $L$ in the support of $w$. Then $(L, o(L))$ lies in the support of the lift, hence of the lift by $o'$, so by the previous point it is $(L, o'(L))$, and $o(L) = o'(L)$. Since $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ is convex, hence preconnected, two orientations of $\mathrm{DET}(f, x)$ that agree at $L$ agree everywhere (Part I, as above).

Since operators of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ are determined by their values, formal sums of members with the same image as sums of operators are equal ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.mapDomain_val_injective⟧, and so have the same lift ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.canonLift_eq_of_mapDomain_eq⟧.

**The kernel orientation of an onto member.** The text identifies $\Lambda^{\max}\ker L$ with $\det L = \Lambda^{\max}\ker L \otimes (\Lambda^{\max}\operatorname{coker}L)^*$ when $L$ is onto (p. 702). Formally, for $L \in \mathrm{Lin}(f, x)$ onto and $N \subset E_0$ the image of $\ker L$ under the inclusion $T_zO \subset E_0$, the ray $o(L)$ is carried to a ray of $\Lambda^{\max}N$ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orKer⟧ by Part I's map $\det L \cong \Lambda^{\max}\ker L \cong \Lambda^{\max}N$ ⟦Chapter6/SolutionOrientation · surjRay⟧, built from the determinant of an onto operator (the cokernel being $\{0\}$) and the isomorphism $\ker L \cong N$ ⟦Chapter6/SolutionOrientation · kerEquivOfMap⟧. Composed with $o \mapsto o(L)$ this is a bijection $\mathrm{Or}^f_x \cong \mathrm{Ray}(\Lambda^{\max}N)$ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orKerEquiv⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orKerEquiv_apply⟧: the orientation of $f$ at $x$ and the induced orientation of the kernel determine each other. The kernel is finite-dimensional ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.finiteDimensional_of_mem_linSet⟧, and the ray can be read as an orientation of the subspace $N$ in Mathlib's sense, through Part I's pull-back of rays to orientations ⟦Chapter6/SolutionSetOrientation · pullOr⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orKerOrientation⟧: a basis $b_1, \dots, b_k$ of $N$ is positive iff $b_1 \wedge \dots \wedge b_k$ lies in the ray ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orientation_eq_orKerOrientation_iff⟧. This orientation still determines $o$ ⟦Chapter15/OrientedLinearizations · ChartPoint.orKerOrientation_injective⟧. *Proof.* Fix a basis $b$ of $N$; its orientation is either the orientation from $o$ or its negative. In the first case the characterization above says that the ray from $o$ is the ray of $b_1 \wedge \dots \wedge b_k$, and the same holds for $o'$, whose orientation is the same by hypothesis. In the second case the ray from $o$ is not that of the wedge (the characterization would make the orientations equal, while an orientation is never its negative), so, $\Lambda^{\max}N$ being a line, it is the opposite ray ⟦Chapter6/SolutionSetOrientation · ray_eq_or_eq_neg_of_finrank_eq_one⟧; again the same for $o'$. In both cases the two rays agree, and the bijection $\mathrm{Or}^f_x \cong \mathrm{Ray}(\Lambda^{\max}N)$ gives $o = o'$.

**The operators $(f - s)'(y)$ as members of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$.** The bridge between §15.1 and Part I is the chart point at which the linearization of §§XV.7–XV.8 is read: for a chart $c$ around $y \in X_1$, the point $z$ is the level-$1$ lift of $\varphi(y)$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.liftPt⟧. Let $s$ be an sc⁺-section on an open $O$ containing the domain of $c$, with $f(y) = s(y)$. Then $\hat f(\varphi(y)) = \hat s(\varphi(y))$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.principal_eq_of_eq⟧, and the derivative $D((\hat f - \hat s) \circ r)(z)$ of §XV.10 takes values in $K_z$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.subDeriv_mem_K⟧: the principal part of the section $f - s$ maps $r(U)$ into the fibres $K$ of the retract, and at a zero the derivative of such a map lies in $K_z$ (Part I's lemma on derivatives of sections of a strong bundle retract). Restricted to $T_zO$ it is an operator $(f - s)'(y) : T_zO \to K_z$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.subOp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.coe_subOp⟧; composed with $K_z \subset F$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.toF⟧, an injective operation ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.toF_injective⟧, it is the operator of the chart sum of Theorem 15.1.1 ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.toF_subOp⟧.

**Lemma** (extra) ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.subOp_mem_linSet⟧: at a smooth $y$, under §12.5's standing hypothesis, $(f - s)'(y) \in \mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$. This is what lets $o_y$ orient every summand of $T(f, \Lambda)(y)$; the text takes it from §12.5 (p. 701, "see Section 12.5"). *Proof.* By the standing hypothesis there is an sc⁺-section $s_0$ with $(f + s_0)(y) = 0$ ⟦Chapter6/LinSet · ChartPoint.linSet_nonempty⟧, and by Part I's description for one section it suffices to write $(f - s)'(y) = (f + s_0)'(y) + a$ with $a$ an sc⁺-operator. In the chart, both $s$ and $s_0$ are sc⁺, so their principal parts lift to the shifted fibre $F^1$, and these lifts composed with $r$ are sc-smooth into the shift ⟦Chapter5/IndexLocallyConstantLocal · StrongBundle.HasScPlusSectionsThrough⟧ (for $s$ through Part I's chart lemma for local sc⁺-sections, §XV.10). Put $h = (\hat s_1 + \hat s_{0,1}) \circ r$. On $U$,
$$\hat{(f + s_0)} \circ r = (\hat f - \hat s) \circ r + \iota \circ h ,$$
$\iota : F^1 \to F^0$ the inclusion, an identity checked pointwise from the principal part of a sum of sections and the lifts. Differentiating at $z$ gives $D(\hat{(f + s_0)} \circ r)(z) = D((\hat f - \hat s) \circ r)(z) + \iota \circ Dh(z)$. Here $Dh(z)$ is an sc-operator into the shifted space, because $h$ is sc-smooth and $z$ is smooth; its values composed with $\iota$ lie in $K_z$, being the difference of two derivatives with values in $K_z$. So $b = \iota \circ Dh(z)$ restricted to $T_zO \to K_z$ is an sc⁺-operator, by Part I's criterion for operators that factor through the shift ⟦Chapter6/LinSet · ChartPoint.isScPlusOp_of_shift⟧, and $(f - s)'(y) = (f + s_0)'(y) + (-1)b$. The standing hypothesis holds whenever $X$ has sc-smooth bump functions; it is the hypothesis Part XII carries for §12.5.

**The formal sum with members of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$.** Let $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ be a local section structure of $\Lambda$ on an open $O$ (Definition 13.2.1) and $c$ a chart with domain in $O$. At a smooth $y$ in the domain, $\sum_{i : s_i(y) = f(y)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(y)$ is a formal sum of members of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.linW⟧. Its image as a sum of operators $T_zO \to F$ is the chart sum $\sum_{\hat s_i = \hat f}\sigma_i(\hat f - \hat s_i)'(z)$ of Theorem 15.1.1 in a chart (§XV.7) ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.mapDomain_toF_linW⟧. *Proof.* Term by term. The condition $s_i(y) = f(y)$ is equivalent to $\hat s_i(\varphi(y)) = \hat f(\varphi(y))$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.eq_iff_principal_eq⟧, because the chart is injective on the fibre over $y$ and maps $s_i(y)$, $f(y)$ to $(\varphi(y), \hat s_i(\varphi(y)))$, $(\varphi(y), \hat f(\varphi(y)))$; where it holds, the operator of the term is that of the chart sum (above), and where it fails both terms are $0$.

**Theorem 15.4.1 at a chart point** ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_lift⟧. Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section, $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ a local sc⁺-section structure of $\Lambda$ on an open $O$, $c$ a chart with domain in $O$, $y$ a smooth point of the domain, and $o \in \mathrm{Or}^f_y$ read at the chart point of $y$. Then $\hat T = \mathrm{lift}_o(\sum_{s_i(y) = f(y)}\sigma_i \cdot (f - s_i)'(y))$ satisfies (1) $f_F(\hat T) = T(f, \Lambda)(y)$, the chart sum of Theorem 15.1.1; (2) every summand of $\hat T$ carries the orientation induced by $o$; and $\hat T$ is the only element of $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}_F(y)$ with (1) and (2). *Proof.* The general characterization ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.canonLift_spec⟧: for any formal sum $w$ of members of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$ whose image as operators is a given $T$, the lift of $w$ maps to $T$ (by $f_F \circ \mathrm{lift}_o = \mathrm{id}$), its summands carry $o$, and any $W$ with these two properties has $f_F(W) = w$, because the passage from formal sums of members to formal sums of operators $T_zO \to F$ is injective (inclusion of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$, then the injective $L \mapsto \iota \circ L$), so the uniqueness of the lift applies. With $w$ the formal sum above and $T$ the chart sum, the hypothesis of the characterization is the previous paragraph. *The formula* is the text's, $\hat T = \sum_{f(y) = s_i(y)}\sigma_i \cdot ((f - s_i)'(y), o((f - s_i)'(y)))$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.canonLift_linW_eq_sum⟧, by additivity of the lift on the finite sum over $i$.

**Independence of the local structure** ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_indep⟧: two local section structures $(s_i, \sigma_i)$ on $O$ and $(t_j, \tau_j)$ on $O'$, both containing the domain of $c$, give the same $\hat T$. This is the text's argument (p. 701), that the occurring operators do not depend on the choice. *Proof.* By Theorem 15.1.1 in a chart (§XV.7) the two chart sums agree at the point ⟦Chapter14/Linearization · SBLocalization.linSumChart_eq⟧. Both are the images of the corresponding formal sums of members of $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$, and that passage is injective, so the two formal sums of members agree, and so do their lifts.

**Definition 15.4.2 at a chart point** ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.canonicalLift⟧: the canonical lift $\hat T_{((f,o),\Lambda)}(y) \in \widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}_F(y)$, read at the chart point. *The kernel orientations.* For an onto summand, surjectivity onto $K_z$ is the chart surjectivity of §XV.10 ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.surjective_subOp_iff⟧ (onto $K_z$ means: every $k$ in the range of $\rho(z)$ is hit, and $K_z$ is that range), and $o_{i,y}$ is the orientation of $\ker(f - s_i)'(y)$ induced by $o_y$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.branchOr⟧, the kernel-orientation construction above applied to the member $(f - s_i)'(y)$. It determines $o_y$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.branchOr_injective⟧, by the bijection $\mathrm{Or}^f_y \cong \mathrm{Ray}(\Lambda^{\max}N)$.

**Theorem 15.4.1** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationStokes · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1⟧. *Statement.* Let $X$ be tame with sc-smooth bump functions, $f$ an sc-Fredholm section functor with tame germ data, $\Lambda$ an sc⁺-multisection, and $o$ an orientation of $f$: an orientation of the section in the sense of Definition 6.6.16 ⟦Chapter6/SectionOrientation · IsSectionOrientation⟧, invariant under morphisms, $\varphi_*o_{s(\varphi)} = o_{t(\varphi)}$ (Definition 12.5.4) ⟦Chapter12/OrientationNaturality · EpStrongBundle.IsOrInvariant⟧. Let $(f, \Lambda)$ be in good position on $\operatorname{supp}(\Lambda \circ f)$ and $f$ of index $n$ there. Then $\hat T_{((f,o),\Lambda)}$ is an orientation of $\Theta = \Lambda \circ f$ in Chapter 9's $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}(X)$ (Definition 9.3.2), represented near every support point by the oriented tame branching structure of the solution sets $M_i = \{f = s_i\}$, with $T_yM_i = \ker(f - s_i)'(y)$ oriented by $o$.

*Proof.* (i) *Tangent spaces are kernels.* At a point of good position a solution set $M = \{f = s\}$ is a submanifold with $T_yM = \ker(f - s)'(y)$ ⟦Chapter15/BranchKernelTangent · SBLocalization.exists_branch_tangent_eq_ker⟧ ⟦Chapter15/BranchKernelTangent · SBLocalization.kerSet⟧. (ii) *Kernel branchings.* A local sc⁺-section structure of $\Lambda$ gives a local branching structure of $\Theta$ described by kernels ⟦Chapter15/KernelBranching · SBLocalization.KernelBranching⟧ ⟦Chapter15/KernelBranching · SBLocalization.exists_kernelBranching⟧, Theorem 15.2.5 with the tangent spaces; with the index $n$ its sheets have dimension $n$ ⟦Chapter15/KernelBranchingIndex · SBLocalization.exists_kernelBranching_dim⟧ ⟦Chapter15/KernelBranchingIndex · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.dim_eq_of_isIndexAt⟧. (iii) *The formula.* $\sum_{i : y \in M_i}\sigma_i \cdot (T_yM_i, o_{i,y})$ lies in $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}(y)$ and forgets to $T\Theta(y)$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.canonSum⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.canonSum_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.grForget_canonSum⟧; each $(T_yM_i, o_{i,y})$ is an oriented tangent set whose positive frames are those of the kernel orientation at a chart point (above) ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.isOrientedAt⟧, the same in every chart ⟦Chapter15/KernelChartChange · SBLocalization.orKer_orTransfer⟧ ⟦Chapter15/KernelChartChange · SBLocalization.kerFrames_orTransfer⟧ ⟦Chapter15/KernelChartChange · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.frames_eq_chart⟧. (iv) *Three properties.* Independence of the local sc⁺-section structure ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftIndependence · SBLocalization.canonIndependent⟧: the formula is read off the formal sum of oriented operators $\sum\sigma_i \cdot ((f - s_i)'(y), o)$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftIndependence · SBLocalization.linOrTangent⟧, which does not depend on the structure by Theorem 15.1.1 and the chart-point lift. Naturality under morphisms for invariant $o$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftNaturality · SBLocalization.canonNatural⟧: conjugating an operator by the local isomorphism of a morphism conjugates its kernel orientation ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftNaturality · SBLocalization.linOrTangent_conj⟧. Continuity along the sheets, boundary points included ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftContinuity · SBLocalization.canonContinuous⟧: $(f - s_i)'$ is the linearization of the section $f - s_i$ at its zeros ⟦Chapter15/KernelFramesPerturbed · SBLocalization.subOp_eq_linZero⟧, its positive kernel frames are those of Part I's tangent orientation ⟦Chapter15/KernelFramesPerturbed · SBLocalization.mem_kerFrames_iff_tangentOr⟧ ⟦Chapter15/KernelFramesPerturbed · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.mem_frames_iff_tangentOr⟧, and Part I's orientation along a family of kernels is continuous ⟦Chapter6/TangentOrientationFamily · isLineOrientation_tangentOr_of_family⟧, so the frames vary continuously ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftContinuity · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.framesContinuous⟧. (v) *Assembly.* Local oriented structures that are independent, natural and continuous define an orientation ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_of_local⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftIndependence · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_of_natural_continuous⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftContinuity · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_of_isSectionOrientation⟧, with Chapter 9's independence of tangent sums ⟦Chapter9/GeneralCorollaries · EpGroupoid.hasIndependentTangentSums⟧. $\square$ *Hypotheses not printed.* Tame germ data, under the adopted reading of "tame strong bundle"; sc-smooth bump functions, standing from §15.3 (p. 692) and read pointwise, with the sc⁺-sections through smooth fibre points that they give; and one index $n$ of $f$ on the support, the dimension of the sheets. Assumption 15.4 does not fix the index, and Definition 9.1.2 allows sheets of different dimensions, so this hypothesis is beyond the text: hence narrower. The form at a chart point needs no index ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLift · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_lift⟧. With Part I's hypotheses the two parts of "oriented" are together Definition 12.5.4 ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationOrientedSection · SBLocalization.definition_12_5_4_iff_isOrInvariant_isSectionOrientation⟧.

**Definition 15.4.2** (restated) ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.canonicalOrientation⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.IsCanonicalFor⟧. $\hat T_{((f,o),\Lambda)}$ is given at each object by the kernel formula; an orientation $T$ of $\Theta$ is *canonical for* $(f, o)$ if near each support point it is the kernel formula with oriented tame structures of dimension $n$. Two orientations canonical for the same $(f, o)$ agree at every smooth point ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftIndependence · SBLocalization.IsCanonicalFor.unique⟧, so "the" canonical orientation is determined. The oriented tangent spaces $T_y(M_i, o_i)$ of the text are the kernels with the frames of $o_{i,y}$, continuous along $M_i$. *A reading* (Findings, Chapter 15, item 25). $o_{i,y}$ depends on the operator $(f - s_i)'(y)$, not only on the subspace $T_yM_i$: for $f(x) = x$ on $\mathbb R$ the sections $s = 0$ and $s' = 2x$ have the same solution set $\{0\}$ and the operators $\mathrm{id}$ and $-\mathrm{id}$, which one $o$ orients oppositely. So the local structures are taken from local sc⁺-section structures of $\Lambda$, and the independence goes through the formal sum of operators of Theorem 15.1.1, not through Theorem 9.2.1.

*Further forms.* With "$(f, o)$ oriented" as the single hypothesis of Definition 12.5.4 ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationOrientedSection · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_of_definition_12_5_4⟧; with the index given at one point of each sc-smooth path component meeting the support ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationOrientedSection · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_of_index_on_paths⟧; for an orientation made with local sc⁺-sections ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationLocal · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_loc⟧. Under a strong bundle equivalence $h$ with $f = h^*f'$ the formula commutes with $h$ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationEquivalence · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.canonSum_pullOr⟧, canonical orientations correspond ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationEquivalence · SBLocalization.IsCanonicalFor.eq_pullSection⟧, and the pull-back of a canonical orientation is canonical ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationPullback · SBLocalization.IsCanonicalFor.pullSection⟧. *Witness* ⟦Chapter16/CanonicalOrientationWitness · HalfSpaceBundle.theorem_15_4_1_hypotheses_halfSEP⟧ ⟦Chapter16/CanonicalOrientationWitness · HalfSpaceBundle.theorem_15_4_1_halfSEP⟧: the half-plane bundle of §XV.27 with the half-disc section and the orientation ⟦Chapter16/HalfSpaceOrientedSection · HalfSpaceBundle.halfOrientation⟧: $\partial X \ne \emptyset$, non-zero fibres, the boundary point $(0, 1)$ in the support, every hypothesis with $n = 1$, so the continuity is not the empty condition of dimension $0$. Weak for naturality: the morphisms are units. For equivalences, the circle section pulled back along $-\mathrm{id}$ ⟦Chapter16/CanonicalOrientationPullbackWitness · CanonicalOrientationPullbackExamples.isCanonicalFor_pullSection_negSection⟧; weak: no boundary and no isotropy. Point G.159 is closed.

### XV.35 Theorem 15.4.3 (2017: 15.7): branched, compact, Stokes

The theorem (pp. 702–703) has two parts: under (1) $N(\Lambda) \le 1$, (2) $T(f, \Lambda)$ onto on the support and (3) good position at the support points in $\partial X$, $\Theta = \Lambda \circ f$ is a compact, tame, branched ep⁺-subgroupoid, oriented by $o$; and Stokes' theorem holds for it, with the effective version under the local unique continuation property. The first part is a statement of §§15.2–15.3, proved here from them. The second is Part IX's Stokes theorem applied to $\Theta$ with the canonical orientation of Theorem 15.4.1, which is given by oriented tame structures.

**The local branching at a point of good position** ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.localBranching_of_isGoodPositionAt⟧. If $(f, \Lambda)$ is in good position at $y$ (Definition 15.2.4, §XV.15) and $f$ has tame germ data, then $z \mapsto \Lambda(f(z))$ has a local branching structure at $y$ in the sense of Definition 9.1.2 (2)–(4) (Part IX). *Proof.* Theorem 15.2.5 at $y$ (§XV.16) ⟦Chapter15/BoundaryPerturbation · SBLocalization.theorem_15_2_5_hyp⟧ gives a natural representation $U(y)$, a local section structure $(s_i, \sigma_i)_{i \le k}$ and the sets $M_i = \{z \in U(y) : f(z) = s_i(z)\}$, each a smooth submanifold properly embedded in $U(y)$, the count $\Lambda(f(z)) = \sum_{f(z) = s_i(z)}\sigma_i$ on $U(y)$, and the clause that only the branches through $f(y)$ meet $f$ in $U(y)$. The branching structure is indexed by the branches through $f(y)$, $\{i : s_i(y) = f(y)\}$, with the sheets $M_i$ and the weights $\sigma_i$; each sheet contains $y$, is a submanifold and properly embedded. The count is a chain of four rewritings: the count formula as a sum over all $i$ of $\sigma_i$ or $0$; restriction of the sum to the branches through $f(y)$, the others contributing $0$ at points of $U(y)$ by the last clause; and two changes of the index type (a subtype, then its lift to the universe of $X$) which do not change the sum.

**Branched and compact** ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_3_isBranched_hyp⟧ (narrower). Let $f$ be an sc-Fredholm section functor with tame germ data, $(N, U)$ control the compactness of $f$ (Definition 15.3.1, §XV.17), and let $\Lambda$ be an sc⁺-multisection with domain support in $U$, $N(\Lambda) \le 1$, transversal (Definition 15.2.1, §XV.12), and in good position at every support point in $\partial X$. Then $\Theta = \Lambda \circ f$, read with values in $\mathbb Q$, is a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid with compact support. *Proof.* The compactness of $|\operatorname{supp}\Theta|$ is that of the "in particular" of Theorem 15.3.7 (§XV.21) ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBranched · SBLocalization.isCompact_orbitSupp_comp⟧: the support lies in the sublevel set $\{x \in U : N(f(x)) \le 1\}$, whose orbit set has compact closure, and is closed. Compactness gives the paracompactness of the orbit space of the support, Definition 9.1.2 (1). $\Theta$ is nonnegative, a functor (because $f$ and $\Lambda$ are) ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBranched · SBLocalization.isFunctorial_comp⟧, and its support consists of smooth points under the tame germ data ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.mem_smoothSet_of_pos⟧. The local branching at a support point $z$ is a case split. If $z \in \partial X$, (3) and the previous paragraph. If not, $d_X(z) = 0$, and Remark 15.2.3 at an interior point of transversality (§XV.14) ⟦Chapter15/Transversality · SBLocalization.remark_15_2_3_hyp⟧ gives the structure. *Readings.* The text's (2) is transversality in the sense of Definition 15.2.1 (the operators of $T(f, \Lambda)$ are onto on the support); its (3), "in good position to $\partial X$", is Definition 15.2.4; "tame" for $\Theta$ is the tame form of §XV.21. The tameness of $X$, which the text assumes, is not used: good position carries what is needed near the boundary points. *Narrower* only through the tame germ data of $f$, inherited from §15.2 (§XV.13).

**Theorem 15.4.3** (explicit) ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationStokes · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_3⟧. Under the hypotheses above, with $X$ tame, sc-smooth bump functions and partitions of unity on $X$ (Definition 7.5.5), $o$ an orientation of $f$ as in Theorem 15.4.1, and $f$ of index $m + 1$, $m \ge 1$, on the support: $\Theta$ is a compact branched ep⁺-subgroupoid, the canonical orientation $\hat T$ of Definition 15.4.2 is canonical for $(f, o)$, and for every $\omega \in \Omega^m_{\mathrm{ep},\infty}(X)$
$$\int_{(\Theta, \hat T)} d\omega = \mu^{\partial\Theta}_\omega(\partial S),$$
the boundary measure of Theorem 9.5.15 of the whole boundary $\partial S = |\operatorname{supp}\partial\Theta|$, which is the text's $\int_{\partial(\Theta, o)}\omega$. *Proof.* The first part is the lemma above. (2) and (3) give good position on the whole support ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationStokes · SBLocalization.isGoodPositionAt_of_transversal⟧, so Theorem 15.4.1 gives $\hat T$, represented by oriented tame structures of dimension $m + 1$. The identity is then the form with the orientation as data ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_3_hyp⟧, Part IX's Theorem 9.5.17 ⟦Chapter9/StokesTextHypotheses · EP.theorem_9_5_17⟧, whose hypotheses are exactly these: partitions of unity on $X$, $\Theta$ compact and branched, oriented, and tame of dimension $m + 1$. The boundary measure ⟦Chapter9/StokesTextHypotheses · EP.boundaryMeasure⟧ is defined on the $\sigma$-algebra $L(\partial S)$ of Definition 9.5.14 over the orbit space of the support of $\partial\Theta$ (Definition 9.2.16) ⟦Chapter9/Boundary · EpGroupoid.boundaryTheta⟧.

*The effective version* ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationStokes · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_3_eff⟧ ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_3_eff_hyp⟧: if moreover $X$ has the local unique continuation property (Definition 7.2.18) ⟦Chapter7/ReducedCriterion · EpGroupoid.IsLUC⟧, then $\oint_{(\Theta, \hat T)}d\omega$ is the effective boundary measure of $\partial S$ (Theorem 9.5.16), by Part IX's Theorem 9.5.18 ⟦Chapter9/StokesTextHypotheses · EP.theorem_9_5_18⟧; the local structures then have the property needed for the effective integral ⟦Chapter9/StokesTextHypotheses · EP.hloc_hasLUC⟧. *Dimension $1$* ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_3_dim1_hyp⟧: for $\Theta$ of dimension $1$ and $\omega$ a function, $\int_\Theta d\omega$ is the signed count over the finitely many oriented boundary points ⟦Chapter9/StokesDimOne · EP.boundaryBranchedMeasureDim1⟧, Part IX's Stokes theorem in dimension $1$ ⟦Chapter9/StokesDimOne · EP.theorem_9_5_17_dim1⟧. Together these cover the text's "every sc-differential form of degree $k - 1$" for every index $k \ge 1$, $k = m + 1$.

*Hypotheses not printed.* (a) Sc-smooth partitions of unity on $X$, which Theorem 9.5.17 as printed assumes; their removal is the text's own open question (Remark 9.5.19, point G.153), and Part IX has witnesses for them. (b) The standing hypotheses of Theorem 15.4.1 (bump functions, tame germ data, sc⁺-sections through smooth fibre points) and the index $k = m + 1$ on the support, the text's $k$. "Oriented" is read as in §XV.34, and (3) as good position (Definition 15.2.4). The witnesses are those of Theorem 15.4.1, weak for naturality.

### XV.36 Corollary 15.4.4 (2017: 15.2): the invariant and the cobordism argument

The corollary (p. 703) asserts, for $d_X \equiv 0$, paracompact $|X|$, compact $|S_f|$ and an orientation $o$ of $f$, a well-defined map $\Phi_{(f,o)} : H^*_{\mathrm{dR}}(X) \to \mathbb R$ with $\Phi_{(f,o)}([\omega]) = \int_{(\Lambda \circ f, o)}\omega$ for any regular perturbation $(N, U, \Lambda)$, $\Lambda \circ f$ carrying the canonical orientation; and the effective version under the local unique continuation property. Its proof is left to the reader. Read degree by degree, "well defined" says two things: the integral of an exact form over $(\Lambda \circ f, o)$ vanishes, so the integral descends to cohomology; and a closed form has the same integral over any two regular perturbations. The first is Stokes with $\partial\Theta = 0$. The second is the cobordism argument: two regular perturbations are joined by a compact oriented cobordism $\tilde\Theta$ over $[0, 1] \times X$ (Corollary 15.3.10 (3)), and Stokes on $\tilde\Theta$ for the pull-back of a closed form. The formalization proves both steps for an abstract cobordism (this subsection) and then builds the cobordism on the cylinder in the text's order (§XV.37).

**Oriented regular perturbations.** An oriented regular perturbation of dimension $m + 1$ ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.OrientedRegularPerturbation⟧ is a regular perturbation $(N, U, \Lambda)$ of $f$ (Corollary 15.3.10 (1), §XV.31: $(N, U)$ controls compactness, $\Lambda$ structurable, domain support in $U$, $N(\Lambda) < 1$, transversal) ⟦Chapter15/RegularPerturbations · SBLocalization.IsRegularPerturbation⟧, with $\Theta = \Lambda \circ f$ compact and branched, and an orientation $\hat T$ of $\Theta$ represented by oriented tame structures of dimension $m + 1$, the data that Theorem 15.4.1 provides. Its integral is $\int_{(\Theta, \hat T)}\omega$ for $\omega \in \Omega^{m+1}_{\mathrm{ep},\infty}(X)$ ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.OrientedRegularPerturbation.integral⟧, the branched integral of Definition 9.5.6 (1) ⟦Chapter9/TameOpen · EpGroupoid.FormEval.tameBranchedIntegral⟧.

**The statement** ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_statement⟧. For a predicate $\mathrm{Can}$ on oriented regular perturbations, standing for "the orientation is the canonical one from $(f, o)$": (i) for every $P$ with $\mathrm{Can}(P)$ and every $\eta \in \Omega^m_{\mathrm{ep},\infty}(X)$, $\int_P d\eta = 0$; (ii) for any $P_0$, $P_1$ with $\mathrm{Can}$ and every closed $\omega \in \Omega^{m+1}_{\mathrm{ep},\infty}(X)$, $\int_{P_0}\omega = \int_{P_1}\omega$. Then $\Phi_{(f,o)}([\omega]) = \int_P\omega$ is well defined on $H^{m+1}_{\mathrm{dR}}$, in the degree $m + 1 = \dim\Theta$; on the other degrees the text's $\Phi$ is $0$ by the degree of the integrand, which the formal statement does not spell out. In the corollary $\mathrm{Can}$ is the class of regular perturbations with the canonical orientation of Definition 15.4.2 ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalLiftIndependence · SBLocalization.IsCanonicalPerturbation⟧; it is inhabited ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationStokes · SBLocalization.OrientedRegularPerturbation.canonical⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationStokes · SBLocalization.isCanonicalPerturbation_canonical⟧.

**Exact forms.** *The integral of the zero form vanishes* ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.tameBranchedIntegral_zero⟧, by linearity of the sheet integrals. *If $\partial X \cap \operatorname{supp}\Theta = \emptyset$, every signed measure on $\partial S$ gives $0$ to $\partial S$* ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.signedMeasure_univ_eq_zero_of_boundary⟧: $\operatorname{supp}\partial\Theta \subset \partial X \cap \operatorname{supp}\Theta$ ⟦Chapter9/Boundary · EpGroupoid.supp_boundaryTheta_subset⟧, so the orbit set of $\operatorname{supp}\partial\Theta$ is empty and its whole space is the empty set; in particular the boundary measure vanishes ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.boundaryMeasure_univ_eq_zero⟧. **Lemma** ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.integral_d_eq_zero_of_degIndex_eq_zero⟧: if $d_X \equiv 0$ and $X$ has sc-smooth partitions of unity, then $\int_{(\Theta, \hat T)}d\eta = 0$ for every compact, branched, oriented, tame $\Theta$ of dimension $m + 1 \ge 2$. *Proof.* Theorem 9.5.17 turns the left side into the boundary measure of $\partial S$; $\partial X = \{d_X \ge 1\}$ is empty, so the previous fact applies. In dimension $1$ the same with the dimension-$1$ Stokes theorem ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.integral_d_eq_zero_of_degIndex_eq_zero_dim1⟧.

**The cobordism argument** ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.integral_eq_of_cobordism⟧. Let $F : Y \to X$ be an sc-smooth functor between ep-groupoids (in the text, the projection $[0, 1] \times X \to X$), $Y$ with sc-smooth partitions of unity, $\tilde\Theta$ a compact, branched, oriented, tame ep⁺-subgroupoid of $Y$ of dimension $m + 2$, and $I_0$, $I_1$ two functions on $\Omega^{m+1}_{\mathrm{ep},\infty}(X)$ such that for every $\omega$
$$\mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_{F^*\omega}(\partial\tilde S) = I_1(\omega) - I_0(\omega).$$
Then $I_0(\omega) = I_1(\omega)$ for every closed $\omega$. *Proof.* Stokes (Theorem 9.5.17) on $Y$ for the form $F^*\omega$ of degree $m + 1$: $\int_{\tilde\Theta}dF^*\omega = \mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_{F^*\omega}(\partial\tilde S)$. The exterior derivative commutes with the pull-back along a functor, $dF^* = F^*d$ on $\Omega^*_{\mathrm{ep},\infty}$ ⟦Chapter10/EpFormsFunctor · EP.epFormsInftyD_comp_epFormsInftyPullback⟧ (Theorem 11.2.1 (2), the functor case, Part XI; on the direct limit it is checked level by level from the same identity for forms of a fixed level). So $dF^*\omega = F^*d\omega = 0$, the left side is the integral of the zero form, $0$, and $I_1(\omega) - I_0(\omega) = 0$. The text's form, with $\partial\tilde\Theta = \Theta_1 \sqcup -\Theta_0$, is the case $I_i = \int_{\Theta_i}$; the lemma separates the analytic content (Stokes and $dF^* = F^*d$) from the identification of the boundary, which is §XV.37.

**Cobordant perturbations** ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.OrientedRegularPerturbation.HasOrientedCobordism⟧. $P_0$ and $P_1$ are joined by an oriented compact cobordism if there are $Y$, $F$, $\tilde\Theta$ as in the lemma with $\mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_{F^*\omega}(\partial\tilde S) = \int_{P_1}\omega - \int_{P_0}\omega$ for all $\omega$. **Corollary 15.4.4 from cobordisms** ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_hyp⟧: for $d_X \equiv 0$ and $X$ with sc-smooth partitions of unity, in every dimension $m + 1 \ge 1$, the statement holds for every $\mathrm{Can}$ whose members are pairwise cobordant. *Proof.* (i) is the exact-form lemma, split by $m = 0$ (dimension $1$) or $m \ge 1$. (ii): the cobordism between $P_0$ and $P_1$ and the cobordism argument with $I_i = \int_{P_i}$.

*The cobordism hypothesis* holds for the canonical class: two canonically oriented regular perturbations are joined over the cylinder (§XV.37). Trivially, every oriented regular perturbation is cobordant to itself ⟦Chapter15/PerturbedStokesInvariants · SBLocalization.OrientedRegularPerturbation.hasOrientedCobordism_refl⟧, with $Y = X$, $F$ the identity and $\tilde\Theta = 0$, which is branched ⟦Chapter9/Basic · EpGroupoid.isBranchedEpSubgroupoid_zero⟧, compact (its support is empty) and oriented by the zero lift ⟦Chapter9/OrientationBasic · EpGroupoid.isOrientation_zero⟧, with vanishing boundary measure. *Further hypotheses*: partitions of unity on $X$, for Stokes; the paracompactness of $|X|$, which the corollary assumes, enters through the cylinder (§XV.37).

### XV.37 Corollary 15.4.4 on the cylinder: the ends, the boundary measure, the effective version; Remark 15.4.5

The text's argument, "in view of Corollary 15.3.10", has four steps: (a) two regular perturbations $(N^i, U^i, \Lambda^i)$ of $f$ with $\Theta_i = \Lambda^i \circ f$; (b) Theorem 15.3.9 on $[0, 1] \times X$ gives $\tilde\Lambda$ with $\tilde\Lambda = \Lambda^i$ over $\{i\} \times X$, and $\tilde\Theta = \tilde\Lambda \circ \tilde f$ is a compact cobordism between $\Theta_0$ and $\Theta_1$ (Corollary 15.3.10 (3)); (c) $\tilde\Theta$ carries the canonical orientation from $(\tilde f, \tilde o)$, and with the boundary orientation of Definition 9.3.10, $\partial\tilde\Theta = \Theta_1 \sqcup -\Theta_0$; (d) Stokes on $\tilde\Theta$ (§XV.36). The formalization follows this order. All four steps are proved: (a); (b) by Corollary 15.3.10 (3) (§XV.33), with the restriction of $\tilde\Theta$ to the ends as sets, orbits and weights; (c) with the canonical orientation from $\tilde o = e \wedge o$ and the splitting of the boundary measure over the two ends; and (d).

Throughout, the cylinder is $[0, 1] \times X$, the case $n = 1$ of $[0, 1]^n \times X$ (§XV.30), with the pulled-back bundle and $\tilde f(t, x) = (t, f(x))$; $\tilde\Lambda$ is any function on the cylinder bundle with $\tilde\Lambda(w) = \Lambda^0(w_2)$ over $t = 0$ and $\tilde\Lambda(w) = \Lambda^1(w_2)$ over $t = 1$, $w_2$ the $W$-component.

**$\tilde\Theta$ on the ends** (same; V15.48). Over $t = 0$, $\tilde\Lambda(\tilde f(t, x)) = \Lambda^0(f(x))$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.theta_cyl_of_time_zero⟧, and over $t = 1$, $\Lambda^1(f(x))$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.theta_cyl_of_time_one⟧: the $W$-component of $\tilde f(t, x)$ is $f(x)$. Hence $\tilde\Theta \circ i_0 = \Theta_0$ and $\tilde\Theta \circ i_1 = \Theta_1$ for the inclusion functors $i_q : X \to [0, 1] \times X$, $x \mapsto (q, x)$ (§XV.30) ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.theta_cubeIncl_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.theta_cubeIncl_one⟧, the text's "restricts to $\{i\} \times X = X$ as $\Theta_i$" (p. 700). **The boundary part of the support** ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.mem_boundary_inter_supp_cyl_iff⟧: for $d_X \equiv 0$, $(t, x) \in \partial([0, 1] \times X) \cap \operatorname{supp}\tilde\Theta$ iff $t = 0$ and $x \in \operatorname{supp}\Theta_0$, or $t = 1$ and $x \in \operatorname{supp}\Theta_1$. *Proof.* $d_X \equiv 0$ makes $X$ tame (§XV.30) ⟦Chapter7/DegeneracyZeroTame · EpGroupoid.isTame_of_degIndex_eq_zero⟧, and then $\partial([0, 1] \times X) = \{0, 1\} \times X$ ⟦Chapter15/RegularPerturbations · SBLocalization.mem_boundary_cubeSEP_iff⟧; on each end the value of $\tilde\Theta$ is that of $\Theta_i$, by the two identities above.

**The ends on orbit spaces.** The orbit space of the cylinder is $[0, 1] \times |X|$ (the homeomorphism of §XV.30) ⟦Chapter15/Cylinder · EpGroupoid.cubeOrbitHomeomorph⟧. Its time coordinate, $|(t, x)| \mapsto t$, is defined through the inverse of this homeomorphism ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.cylOrbitTime⟧, takes the value $t$ on the orbit of $(t, x)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.cylOrbitTime_π⟧ and is continuous ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.continuous_cylOrbitTime⟧. For $\Theta$ on the cylinder, the *end at time $i$* of $\partial S = |\operatorname{supp}\partial\Theta|$ is the set of its points of time $i$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.cylEnd⟧. It is closed, as the preimage of a point under a continuous map ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.isClosed_cylEnd⟧, hence Borel, hence in the $\sigma$-algebra $L(\partial S)$ of Definition 9.5.14, which contains the Borel sets ⟦Chapter9/BoundaryNullSigmaAlgebra · EpGroupoid.borel_le_lebesgueSigmaBoundaryH⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.measurableSet_cylEnd⟧. The map $|(i, x)| \mapsto |x|$ from the end at time $i$ to $|X|$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.cylEndMap⟧ is $|x|$ on the orbit of $(i, x)$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.cylEndMap_π⟧, continuous ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.continuous_cylEndMap⟧ and injective ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.injective_cylEndMap⟧. *Proof of injectivity.* Two points of the end at time $i$ come from objects $(q, x)$, $(q', x')$ of the support with the same time $i$, so $q = q'$ (the cube $[0, 1]^1$ has one coordinate); if $|x| = |x'|$, the two objects are isomorphic in $[0, 1] \times X$, because two objects of the product are isomorphic iff their cube coordinates agree and their $X$-components are isomorphic (§XV.30), and so the two orbits agree. For $d_X \equiv 0$ the end at time $i$ of $|\operatorname{supp}\partial\tilde\Theta|$ maps into $|\operatorname{supp}\Theta_i|$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.cylEndMap_mem_orbitSupp_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.cylEndMap_mem_orbitSupp_one⟧: a point of $\operatorname{supp}\partial\tilde\Theta$ lies in $\partial([0, 1] \times X) \cap \operatorname{supp}\tilde\Theta$ (Definition 9.2.16), so by the description of the boundary part of the support its $X$-component lies in $\operatorname{supp}\Theta_i$ for its time $i$; the other end is excluded because $0 \ne 1$.

**Weights on the ends** (same; V15.48) ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.boundaryTheta_cyl_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.boundaryTheta_cyl_one⟧. For $d_X \equiv 0$, $f$ sc-Fredholm with tame germ data, $\tilde\Lambda$ an sc⁺-multisection equal to $\Lambda^i$ over $t = i$, and the face condition of the glued data (§XV.32): $\partial\tilde\Theta(i, x) = \Theta_i(x)$, and the end $i$ of $|\operatorname{supp}\partial\tilde\Theta|$ maps bijectively onto $|\operatorname{supp}\Theta_i|$ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.bijective_cylEndProj_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.bijective_cylEndProj_one⟧. The general fact behind it: in general position at a boundary point $y$ of a tame $X$, every sheet through $y$ has $y$ in its boundary, since $T_yM = \ker(f - s)'(y)$ is not inside $T^R_yX$ (Remark 9.2.14) ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.exists_branch_mem_submanifoldBoundary⟧, so $\partial\Theta(y) = \Theta(y)$ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.boundaryTheta_eq_of_isInGeneralPositionAt⟧; the face condition gives general position at the boundary points of the support ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.isInGeneralPositionAt_of_isBoundaryFaceOnto⟧ ⟦Chapter15/GeneralPositionBoundaryWeight · SBLocalization.hasFullBoundaryWeight_cyl⟧. Good position alone would not give the weights: a point sheet at a boundary point is in good position with $d_M = 0$. *Witness* ⟦Chapter15/HalfSpaceBoundaryWeightWitness · HalfSpaceBundle.boundaryTheta_halfSEP_witness⟧: a half-space bundle with $f$ the graph of the projection, $0 \in \partial X$, non-zero fibres, general position at $0$, $\partial\Theta(0) = \Theta(0) = 1$. Point G.190 is closed.

**The boundary measure splits** ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.boundaryMeasure_cyl_univ⟧. For $d_X \equiv 0$ and any compact, branched, oriented, tame $\tilde\Theta$ of dimension $m + 2$ on the cylinder, $\mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_\omega(\partial S) = \mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_\omega(\text{end } 0) + \mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_\omega(\text{end } 1)$; the same for the effective boundary measure ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.boundaryMeasureEff_cyl_univ⟧. *Proof.* A general lemma ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.signedMeasure_univ_eq_cylEnds⟧: if every boundary object of the cylinder has time $0$ or $1$, then for every $\sigma$-algebra on $\partial S$ containing the Borel sets and every signed measure $\mu$ on it, $\mu(\partial S) = \mu(\text{end } 0) + \mu(\text{end } 1)$. Every point of $\partial S$ has time $0$ or $1$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · EpGroupoid.cylOrbitTime_eq_zero_or_one⟧, because it is the orbit of a point of $\operatorname{supp}\partial\Theta \subset \partial([0, 1] \times X)$; so $\partial S$ is the union of the two ends, which are disjoint and measurable, and $\mu$ is additive on them. For $d_X \equiv 0$ the boundary objects have time $0$ or $1$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEnds · SBLocalization.boundary_cyl_time⟧, and the boundary measures of Theorems 9.5.15 and 9.5.16 live on $L(\partial S)$, which contains the Borel sets.

**Step (c): the end identities.** *Definition* ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.IsCylinderBoundaryOf⟧: for oriented regular perturbations $P_0$, $P_1$ of dimension $m + 1$ and a compact, branched, oriented, tame $\tilde\Theta$ of dimension $m + 2$ on the cylinder, for every $\omega \in \Omega^{m+1}_{\mathrm{ep},\infty}(X)$,
$$\mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_{pr^*\omega}(\text{end } 1) = \int_{P_1}\omega, \qquad \mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_{pr^*\omega}(\text{end } 0) = -\int_{P_0}\omega ,$$
$pr = $ the projection functor to $X$ (§XV.30) ⟦Chapter15/CubeEp · EpGroupoid.cubeProj⟧. This is the text's "$\partial\tilde\Theta = \Theta_1 \sqcup -\Theta_0$", read on the level of the boundary measure, which is what Stokes uses: with the outward normal first (Definition 9.3.10), the outward normal is $+\partial_t$ at $t = 1$ and $-\partial_t$ at $t = 0$, whence the sign. *The orientation statement* ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.CylinderOrientationStatement⟧: every $\tilde\Lambda$ that step (b) can produce, that is, every regular perturbation of $\tilde f$ for the interpolated pair $(\tilde N, \tilde U)$ of Corollary 15.3.10 (2) (§XV.31) ⟦Chapter15/CubeAuxNorm · SEP.interpAuxNorm⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CubeAuxNorm · SEP.interpSet⟧ with $\tilde\Lambda = \Lambda^i$ over $t = i$ and $\tilde\Theta$ compact and branched, carries an orientation by oriented tame structures of dimension $m + 2$ with the end identities. In the text this orientation is the canonical one from $(\tilde f, \tilde o)$; the statement asks only for the properties of it that the argument uses.

**The cobordism from the end identities** ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.hasOrientedCobordism_of_cylinder⟧: for $d_X \equiv 0$ and the cylinder with sc-smooth partitions of unity, a $\tilde\Theta$ with the end identities joins $P_0$ and $P_1$ in the sense of §XV.36, with $Y = [0, 1] \times X$ and $F = pr$. *Proof.* $\mu^{\partial\tilde\Theta}_{pr^*\omega}(\partial S)$ is the sum over the two ends, and the end identities make it $\int_{P_1}\omega - \int_{P_0}\omega$. *Partitions of unity on the cylinder* ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.hasScPartitionsOfUnity_cyl⟧: in the ep-groupoid sense of Definition 7.5.5, from Theorem 7.5.7 ⟦Chapter7/PartitionsOfUnity · EpGroupoid.theorem_7_5_7⟧, given the paracompactness of the orbit space of the cylinder, which follows from that of $|X|$ ⟦Chapter15/Cylinder · EpGroupoid.hasParacompactOrbitSpace_cubeProd⟧, and partitions of unity on the object M-polyfold of the cylinder, which follow from those of $X$ (§XV.32) ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPartitions · SBLocalization.admitsScPartitionsOfUnity_cubeSEP⟧.

**The orientation of the cylinder** ⟦Chapter15/CylinderProductOrientation · SBLocalization.cubeOrientation⟧. The cobordism is oriented by $\tilde o = e \wedge o$, $e$ the positive direction of $[0, 1]$, an orientation of $\tilde f$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderProductOrientation · SBLocalization.isSectionOrientation_cubeOrientation⟧. It is invariant under the morphisms $(t, \varphi)$ of $[0, 1] \times X$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderOrientationInvariance · SBLocalization.isOrInvariant_cubeOrientation⟧, from $\varphi_*o = o$: the local isomorphism of $(t, \varphi)$ is $\mathrm{id} \times$ that of $\varphi$ near $(t, t(\varphi))$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderOrientationInvariance · SBLocalization.isIntervalProductAt_morIso_cubeSEP⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderOrientationInvariance · EpGroupoid.cubeProd_tgtInv_eq_near⟧, and its orientation map carries $e \wedge o$ to $e \wedge \varphi_*o$ ⟦Chapter12/IntervalProductLocalIso · StrongBundle.LocalIso.orVia_ivOrFirst₀⟧ ⟦Chapter12/IntervalProductLocalIso · StrongBundle.LocalIso.orVia_ivFirstFun⟧ ⟦Chapter12/IntervalProductLocalIso · ChartPoint.ivOrFirst₀_conj⟧. So Theorem 15.4.1 holds for $(\tilde f, \tilde o)$ and every regular $\tilde\Lambda$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_cylinder⟧, $\tilde f$ having index $m + 2$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.isIndexAt_cubeLiftEp⟧; on the ends the kernels are oriented with the $t$-direction first ⟦Chapter15/CylinderProductOrientation · SBLocalization.exists_timeFirst_frame_cubeOrientation⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderProductOrientation · SBLocalization.cylinderKernelFrameConvention_cubeOrientation⟧. Point G.182 is closed.

**The end identities hold** ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndLocalization · SBLocalization.isCylinderBoundaryOf_of_local⟧.
