Near $(i, x)$, $x \in \operatorname{supp}\Theta_i$, the sheets of $\tilde\Theta$ are solution sets $\tilde M_j$ with $\partial_1\tilde M_j = \{i\} \times M_j$, the $M_j$ a kernel branching of $\Theta_i$ with the same weights and $\dim\tilde M_j = \dim M_j + 1$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderSliceKernels · SBLocalization.exists_matched_kernel_branching⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderSliceKernels · SBLocalization.sliceKernelStatement⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndBranching · SBLocalization.exists_matched_branching⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndBranching · SBLocalization.KernelBranching.boundaryStratum_cyl⟧; the isotropy groups have the same order, $\sharp G_{(i,x)} = \sharp G_x$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndStructures · EpGroupoid.card_isotropy_cubeProd⟧; the integral of $pr^*\omega$ over $\{i\} \times M_j$ is that of $\omega$ over $M_j$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceIntegration · SBLocalization.integ_cubeFace⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderFaceIntegration · SBLocalization.sheetSum_boundaryFamily_cubeFace⟧; and the boundary orientation of $\{i\} \times M_j$ is $(-1)^{i+1}$ times that of $M_j$, given one positive frame with the $t$-direction first ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndOrientationSign · SBLocalization.boundaryOrient_tangent_eq_faceOrient_of_end_one⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndOrientationSign · SBLocalization.boundaryOrient_tangent_ne_faceOrient_of_end_zero⟧, by the criterion that reads the boundary orientation off a frame with the outward normal first ⟦Chapter15/BoundaryFrameCriterion · MPolyfold.SubmanifoldOrientation.stratumOrientation_tangent_eq_of_outward⟧. These give the local end identities ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndStructures · SBLocalization.isLocalEndIdentity_of_matched⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndLocalization · SBLocalization.isLocalEndIdentity_of_structures⟧, and local identities give global ones ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndLocalization · SBLocalization.signedMeasure_closed_eq_of_local⟧. With the canonical orientations of $\Theta_i$ and $\tilde\Theta$ the kernel branchings are matched with their orientations ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndCanonical · SBLocalization.exists_areMatchedAt_of_kernelCanonical⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndCanonical · SBLocalization.IsCanonicalFor.isKernelCanonical⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndCanonical · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_of_canonical⟧.

**Corollary 15.4.4** (narrower) ⟦Chapter15/CollarCobordismCorollary · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_oriented_collar⟧. *Statement.* Let $d_X \equiv 0$, $|X|$ paracompact, the object M-polyfold of $X$ admit sc-smooth partitions of unity, $f$ an sc-Fredholm section functor with tame germ data, $o$ an orientation of $f$ (Definition 6.6.16 with the invariance of Definition 12.5.4), and $f$ of index $m + 1$ at every smooth point. Then for the canonically oriented regular perturbations: (i) $\int_P d\eta = 0$ for every $P$; (ii) a closed $\omega$ has the same integral over any two. So $\Phi_{(f,o)}$ is well defined in degree $m + 1$. *Proof.* Corollary 15.4.4 from cobordisms (§XV.36). Two canonically oriented regular perturbations are joined by the $\tilde\Theta$ of Corollary 15.3.10 (3) for the collar norm (§XV.33), canonically oriented by $\tilde o$, with the end identities proved near the ends ⟦Chapter15/CollarCobordismCorollary · SBLocalization.exists_collar_local_ends⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CollarCobordismCorollary · SBLocalization.hasOrientedCobordism_collar⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CollarCobordismCorollary · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_collar⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CollarCobordismCorollary · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_collar_of_admits⟧. $\square$ *Existence.* A canonically oriented regular perturbation in general position exists ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalPerturbationExists · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_exists⟧: with bump functions and a compact solution set, Theorem 12.2.2, a norm controlling compactness and Corollary 15.3.10 (1) give a regular perturbation in general position ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalPerturbationExists · SBLocalization.exists_isRegularPerturbation_isInGeneralPosition⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalPerturbationExists · SBLocalization.isGoodPositionAt_of_isInGeneralPosition⟧, and it carries the canonical orientation. Under the hypotheses of the corollary and a compact solution set, the hypothesis of Corollary 15.3.10 (1), existence and (i), (ii) hold together ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalPerturbationExists · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_wellDefined⟧. *Narrower* only by the partitions of unity on $X$, used only for Stokes on $X$ and on $[0, 1] \times X$ (Theorems 9.5.17, 9.5.18 as printed); their removal is the text's own open question (Remark 9.5.19, point G.153). *Readings.* "$(f, o)$ oriented" as in §XV.34; the sc⁺-sections through smooth fibre points, which bump functions give, appear because the canonical class is stated with them; the index $m + 1$ is asked at every smooth point of $X$, not only on the supports, because $\tilde\Theta$ lies over points of $X$ outside $\operatorname{supp}\Theta_0 \cup \operatorname{supp}\Theta_1$. The collar form needs no compact $|S_f|$, the compactness of $\Theta_i$ being part of the regular perturbations. $\Phi$ on $H^*_{\mathrm{dR}}$ is read degree-wise, in the degree $m + 1 = \dim\Theta$. *Further forms.* With the interpolated norm and the text's compact $|S_f|$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderOrientationInvariance · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_oriented⟧; with "$(f, o)$ oriented" as the single hypothesis of Definition 12.5.4 ⟦Chapter15/CylinderOrientationInvariance · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_of_definition_12_5_4⟧; for an orientation made with local sc⁺-sections ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationLocal · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_loc⟧.

**Intermediate forms.** The forms with steps of the argument as hypotheses remain as declarations, their hypotheses all proved: from the end identities ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.hasOrientedCobordism_cylinder_hyp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_cylinder_hyp⟧; with the relative step on the cylinder ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_cylinder_of_rel⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCobordism · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_of_rel⟧; through Theorem 15.3.9 on the cylinder ⟦Chapter15/FaceOntoCorollaries · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_of_or⟧; with the cobordism for the canonical class ⟦Chapter15/CanonicalOrientationStokes · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_canonical⟧; with $\tilde o$ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_cubeOrientation⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderCanonicalOrientation · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_canonicalOrientation⟧; from kernel-canonical or matched orientations ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndCanonical · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_of_kernelCanonical⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndCorollary · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_of_matchedOrientations⟧; and from local ends ⟦Chapter15/CylinderEndLocalization · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_of_local_ends⟧.

**Index $0$** ⟦Chapter16/IndexZeroCylinder · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_index_zero_oriented⟧. A canonical point perturbation in general position exists ⟦Chapter16/InheritedPerturbationExists · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_index_zero_exists⟧, and with the hypotheses of the corollary existence and the equal counts hold together ⟦Chapter16/InheritedPerturbationExists · SBLocalization.corollary_15_4_4_index_zero_wellDefined⟧. For index $0$ the sheets of $\Theta_i$ are points and those of $\tilde\Theta$ curves, and the convention "one positive frame with the $t$-direction first" needs dimension $\ge 1$. *A reading* (Findings, Chapter 15, item 26): it is read in a signed form, the curve over a point $y$ being oriented by $w$ with $dt(w) = +1$ at a positive point and $dt(w) = -1$ at a negative one ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPointFrames · SBLocalization.exists_signed_frame_cubeOrientation⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPointFrames · SBLocalization.exists_signed_generator_cubeOrientation⟧ ⟦Chapter6/IntervalSliceSignedFrames · ChartPoint.exists_timeFirst_wedge_signed_of_slice⟧, and the orientation of a point read through its sign, as in Chapter 9's point integration ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPointEnds · SBLocalization.frameSign_boundaryOrient_of_generator⟧. With it the ends match ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPointEnds · SBLocalization.exists_arePointMatchedAt_of_kernelCanonical⟧ and the dimension-$1$ boundary count splits over them ⟦Chapter15/CylinderPointEnds · SBLocalization.boundaryBranchedMeasureDim1_cylEnd_of_pointMatched⟧. So for $(f, o)$ oriented of index $0$ and two canonically oriented point perturbations ⟦Chapter16/IndexZeroCylinder · SBLocalization.PointPerturbation.canonical⟧, closed $0$-forms have the same count and the same effective count, with the same standing hypotheses; the cobordism is constructed ⟦Chapter16/IndexZeroCylinder · SBLocalization.hasOrientedCobordism_of_isCanonical⟧ from Theorem 15.4.1 on the cylinder for index $0$ on $X$ ⟦Chapter16/IndexZeroCylinder · SBLocalization.theorem_15_4_1_cylinder_index_zero⟧.

**Remark 15.4.5** (nothing to formalize). The remark mentions another route to invariants, triangulating the weighted solution set into a rational chain, and says it is not carried out; there is nothing to formalize.

### XV.38 What is not formalized

Every statement of the chapter is formalized, and no point of the chapter is open. Where each point is settled:

- **G.159**: Theorem 15.4.1 and Definition 15.4.2 as an orientation of $\Theta = \Lambda \circ f$ in Chapter 9's $\widehat{\mathrm{Gr}}(X)$: tangent spaces as kernels, continuity of the kernel orientations, naturality, assembly (§XV.34).
- **G.160**: the products remark after Definition 15.3.3 and Remark 15.3.4: $f \times f'$ sc-Fredholm with tame germ data, $\Lambda \times \Lambda'$, its norm, admissibility and domain support, the inclusion sharp (§XV.19).
- **G.162**: the hypotheses of Theorem 15.3.7 (§XV.26), Theorem 15.3.9 (§XV.27) and Corollary 15.3.10 (1), (3) (§§XV.31, XV.33) are the text's, up to the inherited tame germ data and, for the corollary, bump functions.
- **G.177**, **G.178**: step (a) of Theorem 15.3.9 from tameness (§XV.28), and the relative local families of steps (c)–(d) (§XV.29).
- **G.179**: the face condition of Theorem 15.3.9 is a condition on one extension (§XV.27).
- **G.180**, **G.193**: the face condition and the structurability of the glued data on $\{0, 1\} \times X$, through lifted sc⁺-sections being sc⁺ on the faces (§XV.32).
- **G.182**, **G.190**: the invariance of $\tilde o = e \wedge o$ on the cylinder, the weights on the ends and the oriented end identities (§XV.37).
- **G.189**: tame strong bundle charts exist on every strong bundle over a tame ep-groupoid, the cylinder included (§§XV.27, XV.28).
- **G.191**: the local unique continuation property on $[0, 1] \times X$ from that on $X$ (§XV.37).
- Also closed: G.144 (§XV.9), G.145 (§XV.11), G.150–G.152 (§§XV.14, XV.16), G.161 (§XV.20), G.181 (§XV.32), G.183 (§XV.30).

**Hypotheses beyond the text.** What remains are readings and standing hypotheses: Part I's tame germ data of $f$, inherited from Part III; sc-smooth bump functions, which Remark 15.3.2 says the theory needs; sc-smooth partitions of unity on $X$ for Stokes in Theorem 15.4.3 and Corollary 15.4.4, as Theorems 9.5.17 and 9.5.18 print them (their removal is the text's own question, Remark 9.5.19, point G.153, Part IX); the index of $f$ on the support, or at every smooth point for the corollary; and the non-isolated base point of Proposition 15.1.3.

**Not formalized, and not needed.** The functoriality of $\varphi \mapsto \varphi_*$ on $\mathrm{GrF}$, $(\psi\varphi)_* = \psi_*\varphi_*$ and $1_* = \mathrm{id}$, and $\mathrm{GrF}(X)$ as a category over $X$, which the text uses without a numbered statement; about 60–100 lines from Chapter 7, and not used by the functor property (§XV.9). The independence of the sc-structures of $T_zO$ and $K_z$ from the chart, not used since the sc-Fredholm property holds in every chart (§XV.11). "Tame" in the conclusions of Theorem 15.3.9 and Corollary 15.3.10 (3): the formal conclusions say branched and compact; the perturbations built are in general position, so the tame form of the "in particular" of Theorem 15.3.7 (§XV.21) applies to them, but the combination is not stated.

**Weak witnesses.** Every hypothesis of the Part has a witness, named in its subsection, with boundary or non-zero fibres where the statement concerns them. Weak in some respect: the naturality of the canonical orientation is tested only on units, and under equivalences without boundary and isotropy (§XV.34); the non-zero boundary datum of Theorem 15.3.9 lives over the trivial ep-groupoid with $U = X$ (§XV.27); the cylinder witness of Corollary 15.3.10 (3) joins one regular perturbation to itself (§XV.33); in the circle witness of Corollary 15.4.4 $P_0 = P_1$, the $\mathbb Z/3$ example acts trivially on the base, and the index-$0$ witnesses have $\partial X = \emptyset$ (§XV.37); general position along equivalences is witnessed by a diagram that is the identity on objects (§XV.20).

**Prose.** Remarks 15.2.6, 15.3.2, 15.3.11 and 15.4.5 have nothing to formalize; Remark 15.3.8 holds in the form of §XV.26.

### XV.39 What the formalization taught us

*§15.1 is topology plus Part I's chain rule.* The proof of Theorem 15.1.1 is a statement about finitely many continuous maps and their germs, and it holds in that generality: two finite families of branches with the same weighted count of every value at every point have the same formal sums of (value, germ invariant) pairs at every point, as soon as the germ invariants vary continuously (§XV.7). Lemmas 15.1.4, 15.1.5, Proposition 15.1.3 and Remark 15.1.6 are pure topology, and Lemma 15.1.5 needs only the observation that two continuous maps into a Hausdorff space differ on an open set, so it is proved pair by pair without Lemma 15.1.4 (§XV.5). Written this way, two places of the text needed a reading. The derivative of an sc¹ map is continuous only on level $1$, and there pointwise; so the argument runs in the level-$1$ topology, where it holds equally, and the text's "weak topology" is pointwise convergence. And the final display of the text sums over all branches, as if every $s_i(0)$ equalled $f(0)$; comparing pairs (value, derivative) for all branches and selecting the value $f(0)$ at the end avoids this. Separating the two families together, rather than their union with halved weights, gives the comparison in one step, and it lets $x_0$ itself serve as a separated point. So Proposition 15.1.3, which asks for $x_k \ne 0$ converging to $0$ and does not hold as we read it at an isolated point, is not needed. The step "without loss of generality $X = O$" is the independence of the intrinsic operator $(f - s)'(x)$ from the chart, which Part I's chain rule gives at a zero of $f - s$ made a global section (§XV.7). The functor property, for which the text gives no argument, is the same chain rule once more: a local structure near $s(\varphi)$ is carried to one near $t(\varphi)$ by the local strong bundle isomorphism of the sheet of $\varphi$, and in the carried chart $T\varphi$ is the identity on chart vectors, so the intrinsic operators are conjugate by $\mu(\varphi, \cdot)$ (§XV.9).

*§15.2 is Part I branch by branch, and so inherits Part I's hypothesis.* A local sc⁺-section on $O$ is a global sc⁺-section of $W|_O$, and $(f - s_i)|_O$ is an sc-Fredholm section there, so Part I's Theorems 3.1.18, 3.1.26 and 3.6.8 apply to each branch (§XV.10). This gives the sc-Fredholm summands of $T(f, \Lambda)$ in the chart reading, the local solution sets, their tameness, and good position along them. The price is Part I's tame germ data, without which Theorem 3.6.8 does not hold as we read it; it is inherited, not new, and it is the same hypothesis that §12.5 and the cylinder carry. Definition 15.2.1 asks for less than it says: "nonzero" and "sc-Fredholm" are automatic, so transversality is the surjectivity of every summand (§XV.12). The proof of Theorem 15.2.2, which writes $f(x) = s_i(x)$ for all $i$, presumably means the branches through $f(x)$; the others have empty solution sets near $x$ (§XV.13). "The ep-groupoid $U(x)$" is the translation groupoid $G_x \ltimes U(x)$, and on it the paracompactness of the support, which Definition 9.1.2 (1) asks of a branched ep⁺-subgroupoid since 2021, is automatic, its orbit map being closed with finite fibres; on $X$ it is a hypothesis that the remarks do not address (§XV.14).

*General position is Part I's general position of each branch.* On $T^R_xX$ the intrinsic operator of a branch is the derivative of the branch in a transported chart of $W|_O$, so surjectivity there is Part I's general position of $(f - s_i)|_O$; that gives good position by Part I and persists near $x$. This is why Remark 15.3.6 holds at every point of the support and not only at $\partial X$, and why it needs no $d_X = 0$ (§§XV.16, XV.20). General position at a point is invariant under morphisms, which the text uses silently when it passes to saturations "by equivariance" (§XV.25).

*The existence theorems separate into an assembly and Part I.* The assembly of Theorem 15.3.7 is finite covers of $|S_f|$, sums of linear families, the bounds $N(\Lambda \oplus \Lambda') \le N(\Lambda) + N(\Lambda')$ and on domain supports that the text uses silently, and a small parameter off a null set (§XV.22). The local families are Part XIII's atomic multisections built from seeds, linear in the parameter and carried along morphisms (§XV.23). Parametric transversality is Part I's local Sard step branch by branch: in a tame chart a branch of $\Lambda_t$ is the slice at $t$ of a Part I parametrized section, and (15.1) for the branch is Part I's general position of that section at $(0, x)$ (§XV.25). The one bridge the text takes for granted is between (15.1), which uses $T^R_xX$, and Part I's conditions (i)–(ii), which use the full tangent space; in a tame chart centred at the zero the two are equivalent by two lines of linear algebra in each direction, and the text's appeal to the implicit function theorem for the openness of (15.1) is Part I's openness of general position on the product with $\mathbb R^\ell$ (§XV.24). "Structurable" contains a good system, so the addendum of Theorem 15.3.7 needs one; (1)–(4) do not, with the sums of Definition 13.2.9 in place of structurable ones, which is Remark 15.3.8 (§XV.26).

*Boundary data must charge the boundary.* Theorem 15.3.9 is Theorem 14.1.9 followed by Theorem 15.3.7 relative to the boundary. Its conclusion forces the boundary datum to charge every boundary fibre, a structurable extension having a branch through every fibre (§XV.27). Definition 14.1.3 gives this through its non-empty index sets, which its $|I_x|^{-1}$ presumes; a reading that admitted empty index sets would make the zero function with empty index sets a boundary datum with no structurable extension. A witness on a space without boundary could not reveal this, which is why every hypothesis here is asked for a witness in the non-degenerate case. The face condition, asked for every extension, is a condition on one: on $T^R_xX$ the operators see only the values of the branches over $\partial X$, and a finite-cover argument gives, for each branch of one extension, a branch of the other with the same derivative on the minimal face (§XV.27). Step (a), the cut-off $h_0$, uses the upper semicontinuity of $N(\Lambda)$ along $\partial X$, which holds because a strong bundle over a tame M-polyfold has tame charts at every point, and which the text uses without comment (§XV.28). Steps (c)–(d) are Theorem 15.3.7 with the operators of $T(f, \Lambda_0)$ in place of those of $T(f, 0)$: seeds for all the branches through a point at once, from global sc⁺-sections equal to the branches near the point, and openness of general position near the boundary part of the support (§XV.29).

*The cylinder needs more than the text says.* The product with $[0, 1]$ is built as the product with Part I's cube, whose parametrized Fredholm theory is available; $d_X \equiv 0$ makes $X$ tame, a step the text takes without comment (§XV.30); the auxiliary norm of Corollary 15.3.10 (2), which the text does not give, is the interpolation $\max(\alpha(t)N^0, \beta(t)N^1)$ (§XV.31). The partitions of unity of $[0, 1] \times X$ are a product construction, $\sum_x\rho_x(y)g_x(q)$, and the structurability of the glued boundary data comes from lifting the structures of the $\Lambda^i$ along $[0, 1] \times X \to X$, where two continuous lifts that agree at one point agree on a connected neighbourhood (§XV.32). The text's proof does not say how the face condition of the glued data is checked; it follows from the transversality of $(f, \Lambda^i)$ by slicing, since $T^R_{(i,x)}([0, 1] \times X) = \{0\} \times T_xX$ when $d_X \equiv 0$ (§XV.32). And the corollary needs less than the text's route: the explicit extension $(\beta_0 \circ t) \odot pr^*\Lambda^0 \oplus (\beta_1 \circ t) \odot pr^*\Lambda^1$ with a collar norm takes the place of Theorem 14.1.9 on the cylinder, so that bump functions suffice where the route through Theorem 15.3.9 takes partitions of unity (§XV.33).

*In §15.4 the argument is short and the orientation needs care.* The canonical lift is pointwise linear algebra once the operators $(f - s_i)'(y)$ are seen to be in Part I's $\mathrm{Lin}(f, y)$, because $f - s_i$ differs from $f$ by an sc⁺ term (§XV.34). The kernel orientation $o_{i,y}$ depends on the operator, not only on the kernel: for $f(x) = x$ the sections $0$ and $2x$ have the same solution set and opposite kernel orientations, so the canonical orientation is assembled from local sc⁺-section structures, and its independence goes through the formal sum of operators of Theorem 15.1.1 (§XV.34). The cobordism argument for the invariants is Stokes' theorem with $dF^* = F^*d$ (§XV.36), and the branching and compactness in Theorem 15.4.3 do not use the tameness of $X$, good position carrying what is needed at boundary points (§XV.35). On the cylinder, $e \wedge o$ is invariant because the local isomorphisms of $[0, 1] \times X$ are products with the identity of $[0, 1]$; the weights on the ends come from general position, not from good position, since a point sheet at a boundary point is in good position; and the signs $(-1)^{i+1}$ come from one frame with the $t$-direction first, which for index $0$ is read in a signed form, the curve over a negative point being oriented against $\partial_t$ (§XV.37).
